#3020. What Tom Gardner Said About Days Like This

Tom didn't predict today's drop. He did explain why it shouldn't surprise us and what Fools should do about it.

If you opened your portfolio today and winced, you're in good company. As of market close, the S&P 500 was down 4.36% and the Nasdaq 5.52%. If you own a lot of growth stocks, your account probably fell harder than that.

Back in September, Tom Gardner sat down to record his sixth Quarterly Call, and he spent a good chunk of it on days like this one. He didn't predict today. Tom doesn't make market calls, and he gave "a mild wrist slap" to people who make spectacular ones. Instead, he explained why a drop like this shouldn't catch a Fool off guard and what to do when one arrives. Here are five ideas from that call worth rereading before you touch the sell button.

Tom reminded us that the S&P 500 has historically fallen 10% about once a year. A pullback of 10% to 15%, he said, is "par for the course." Think of it as the toll you pay for decades of stock market returns.

He also argued the odds of a pullback were higher than usual when he recorded the call. The S&P 500 and Nasdaq were trading 10% to 30% above their typical historical valuation ranges. When stocks are priced for good news, it takes less bad news to knock them down.

And he warned that when the index falls, the high-growth, high-beta stocks many Fools own tend to fall further. High beta means a stock swings harder than the market in both directions. So if your portfolio is down more than the S&P 500 today, that's the trade-off Tom told us to expect.

"Emotional reactivity is the enemy of wealth creation," Tom said. A red day tempts you toward the dramatic move: Sell everything, or back up the truck.

Tom's alternative is incrementalism. Buy and sell in stages and recheck your thesis at every step. His example came from Hidden Gems, which sold 15% of its CrowdStrike (CRWD -9.88%) position in the weeks before the call. Tom said the team didn't expect calamity for the business. The stock looked richly priced, so they took some cash off the table and kept the rest.

Tom argued that moving in steps builds conviction. Each partial sale makes you rethink your reasons, and that can get you out of a bad position sooner than an all-or-nothing standoff in your head. If you feel the urge to do something today, do a small something.

Tom's first recommendation for this market was to pair higher-quality companies with lower return expectations. He used Costco (COST +1.83%) to show what he means.

He thinks Costco could deliver about 10% a year plus a small dividend. Over a decade, that turns $10,000 into roughly $27,000. Tom admitted that sounds dull to anyone who's seen a stock do 2.7 times its money in six months. So he asked members to picture Costco handing them about 10% a year for the next decade and to decide honestly whether that would satisfy them. Your answer, he said, sets the frame for how you invest from here.

The danger runs the other way. Lower-quality companies carrying high expectations can fall 50%, 70%, or 90% "and never come back," Tom warned. If one of those becomes one of your largest positions, it can be "quite damaging to your pursuit of financial freedom," he said, noting that it's a good idea to have "solid ballast" in your portfolio. Ballast is the heavy weight in a ship's hold that keeps it steady.

Tom reaffirmed a core Foolish principle: Own 50 or more companies and hold them for more than five years on average.

His reminder of why came from a stock he owns. Rocket Lab (RKLB -12.18%) had run up to $150 a share and then been cut in half by the time of the call. Tom still believes in the business, but he called that kind of swing a reminder of "the importance of having a diversified portfolio."

Tom's final action step was about your household, and it may matter most on a day like today. He suggested a family exercise. Figure out how cheaply you could live for a month and still enjoy your life. Then ask how big your emergency fund should be. Is six months enough? Nine? Twelve?

His goal is to simulate a recession, a market decline, or a job change before any of them shows up. "Taking the step to improve the sustainability of a high-quality life is vastly more significant and important than squeezing out extra percentage points of returns, particularly in a richly priced market," he said.

A household with its bills covered and cash in reserve can treat a falling market as a sale. Tom described investors who get through a 20% or even 40% decline "in style." They keep a cash position on the side and put it to work in their favorite companies at lower prices. In a reply to members in the call's comments section, Tom said his own cash position sits in the 10% to 15% range.

Tom closed the first section of his call with a line from his father, who created the Potential Growth Indicator you'll find on our site. After a long, happy day, when the Gardner kids asked to stay up late, their dad would say, "Tomorrow's another day." Tom offered that advice to investors who try to cram every win into today. It works just as well for losses. A bad day is one day, and Fools invest for decades.

Tom Gardner has positions in CrowdStrike and Rocket Lab. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Costco Wholesale, CrowdStrike, and Rocket Lab. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Invest better with The Motley Fool. Get stock recommendations, portfolio guidance, and more from The Motley Fool's premium services.

#3017. Biotech stocks are surging. How investors can play the new drug innovation boom

Moderna gave investors a scare as its stock plunged on Thursday. But it's still one of the hottest biotechs this year. And it's hardly alone – the sector is on a roll and looks poised for more gains.

The iShares Biotechnology ETF (IBB) is up 24% through October 23, building on a 28% gain in 2025. Two dozen of the ETF's 233 holdings have more than doubled year-to-date. Moderna leads the pack with a 587% surge — even after an 18% decline following a leak of clinical data for its cancer vaccine (which was actually positive).

Analysts say a new biotech cycle is underway, fueled by clinical trial results, M&A activity, and promising technologies such as genomics, mRNA — and AI.

"We're in the midst of a powerful innovation cycle," said John Hall, portfolio manager of the T. Rowe Price Biotechnology ETF. "What's super exciting is we're seeing newer modalities start to make it to market." Companies "understand the disease biology better," he adds, and that could ultimately lead to more novel drugs making it to market.

Investors are excited about technologies like mRNA and genomics, along with a steady stream of new drug approvals. The FDA approved about a dozen novel biologic drugs in 2025, out of 46 approvals in total. Roughly 66 novel drugs are in the pipeline for FDA approval, including potential blockbusters like Moderna and Merck's cancer drug for melanoma, and a next-generation obesity drug from Eli Lilly.

Analysts say AI could also lead to more approvals as companies deploy the software to speed up drug development.

"Biotech is a phenomenal application for artificial intelligence and machine learning," says Piper Sandler analyst Ted Tenthoff. "You can set up exciting predictive models that, frankly, can do things that we've never been able to do in the space in terms of novel drug discovery."

Granted, the industry is recovering from a fallow period. The sector's last strong performance was during the Covid era when ultra-low interest rates encouraged investment in early-stage drug developers, and mRNA vaccines fueled a revenue surge for companies like Moderna, Pfizer and Biontech.

The sector then went cold as many early-stage companies fizzled. Demand for Covid vaccines evaporated, and surging interest rates put a chill in IPOs and M&A.

The risks haven't evaporated – this is still a sector whose products take years to develop, resulting in far more failures than successes. But analysts say the outlook is healthier now. While that's reflected in strong performance and richer valuations, there's still good reason to invest.

Recent clinical trial results are stoking excitement that novel drugs in the works could tackle some of the toughest problems in medicine.

Moderna and Merck's melanoma drug, for instance, uses mRNA to trigger an immune response in patients, targeting tumors that have been surgically removed and genetically analyzed. It's not a preventive vaccine, but it uses a similar immunology mechanism and showed a 42% reduction in cancer recurrence or death (used in conjunction with Merck's cancer drug Keytruda).

While the Phase 3 results disappointed Wall Street, oncologists say the outcomes were still highly positive. And they see the technology as a potential game changer, ushering in an era of personalized treatments for cancers. Moderna and Merck have said they aim to submit the drug for FDA approval and get it launched in 2027.

Inflammation treatment is another promising area. AstraZeneca and Amgen recently released positive Phase 3 results for another use for their drug Tezspire, an injectable approved for nasal polyps and asthma. In clinical trials, it showed that it successfully relieved swallowing symptoms and healed microscopic and visual tissue damage in patients with severe throat inflammation.

Roche, meanwhile, notched a win after the FDA approved its drug Enspryng, a therapy to treat rare inflammatory and autoimmune diseases. It became the first and only at-home injection for adults with thyroid eye disease.

Obesity drugs are also advancing beyond the first generation of GLP-1s. Eli Lilly's retatrutide, a "triple agonist" targeting three different hormone receptors, cleared two more clinical trials over the summer. The drug produced 28%-30% weight loss at the highest doses in the study, Lilly said. The company plans to submit the drug for FDA approval in the first quarter of 2027.

Much of the innovation isn't just happening in drug-making – it's also in areas like gene sequencing, mRNA, and high-tech instruments.

Illumina has more than doubled this year. The DNA and gene sequencing company is seeing accelerating demand for its NovaSeq X high-end sequencing platform. Pharmaceutical companies including Bristol-Myers Squibb, Merck and Kura are working with Illumina to develop precision oncology solutions.

Another big winner is 10x Genomics, up more than 300%. The biotech firm offers its lab instruments to help scientists research cancer, immunology and neuroscience. The company's Atera platform is helping produce data sets to train AI models, a process that helps accelerate personalized therapies.

On the AI front, companies are using the technology for more efficient drug discovery, says Shreefāl Mehta, president of Guidant BioTherapeutics. Using AI, "you can now bring a drug to clinical trials with less capital. More investment is also going into companies that are demonstrating that," he said.

Global AI in drug discovery will grow from a $20 billion market in 2025 to more than $160 billion by 2035, according to forecasts by Towards Health, a research and consulting firm.

One other catalyst for the sector could be M&A. Dealmaking for biopharma hit $134 billion in the first half of 2026, up from $112 billion for all of 2025, notes Jeet Mukherjee, biotech analyst at BTIG. That's a positive signal, indicating more drugs in development are showing clinical promise.

It also helps that Big Pharma has a "patent cliff" ahead. Products representing $180 billion of revenue will lose exclusivity by 2030, according to research firm RTW Institute. Companies with looming expiries "are looking for novel products and innovative products to put into their pipeline," Mehta says.

GLP-1 blockbusters have also left some companies flush with cash for acquisitions. Eli Lilly alone has completed 10 acquisition deals in 2026 worth $25 billion.

ETFs can capture the investing theme, though they aren't all alike. The iShares Biotech ETF is one of the lower cost funds. It tracks a market-cap weighted index of biotech stocks, led by companies like Gilead Sciences, Amgen, Vertex Pharmaceuticals, and Moderna.

The State Street SPDR S&P Biotech ETF holds stocks in equal weight, offering more exposure to small- and mid-cap names. Its top holdings are Moderna, Natera, Twist Bioscience and Halozyme Therapeutics.

Piper Sandler calls Illumina and 10x Genomics the "primary infrastructure plays" in the life science arena, saying their technologies are "driving the next generation of precision medicine."

Piper analyst David Westenberg has a buy rating and $305 target on Illumina, implying 14% gains from recent prices around $267. He has a hold rating on 10X with an $80 target, above recent prices around $70.

Twist Bioscience is another name to consider. The company makes customized synthetic DNA and sells DNA sequencing tools. Twist has partnered with Eli Lilly's AI and machine-learning platform and linked up with Anthropic in August to start testing its AI model, Claude, for protein development.

Twist isn't expected to turn an EPS profit until 2029, according to consensus forecasts. But revenue is expected to go from $572 million in 2027 to $833 million in 2029. Analysts have an average target of $168 on the stock, implying 21% returns from recent prices around $138.

In the small-cap space, Piper Sandler's Tenthoff likes Monte Rosa Therapeutics, a pioneer in protein degradation that selectively destroys disease-causing proteins.

The firm has partnered with Novartis to develop a drug for inflammatory conditions and autoimmune diseases. The companies announced a Phase 2 clinical study in September for an autoimmune disease treatment, which has been "triggering milestones to Monte Rosa," Tenthoff said.

Tenthoff has a buy rating and $37 target on the stock, which recently traded around $10.70.

Also in the small-cap arena, BTIG's Mukherjee recommends Faeth Therapeutics.

The biotech is developing a novel two-drug regimen to treat breast cancer and other tumors. The regimen "has the potential to address safety and dosing limitations of another recently approved drug for breast cancer," he says. And it could become "a mainstay of the future breast cancer treatment paradigm and other cancers."

Faeth is a clinical-stage company that has yet to bring a drug to market. Yet the stock is up 93% year-to-date on positive clinical trial results, trading around $18. Mukherjee has a $55 target on it.

Kymera Therapeutics is worth considering too. The company is developing an oral version of the blockbuster drug Dupixent (made and co-developed by Regeneron Pharmaceuticals and Sanofi). Dupixent is an injectable, however, and oral agents "are frequently preferred by patients," Mukherjee says. "The drug has the potential to not only take share from patients taking Dupixent for numerous immunological diseases but also expanding the market to those not taking systemic biologic drugs," he tells CNBC.

Kymera has said it doesn't expect a patent fight over Dupixent because its drug is distinct, not a biosimilar or biologic copy.

T. Rowe's Hall also likes Kymera for its potential "to unlock disease targets that have been classically considered un-druggable." The stock is the second largest holding in T. Rowe's Biotech ETF, accounting for 4.2% of the portfolio.

#3010. America's Real 1% Has Nothing to Do With Silicon Valley or Wall Street

The wealthiest people in America aren't who you think they are. Together, they have more net worth than the Forbes 400 by a factor of 10 — and almost no one has heard of them.

In this episode of Motley Fool Hidden Gems Investing, Motley Fool contributor Rachel Warren talks with Eric Zwick, professor of economics at the University of Chicago Booth School of Business and co-author of The Everywhere Millionaire, about the decade of research that rewrote what he thought he knew about wealth, inequality, and the American economy.They discuss:

To catch full episodes of all The Motley Fool's free podcasts, check out our podcast center. When you're ready to invest, check out this top 10 list of stocks to buy.

This podcast was recorded on Oct. 25, 2026.

Eric Zwick: When you collectively add them up, they have more net worth than the Forbes 400 by an order of magnitude, so more than 10 times the collective net worth of the Forbes 400, even though individually, they're not as rich as Elon.

Rachel Warren: That was Eric Zwick, Professor of Economics at the University of Chicago Booth School of Business. On a group of Americans almost no one has ever heard of who collectively hold more wealth than the entire Forbes 400. I'm Motley Fool analyst Rachel Warren. Eric spent a decade working through economic data that most researchers never get access to, and what he found completely rewrites the story of wealth in this country. We get into who actually makes up the real 1%, what this hidden economy of Main Street millionaires means for investors watching the public markets, and the statistic that made Eric rethink everything he thought he knew about inequality. We hope you enjoy.

Welcome back to Motley Fool Conversations. I'm Motley Fool analyst Rachel Warren. Today, I'm delighted to welcome Eric Zwick, Professor of Economics and Finance at the University of Chicago Booth School of Business to the show. Eric is also the co-author of an incredibly eye-opening new book, The Everywhere Millionaire. Who is really rich in America and how they got there? Eric has spent a decade digging through economic data to shatter the myth that transformational wealth belongs only to Silicon Valley tech founders and Wall Street executives. Instead, this research has uncovered America's hidden 1%, the regional business owners, pass-through entity founders, and Main Street entrepreneurs, building generational fortunes completely away from the spotlight. Eric, it's wonderful to have you on the show. Welcome.

Eric Zwick: Thank you so much for having me. Great to be here.

Rachel Warren: One of the things that struck me is the core thesis of The Everywhere Millionaire challenges the popular narrative that massive wealth creation requires building a software giant or managing a hedge fund, and there are many other examples. When you look at the data, what does the typical member of America's real 1% actually look like?

Eric Zwick: Yes, so we're told that the path to prosperity is through Silicon Valley or Wall Street, you have to be a tech CEO or a VC-backed start-up founder or a Wall Street trader in order to get really rich in America. What we found through a decade of research, working with unprecedented administrative data that then in the book, we turned into stories by going out and finding these Everywhere Millionaires lurking in plain sight. Wealth is much more pervasive. It's much more abundant across the country. There are people running unglamorous businesses in towns and suburbs and Tier 2, Tier 3 cities all across the country, who are incredibly wealthy. Our average millionaires have an average net worth of around $25 million. They have minimum net worth of about $5 million. They are people who own a private business, and we estimate there are about three million of them.

That's a lot of people. When you collectively add them up, they have more net worth than the Forbes 400 by an order of magnitude. More than 10 times the collective net worth of the Forbes 400, even though individually, they're not as rich as Elon. They're just collectively this huge group of people that we really didn't know that much about, I think, for various reasons that we can talk about. Concretely, who are they? We do something we joke because we both have little kids, on and I, the ABC board book. The ABCs of getting rich. A is for auto dealers, B is for beer distributors, C is for contractors. D is for dentists and it could go on. But it's like that type of mid market business owner across a whole bunch of industries that are not super glamorous. These are the Everywhere Millionaires whose stories we try and tell in this book.

Rachel Warren: I think in the book as well, you call this group the stealthy, wealthy. I wonder why has this segment of the population managed to build this wealth, but very much away from the spotlight and even the radar of Wall Street?

Eric Zwick: We call them stealthy, wealthy, and some are maybe nefarious a little bit in their behavior, but many, they're just flying under the radar, but just not with anything untoward about their behavior. We call them the stealthy wealthy just cause people aren't looking for them in the right places. Looking away from the coasts. A lot of our attention is drawn to the coasts, where we tend to look in the media at public company activity. A lot of retail investors listen to a podcast. Public companies are specific subset of the American economy, and they disclose their information. You know what they are. They tend to be consumer-oriented businesses, not always. They tend to be in more capital-intensive sectors, so they have to raise capital to go public. This is not a representative pick of the American economy, and it's not the ballpark in terms of where people work, where the profits are.

More than half of the total profits in business across the country are in what we call pass-through businesses. These are non-public businesses. There's a ton of activity. Most businesses in the country are these pass-through businesses, non-public businesses, and they’re all over the place. I think the Forbes 400 is another example of when people think about who's really rich, they jump to the top of the Forbes list. Those are tech founders, typically public companies owners, and also people who are seeking attention in some cases by buying media companies. Our stealthy, wealthy are not actively trying to hide, although some are. One car dealer is notoriously referred to as Batman because everybody knows that he exists, but nobody's seen him. I think they just are running their businesses. They're not in these media markets, and they're doing their thing and enjoying it in a lot of cases, without a lot of attention at all.

Rachel Warren: One of the things that struck me I saw there was a comment on your book from the Nobel Prize-winning economist Angus Deaton, and he was saying that in his comment, there's currently five million households in America with over $5 million each running entirely below the radar. I wonder, what does that tell us about just the scaling capacity, but also the overall health of the U.S. consumer economy?

Eric Zwick: I think there's this fisheye lens view of the U.S. economy that is prevailing in the media narrative where it zooms in on Elon or zooms in on a few billionaires. Then it's like everybody is being left behind. I think the message from these stories is that there's a lot more opportunity for people to do incredibly well. We're not talking about people who just have a million bucks in a pension. We're talking about people who don't really have to think too hard about how they spend their money at this point. They're close to post economic, if not post economic, to use a term of art, where they basically, I want this. I'm going to buy it. There are millions of people like that across the country, and many of them weren't born with a lot of opportunities.

We figure that about a third we try and estimate this in various ways of our Everywhere Millionaires inherited businesses or come through that shortcut into prosperity as business owners, but that means the two thirds or maybe 70% either founded their business or bought one and grew it later in their careers. That's a lot of self made stories in the data. We tell some of these stories individually, **** Portillo from Chicago, who started out growing up in the Cabrini Green Housing Project and started a hot dog stand. He didn't actually know how to make hot dogs at the time. He didn't know how to steam a bun. Had an $1,100 investment initially. Built this thing incrementally with no outside investors, very little debt, just plowing his profits back into the business. It was the largest privately owned restaurant company by the time he eventually sold it because he didn't really want to pass it on to his kids. He was business, and so he was I'm going to sell it in his 80s, although he was annoyed to do it after selling it, he's told us he was bored. But he sold it for over a billion dollars, and we found him in yacht registration data.

He has a very large yacht, a 100-foot yacht called Top Dog. There are stories like this. He's an outlier within that class because he's more like a billionaire that people haven't heard of. But there are just tons of stories that look like him in the data, and I think it's quite inspiring, and it says that there's more opportunity available than I think the prevailing narrative. I think it's an optimistic take on the economy that you don't hear as often as maybe we should.

Rachel Warren: What do you think maybe the primary misconceptions are among the public, among investors about where that real durable wealth is being generated right now?

Eric Zwick: Silicon Valley tech apps, influencer economy. These are terms that I think are the flashy Instagram reel images that we see about rich people. We don't see the trash bag liner manufacturer who supplies to all the McDonald's franchises. Then when he retired from that business, bought a submersible so he could hunt for actual treasure in the Caribbean from sunk in treasure ships. We don't see the guy who has 20 Wendy's franchises that he's built that portfolio of franchises over time running Wendy's, who's maybe a decca millionaire or something like that. These are unglamorous businesses. They don't draw our attention in the same way, even though the people are laughing their way to the bank.

They're not as entertaining to look at if you're swiping and scrolling. I think that's why there's just this misconception about it. But once you start to look for them, you do see them everywhere, even if you live in New York City, you actually can see them all over the place. I live in Chicago. I walk from work to school to drop off my kid. There's a beer truck that says Budweiser on the side, and on the side of the door is the name of the distributor. I'm — that's Reyes. That's one of the largest beer distributors in the country, incredibly wealthy, and most people haven't heard of him or that family, and their story is quite interesting in terms of how they rolled up distributors across the country over many decades. But there's that kind of thing. They're right all around us closer to home than you think. But you're not looking for them on your phone, you're looking for them in the world around you.

Rachel Warren: From your research into these Main Street businesses, I wonder what are some takeaways or a framework that maybe you can draw into evaluating public companies? If you're a retail investor listening to this conversation, what are maybe some lessons that can be learned from these private businesses that can be drawn into the health of the public markets?

Eric Zwick: That's a good question. I like that question. One is probably to think about competition a little bit more broadly. There's scope for competition from the private market in certain product lines. Thinking about when you're picking investments, it's not just about competition from other publics. It's competition from products that are being offered just by these Everywhere Millionaires and their businesses, and which ones are robust to that competition? Which ones have an opportunity to disrupt the sleepier Everywhere Millionaires in certain industries.

I think we've seen legal services, companies that are trying to enter and disrupt, say, these law practices across the country because there's tons of profit there. If you're looking for opportunities or growth, I think disrupting some of these businesses where they're not so focused on staying ahead or adopting new technology. I think it's quite interesting. We've seen a little bit of that with the software as a service. Is this going to be disrupted by AI or is it going to be complimentary with AI? I think we could think about some of these more tactile industries, fast casual dining or auto parts, or mid market manufacturing, making frozen quiches, like this stuff in big box stores.

Is AI going to disrupt all aspects of that business or is AI going to help with customer relations management is going to help with sales? What does that mean for some of the other stocks I'm looking at or some of the opportunities I'm looking at? I think it's useful to have a reminder that the economy isn't just zeros and ones and chips and code, that there's this much richer in the world three dimensional economy out there, and the stories might be a little different than the Magnificent 7. I think, as a public market investor, that's what we think about all the investors are flooding into those seven bets. What are some, maybe interesting contrarian bets? There's some thoughts I have on that question.

Rachel Warren: One of the other things that I was thinking about often, when we look at the public markets, we see some companies expend massive amounts of capital on dilutive acquisitions or even poorly time share buybacks. I wonder how do the stealthy, wealthy, as you call them in the book? How do they tend to think about capital allocation? Because I also think that there's often parallels that we can draw when we're looking at quality businesses in the public markets from what we see in terms of the actions taking place in the private markets.

Eric Zwick: I see some of these acquisitions or throwing capital back at investors through buybacks, it's the consequence of having run out of good ideas. In some cases the dilutive acquisition is, well, we see the writing on the wall with our main business line, which is trending down. In the absence of this acquisition, it's going to look even worse. It could still make sense to do it, even if it's not a great, it's not going to lead to salvation for the company. Stealthy, wealthy folks have the benefit of being able to grow without reinventing themselves in a lot of cases, as I mentioned, geographically or adding a few products like the chemical importer exporter that we profile can just find a few more customers can tell you hard to do, but because their market shares are so much smaller, it's an easier problem to solve to try and grow that business, at least conceptually. Sales is not easy if nobody's heard of you, so you really got to get in the door and go to the customers and talk to them and work that network for sure.

But they have a bit of an easier job growing, especially if they're in a growing industry, so then they can just ride those tailwinds. Big public company that's very established market leader. Dilutive acquisition might be the only game in town, or returning money to shareholders might be the only game in town. I think maybe the stealthy, wealthy version of that is, well, reaching the end of my life cycle going to retire. I don't know who's going to take over the business, so maybe I'll just sell this thing to private equity and ride off into the sunset on my yacht. That's what some of those stories look like. It's returning the money to shareholders, as opposed to burning the thing down to maintain control.

ADVERTISEMENT: Every Sunday, we cover the week's tech news on this Weekend Tech. Hi, this is Leo Laporte from the TWiT Podcast Network, inviting you to join me this week for a doozy. Jason Calacanis and Gina Smith join me. We're going to talk about so many things. The flying car from Tesla, the guy who bugged the OpenAI conference room, wrapped it in toilet paper, and the new Surface laptop -- it’s all coming up on TWiT. You'll find it at twit.tv and wherever you get your podcasts.

Rachel Warren: It's interesting to think about how as investors in public companies who gain indirect exposure to these players just by nature of their relationships with some of these big players. When you were writing this book, as we get to the end of our time here together today, I'm curious, What surprised you the most in your research as you were learning about these Main Street entrepreneurs and their businesses?

Eric Zwick: I came at this thinking about the stories on especially like top inequality was maybe one of the early things that we thought about. Piketty’s Capital had just come out, that book that was all about. We're back in the Gilded Age. It's a new Gilded Age. A few billionaire industrialists that are taking over the whole economy. These massive companies, and that's where all the money at the top is. We started to look at, well, what's driving the top 1% share in income? Which in Picketty's book, the top 1% is this nameless, faceless group of people. You don't really get a lot of humans. We joke about the book as Piketty with people, but it’s like you actually meet these people that are in the top 1% and see what they’re doing and get a little bit about their stories from their own mouths. You're, Oh, this is not the typical one percenter or even 0.1 percenter is not like the money bags monopolist, Gilded Age person. It's like a private business owner with a mid market business. There are tons of these people. The top 1%. When we looked at narrow industries, the industry generating the most profits for their owners was doctors' offices. I was, Oh, doctors' offices, that doesn't feel like a capital intensive business at all.

That's really just they are running practices, they've structured as businesses, so they can pay themself a bit lower wages, but higher profits get better tax treatment, but also scale the business up and sell some of the services and so on, that doctors do. But there's just a ton of doctors, and healthcare is 18% of the economy, and a big chunk of it is not hospitals or pharmaceuticals. It's just services being provided by these people. I was, Okay, this is a totally different view on inequality and who's really rich than I entered and you just trying to answer that question. That blew my mind.

The more we talk to people or tell them about what we're learning in the data, not just economists or academics, but just like friends, family, people that we encounter, conferences, and so on, people that I play golf with. They just know all these people. They know these stories. They recognize the stories pretty quickly from their own lives, from their own backgrounds, from where they grew up, from people they know, their regional banker, from who they're lending to, the magnet manufacturer, this kind of thing. I think you realize they're all around us and we just haven't really been looking for them in the right way. It was very eye-opening to me, and I think a lot of people who have read the book, which was written not just to be collected, but also to be enjoyed and read, will have that eye-opening experience, I believe.

Rachel Warren: One final question for you as well. Just as investors who are hearing all of this and drawing on the lessons that you've showed us today, I also wonder, what are some key elements, metrics we can be looking at tracking the health of the economy, tracking the health markets. If I'm an investor listening, trying to see how can I be tracking the health of the economy and the markets over the next 12-18 months? What would you be looking at?

Eric Zwick: That's good. Thinking about these Everywhere Millionaires, some of the more aggregate, tangible retail sales, consumption data. These businesses are often selling, say, the fast casual dining. You're looking at those kinds of metrics. That's not just quarterly earnings, but really what's going on with consumption? Are people able to spend money or people pulling back because prices have gone up? I think that's an important thing going forward on the economy and that's where through a lot of these types of businesses, they'll grow if customers keep coming in the door and they'll stop growing. They won't close, but they'll stop growing if traffic slows down. Then if you're in the supply chain, there are a lot of cost shocks. We're looking at some of the PPI numbers to think about where are inputs, especially tight.

What are some of the second and third order effects of, say, energy prices going up? Well, fertilizer goes into food, so food costs, or you have all the distribution logistics and trucking and so on, that uses fuel. Well, that means like everything that moves around that needs to go from warehouse to consumer, warehouse to store. That's going to be more expensive. You track some of that stuff. A lot of them Main Street millionaires are running the businesses along that supply chain or in those input providing. You can see in some of the aggregate data that comes out of the statistical agencies, you can see the health of the economy that way. I think those are some of the problems that we face today. I'm looking at some of those statistics to see where things are going in the next 12-18.

Rachel Warren: Thank you so much for that, Eric. It's been so great to speak with you today, and for those who are listening or watching, check out Eric's new book co-authored with Owen Zidar, The Everywhere Millionaire: Who Is Really Rich In America and How They Got There. #3004. Bill Belichick, snubbed from Pro Football Hall of Fame last year, among 5 coach nominees advancing in candidacy for 2027 class

The Pro Football Hall of Fame voting process came under fire and was thrust under a microscope last winter when Bill Belichick, arguably the greatest coach in NFL history, was denied first-ballot status.

Almost a year later, the Hall is moving forward in its annual consideration of potential new inductees. Belichick is once again up for enshrinement.

He's among five former NFL head coaches — the others being Tom Coughlin, Mike Holmgren, Dan Reeves and Mike Shanahan — who have advanced in their candidacy to be elected into the 2027 class, the Hall announced Thursday.

Members from the coach subcommittee whittled down a list that originally included 14 nominees. Next, the subcommittee will thoroughly review the five remaining coaches in contention on Nov. 17, when it meets virtually to pick one of them to present to the full selection committee.

Belichick, Coughlin, Holmgren, Reeves and Shanahan all reached this stage of the selection process last year. Ultimately, Belichick — best known for leading the New England Patriots to six Super Bowl victories during his dynastic run as the franchise's head coach — was picked as the candidate from that category to be up for election.

Keep in mind, of a class' 15 finalists presented at the selection committee's annual meeting, only one coach and one "contributor" are included. The other 13 nominees are modern-era players.

A coach hasn't been enshrined into the Hall since former Cardinals and Chargers frontman Don Coryell went in posthumously as part of the 2023 class.

To be enshrined, a candidate must receive at least 80% approval from the member selection committee. Previously, that usually meant a candidate needed 40 votes. Since, however, the board of selectors has shrunk to 28, and just 25 of them will actually vote.

Even though Belichick was the subcommittee's pick to be in the running for induction last year, he still had to meet that 80% threshold while competing with a contributor — ironically, Patriots owner Robert Kraft — and three "seniors" — Kenny Anderson, Roger Craig and L.C. Greenwood — in a separate part of the ballot. A minimum of one person and a maximum of three from those combined categories could be elected, per then-existing Hall bylaws.

Through all of that complicated mess, Belichick fell short of the necessary votes, leading to ubiquitous outrage, with countless people vouching for Belichick, including his longtime quarterback, Tom Brady, and even Kraft.

Craig wound up emerging from the group of nominees that featured Belichick last year. The iconic San Francisco 49ers running back eventually gave a touching pre-recorded speech upon his induction, during which he divulged that he has vascular dementia.

Back in September, significant changes were announced to the Hall's selection process. One of those was the elimination of the "seniors" category (every player will now be considered in the same pool).

Another was the reduction of the selection committee, effectively from 50 to 25 members (there are three alternates in the current system).

Under the Hall's latest bylaws, an induction class can consist of between three and seven honorees. It still takes 80% approval for enshrinement.

Also notable, the Hall decided its selection committee will return to discussing and submitting their final votes in person. That dialogue, in conjunction with the final voting process, had been held virtually since the COVID-19 pandemic, as reported by The Associated Press.

In early 2027, the selection committee will cast ballots to determine who earns spots in the upcoming class.

On Wednesday, the Hall announced that Kraft is one of the five contributors who have advanced in their candidacy to be elected into the 2027 class.

The class will be announced at "NFL Honors" in Los Angeles in February ahead of Super Bowl LXI, then enshrined in August in Canton, Ohio.

#2999. Apollo to buy nearly 10% stake in Abu Dhabi-backed alternative asset manager Mubadala Capital

Investing giant Apollo Global Management is finalizing a deal to buy nearly a 10% stake in Mubadala Capital, the alternative asset manager subsidiary of Abu Dhabi's sovereign wealth fund, Mubadala Investment Co., according to people familiar with the matter who spoke on condition of anonymity.

The deal is expected to be announced later Monday morning, they said. Financial terms were not immediately known.

The transaction is significant on multiple fronts. It further advances Apollo's ambitions in Abu Dhabi often called the "capital of capital." The firm has spent the past 15 years building key strategic relationships there, with both the country's investment arm and its core leadership.

Apollo already has a multibillion-dollar partnership with Mubadala Investment Co., first established in 2022 and extended two years ago. At the time of that extension, Apollo said the agreement would strengthen the firm's Capital Solutions platform by enabling the firms to jointly source and provide customized debt and equity financing to companies worldwide.

In 2023, the institutions formed a $2.5 billion joint venture designed specifically to co-invest in global private credit opportunities over a five-year horizon.

For Mubadala Capital specifically — the region's only large-scale asset manager, advising and administering more than $755 billion in assets — the deal further advances its growth strategy under the direction of CEO Hani Barhoush, and its relevance on the global investing stage. It also marks an inflection point for the company's core alternatives business, which has grown to more than $60 billion.

John Zito, co-president of Apollo's Asset Management arm, and Barhoush have worked closely together over the past several years and, according to sources, their close-knit relationship and the success of prior partnerships led to talks of deepening ties. So did the belief that scale in the asset management business matters more than ever.

"Capital as a moat is a big thing," said one person familiar with the deal.

Importantly, the deal also continues a notable shift in the relationship between American investment firms and the Middle East. While the region, and Abu Dhabi specifically, has long been a sought-after source of capital, this deal further reverses that dynamic, with Apollo making a significant direct investment in the country and its investment vision.

Mubadala Capital's international offices include London, New York, Rio de Janeiro and San Francisco.

Apollo's investment in Mubadala Capital is similar to deals the firm has done with other asset management platforms, including Diameter Capital, Thoreau, Motive Partners and others.

Spokespersons for both firms declined to comment.

#2996. Is this the best version of Lakers star Luka Dončić we've ever seen?

SACRAMENTO – The Los Angeles Lakers aren't even 10 games into the season and superstar Luka Dončić already looks to be in midseason form.

Dončić had his third-consecutive 40-point game, and fifth of the season, as he led the Lakers to a 116-103 win against the Sacramento Kings at Golden 1 Center on Wednesday, Nov. 4. He stuffed the stat sheet with 40 points, nine rebounds, seven assists, one block and one steal.

Dončić leads the league in scoring so far with 38.5 points per game. He eclipsed Michael Jordan for most points through 8 games since the 1988-89 season with his Wednesday performance (Jordan had 303, Dončić reached 308).

Simple and plain, the man is on a heater. And so are the Lakers, now 7-2, after their third straight win.

"Probably this and the entire month of March last year," Lakers head coach JJ Redick said about Dončić and his scoring streak. "I think that back-to-back we had where he closed out Houston (and) ... then we went to Miami and got in at 4 a.m., and then he had 60, I mean, that stretch was pretty magical for him, but he's playing great right now."

Lakers guard Austin Reaves thought Dončić could have poured in a 60-point night on Wednesday, blaming a slow start in the first quarter where Dončić finished with 10 points.

"He could've had 60 if he wasn't asleep in the first quarter," Reaves told reporters. "He's scored 70 in a game. He's good. He's a good basketball player. He's been doing this since he was 19."

The way he's started the season, Dončić is reminding the league what things can be like when he's healthy.

He averaged 37.5 points per game last March, including 7 out of 16 games with 40 or more points. But Dončić then suffered a season-ending Grade 2 hamstring injury on April 2. He spent the entire offseason getting his leg back to where it used to be, even forgoing international FIBA play for his home country of Slovenia.

Dončić's health has lots to do with his blazing start to the season, but he's hesitant to say it's the best he's felt in his career, now in his 9th NBA season.

"I don't know, honestly," Dončić told USA TODAY Sports. "Too early to tell, you know. But I look back and I had a whole summer to recover. A lot of stuff that I couldn't do last maybe eight years. So maybe that's where the energy is coming from."

Redick focused on adding a new aspect to his 27-year-old superstar's game to relieve some of the ballhandling and fatigue that builds from that task alone. His adjustment are paying dividends early on.

"He's doing a great job. You know, playing off the ball," Redick said. "We're trying to set off ball screens for him. Other guys... I think some of that workload from bringing the ball up against pressure every single possession ... it's very intentional."

The Kings tried various defensive schemes to slow Dončić. Kings head coach Doug Christie added a 2-2-1 full court defense that falls back into a zone in the halfcourt.

"Players like Luka have such abilities to play so many different ways that you have to give looks that constantly keep them guessing," Christie said.

"This is one best players in the world and Luka is special," he added. "You're not gonna lock him down and stop him, but you have to have the ability to frustrate him, force him into shots that we consider good shots, which are above the break threes that are contested, middy's that are contested, and live with it. But what you don't want to do is put him on free throw line."

Dončić's much-improved defense also deserves some attention. He's averaging 0.7 blocks and 1.9 steals per game early in the 2026-27 season. He's particularly been better at closing out on shots, defending ballhandlers on the drive and challenging them inside the paint.

Dončić leads the league so far in defense inside the arc, holding opponents to 21.9 percent 2-point field goal shooting, which is lower than players like Miles Bridges, LeBron James, Donovan Clingan, Ronald Holland, Victor Wembanyama and Ausar Thompson.

It's looks like the 2026-27 season could be Dončić's revenge tour with how well he's played on both sides. Through the first month of the season, he's an early favorite for MVP.

#2994. Raiders owner Mark Davis is "not a fan" of gambling on sports

It's fitting that the owner of the team headquartered in the nation's gambling capital doesn't like gambling on sports.

"I'm not a fan per se of gambling on sports, especially the one-on-ones, the proposition types of betting," Raiders owner Mark Davis told Mike Ozanian of CNBC. "I think the key thing is the integrity of the game."

Indeed it is. The integrity of the game — and the perception of integrity.

"People have to believe in the game," Davis said. "Is it worth the risk? I don't know. I can't answer that."

Back when the NFL hated gambling (before it realized how much money it could make by jumping in bed with the sports books), Commissioner Roger Goodell said this: 'If gambling is permitted freely on sporting events, normal incidents of the game such as bad snaps, dropped passes, turnovers, penalties, and play calling inevitably will fuel speculation, distrust, and accusations of point-shaving or game-fixing."

Now that sports gambling and prediction markets premised on sports outcomes have proliferated, there's a constant undercurrent of suspicion that the fix is in. The NFL can pretend all it wants that it's not happening, but it is. As long as it's not impacting the bottom line, the NFL will continue to look the other way.

One big scandal could change that, permanently.

For now, Davis is pragmatic, and candid, about the ensuing tension.

"The revenue that is coming from gambling and those types of entities is large so there has to be a balance between integrity and . . . the good things that it can bring to the league," Davis said.

The only "good things" are money. Directly from the deals with sports books and the ability of owners to hold up to five percent of the equity in companies that operate sports books. And indirectly from the enhanced ratings that come from viewers who have action on a given game or the players in that game.

The indirect benefits were always there, even when gambling was illegal in all states except the state where Davis's team plays. They would still be there if the NFL were to resume its hard line against gambling in all forms.

But it's too late for that. The money is baked into the budgets. And the NFL isn't about to give up a viable revenue stream with which it has become fully accustomed.

#2992. Celtics answered a big question with clutch win over Bucks

When the notification hit that Jayson Tatum would be resting Wednesday's game against the Bucks, I couldn't have been less moved.

Milwaukee is bad. The Celtics shouldn't have any problem taking care of business, right?

Ultimately, they managed to get across the finish line, but the path there wasn't as pretty as many would've hoped. The Cs found themselves in a fourth-quarter dogfight despite leading the one-win Bucks by 22 points earlier in the game.

This scenario was more suprising than it should've been. Tatum has done a lot for this Boston team. Key role players like Payton Pritchard and Derrick White haven't played at the height of their offensive abilities so far this season, and you've felt it.

Take Monday's loss to the Rockets in Houston for example. Paul George had the night off on the second leg of a back-to-back, and the vet was dearly missed. Whenever Tatum headed to the bench, Houston gained more and more leverage until they were in complete control of the matchup in the second half.

In a game where the Celtics lost by 16, Tatum was still a +1 when he was on the floor.

Flash forward back to the matchup with Milwaukee. Boston was forced to fight their way out of a tough situation without their offensive hub.

A Brayden Burries floater gave Milwaukee a 96-95 lead over Boston with 4:54 to play in regulation. All of the momentum was with the Bucks as they continued to light the nets on fire with their efficient shot-making. Anytime the Cs squandered opportunities on the offensive end, it felt like it came back to bite them with a Bucks bucket.

It felt like it was going to be a classic "the Celtics can't close out close games" game. But instead of squandering what should've been a surefire win, they let their vets lead the way and finished the night with a 14-6 run over the final five minutes.

George and White weren't perfect, but they were good enough. The two combined to score 20 of Boston's 22 fourth-quarter points, and each made some tough shots to ice the game.

Look, I'm not going to try and spin a narrow win against one of the league's worst into a monumental victory. But it's not nothing. If this Celtics squad is going to reach its potential, then other players besides Tatum have to be effective in tense spots. Those spots, by the way, don't just happen in the fourth quarter.

Clutch moments happen all the time. It could be making a big shot to cool off a second-quarter run, getting a big stop on a player who's starting to heat up, or simply making the opposition pay for their mistakes and capitalizing on turnovers and extra possessions.

Those opportunities are undisputedly going to arise during the non-Tatum minutes, too.

The fact that Boston has been able to depend on Paul George thus far feels tremendous. It can never been mentioned enough how great he's been on both sides of the ball. Even today, one of the biggest moments in the game came when he turned defense into offense.

George jumped a weakside pass for a steal, then went coast-to-coast for an and-one finish in transition to give the Cs a six-point cushion early in the fourth.

The moment sent a message. The Celtics weren't just going to roll over because things hadn't been going their way.

So far, they've buckled down quite well when push has come to shove. Wednesday's win makes the Cs 4-1 in clutch situations, which the league defines as games within five points with under five minutes left. This one just happened to be an opportunity to flex their depth.

#2989. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

#2985. Bucks vs. Celtics Player Grades: Kuzma and Jaquez sell in failed comeback

The Milwaukee Bucks' 22-point comeback fell short in Beantown, as they lost to the Boston Celtics 109-102 after leading in the fourth quarter. If the Bucks could just avoid one horrendous quarter each game, they'd probably have three or four wins by now. The fight and character the team showed was pleasing, to be sure, but fans want to see the team not just be in games, but win them, no matter the opponent. And as I'll get to, the difference between winning and losing is razor-thin; it is about recognising moments and understanding what the team wants to achieve in those moments; unfortunately, the Bucks have some guys who seem incapable of doing that. On a completely unrelated note, I am officially retiring from inserting sarcastic/self-deprecating dinosaur-related Easter eggs into my writing until I can make it clear to readers that they are, in fact, sarcastic/self-deprecating dinosaur-related Easter eggs! Read our full summary of the game here and catch a six-minute audio recap on the Bucks+ podcast, Bucks In Six Minutes, below.

23 minutes, 13 points, 9 rebounds, 5 assists, 1 steal, 1 block, 4 turnovers, 5/10 FG, 2/3 3P, +3

Finally, Rollins had what I think everyone would call a solid game (minus the turnovers). His shot still isn't falling as we'd like, but man, Rollins was a menace on defence. Just terrific on that end. He was given the primary assignment on Derrick White and forced him into a 7/18 night. Rightfully got the close alongside Herro.

32 minutes, 25 points, 5 rebounds, 3 assists, 1 turnover, 8/17 FG, 4/10 3P, -8

Herro's night was highlighted by a white-hot third quarter in which he scored 17 of his 23 points on 6/8 shooting (after failing to score in the opening quarter). It felt like Herro's aggression flipped a switch at halftime: he came off screens with more vigour, his crossovers were more violent, and he had a stronger will to get to the hoop. Unfortunately, Herro took just one shot in the fourth; not sure whose fault that is, but he needed to be more involved (at least in the final few possessions, Mr Kuzma).

30 minutes, 18 points, 2 rebounds, 2 assists, 1 steal, 7/16 FG, 4/6 3P , -3

On the plus side, Jaquez continues to be money from three this year (although he did take a horrendous one late, as Turner was wide open on the extra pass). On the negative side, he went 0/5 in the fourth quarter and couldn't get anything to go inside the arc. Still, I thought Jaquez was solid in his possessions guarding PG.

27 minutes, 6 points, 5 rebounds, 4 assists, 1 steal, 3 turnovers, 2/5 FG, 1/3 3P, -8

If you looked at the stat sheet, you would think Kuzma played just a meh game, and I suppose he did, but I can't get over how incredibly frustrating some of his decisions are. As I've said before, no matter how he plays overall, Kuzma is guaranteed to give you 2-4 make-you-want-to-pull-your-hair-out plays per game. Most notably, he shot a three off one pass with 18 on the shot clock while down two with three minutes to play. Why!? He also had a play in the third—as the Bucks are desperately trying to string stops together—where he flopped off a slight push-off from Paul George; the refs (rightfully) didn't fall for it, and he was on the ground when George shot and made the three. He had another pathetic defensive possession on PG late, where he was effectively standing straight up—not in a defensive stance at all—as the 36-year-old zipped past him with ease. Kuzma was rightfully pulled for AJ Green to finish, and I honestly think they win if he didn't play in the fourth. Just an infuriating player to watch sometimes.

35 minutes, 12 points, 9 rebounds, 6 assists, 2 steals, 2 blocks, 1 turnover, 4/6 FG, 4/6 3P, -1

I thought Turner got monstered on the inside a few too many times—as he is wont to do—but overall, it was another well-rounded game where you really felt his presence. Shooting the ball much better of late. Just keep stacking good games.

15 minutes, 2 points, 5 rebounds, 1 block, 1/6 FG, 0/2 3P, -6

12 minutes, 3 points, 1 rebounds, 1 assist, 0 turnovers, 1/2 FG, 1/2 3P, -15

Tough game for Jakucionis, who didn't play in the second half and had by far the worst plus/minus on the team (the next-worst was -8). I don't think he necessarily did too much wrong in his minutes; it's more that I don't think Jenkins liked how the team operated when he was running it, and he loved how the team ran in the second half with someone else handling the rock. Things were going well, and as the old saying goes, "If it ain't broke, don't fix it."

22 minutes, 9 points, 5 rebounds, 3 assists, 1 steal, 3 turnovers, 3/7 FG, 0/3 3P, 3/4 FT, +5

We still haven't seen Burries explode on offence in the extended minutes that come with starting (granted, his two starts have been against top-10 defensive teams), but he still did a bunch of typical Burries stuff. He has a knack for impacting the game regardless of whether his shot is going in, a quality I absolutely love. Burries was super active and impactful defensively and pursued each rebound like his life depended on it. Also, the rook was on the bench for most of that electric third-quarter run, but I noticed him up and encouraging his teammates the whole time—brownie points.

20 minutes, 5 points, 2 rebounds, 3 assists, 1/5 FG, 1/4 3P, -4

I feel like a bit of a broken record with Green when I say that even when he doesn't hit shots, it never feels like he impacts the game negatively the way it does with a certain someone. He generally does his job on defence, even if he is undersized in a lot of matchups, and it's just make-or-miss as to how much he plays. Had a killer defensive possession guarding Paul George in the first half.

18 minutes, 9 points, 2 rebounds, 3/9 FG, 2/6 3P, +5

As has been the case a few times this season, Ament had a meh first half and a much better second. He started the game with a major rookie moment, trying to take on Mitchell Robinson and getting it emphatically thrown back in his face, which earned him a seat on the bench immediately. Then in the second half, Ament was just… existing more as part of a coherent unit that was playing extremely well? Like, he took his chances when they came to him instead of forcing stuff, but he also contributed on the other end, which matters to me much more than his scoring. Now, Ament still has a little too much tunnel vision, and I'd like him to set screens and not slip them as much as he does—which will generate scoring chances for him—but hey, encouraging signs.

On the one hand, I totally get going back to your veterans to close a game on the road against a good team—and there are certainly other considerations—but Jenkins closing with Kuzma and Jaquez may well have cost them the game. Maybe Ament should have stayed in (though I reckon he'd played a fair stretch by that point), but definitely Green. Heck, Jenkins could have dusted off Jericho Sims, who played well the game before. Other than that, credit to the Bucks coach for getting the team in the right mindset—and with the right game plan—to mount that comeback.

Limited Minutes: Ousmane Dieng, Jericho Sims

DNP-CD: Bogoljub Markovic, Caris LeVert, Gary Trent Jr.

Inactive: Kam Jones, Cormac Ryan, Pete Nance

The team is guaranteed to lose its collective mind for one quarter of each game.

Jenkins on his team's fight: "Can't overlook that we were down 22 to the Celtics and came back and took the lead… the energy, the life was there… A lot of growth, a lot of progress. This was awesome by our group. We have to continue to not dig ourselves holes… Overall, our guys' resilience was the biggest thing from this game."

Regarding my comments about Brayden Burries' ability to impact beyond scoring, I want Nate Ament to learn from that; it feels like Ament is much more "out there" a lot of the time, rather than you feeling his presence from possession to possession. Now, the fact that Ament is even playing regular NBA minutes at this point is huge in and of itself, so I don't want to put too much on the kid, but it's just something I'd love for him to be thinking about when he's out there.

I found it notable that Brayden Burries matched up with Derrick White, and many draft analysts used White as his player comparison. I wonder if Burries was thinking the same thing.

The Bucks averaged one turnover per minute in the opening six minutes of the game. Crazy.

Tyler Herro started the first quarter scoreless; the same thing happened against the Wolves.

The Bucks shot 14/42 in the first half, and by the halfway mark of the third quarter, they were 10/12. Also, they had just one turnover in the entire third.

The Bucks head home now and will open their NBA Cup campaign Friday night against the Toronto Raptors. Catch the action at 7:00 p.m. CST on MY24.

#2974. Knicks 135, Bulls 105: “We’re so back…”

As the second quarter started last night between the New York Knicks and the Chicago Bulls at Madison Square Garden, Josh Giddey was bringing the ball up for the team from the Windy City. He tried to maneuver through a high pick-and-roll when all of a sudden a large human named OG Anunoby appeared in his path. The Australian must have thought he'd run into a boxing kangaroo, as he was so freaked out that he immediately lost his dribble. As the ball was bouncing away from him, Anunoby lunged after it but couldn't quite grab it. So Mikal Bridges joined the scene as well for the orange and blue, also diving on the floor to ultimately force the refs to call a foul on poor Mr. Giddey.

Just for the record, the Knicks were leading 43-21 at that point.

The game was essentially over long before both of New York's high-priced wings draped themselves across the shiny Garden floor, but this is exactly what makes the symbolism of the aforementioned play so much more distinct. The Knicks decided to show up last night. They decided to put it together and be the hard-nosed, all-out championship team we all love so much. Not only for the layup lines or the press conference but for the entire game. They not only cut up the Bulls into sirloins, briskets and ribs, they surely also took their hearts. And announced, if not to the league, then to their fan base, what Keef so aptly commented in Russell's Postgame Thread: "We are so back…"

A critical comrade might point out that it had more to do with Chicago being gone than with New York being back, but this isn't the day for such rationality. It's a day of basking in the glory of being a good basketball team and showing it for 47 minutes and 58 seconds, maybe even longer. It's exactly what this Knicks team needed after sluggish weeks to start their championship defense, and some of us fans might have needed it even a bit more. We all knew they were good enough to win it all, we all knew that there's a level to their quality that might be unrivaled across the Association. At some point you have to see it, though, and we sure did last night.

From Jalen Brunson to Landry Shamet, from OG Anunoby to Mohamed Diawara, even from James Wiseman to Dalton Knecht, there basically was no Knick who didn't look like his very best last night, at least not if we ignore garbage time and a rusty Jose Alvarado being dealt the thankless job of driving the party boat into the harbor. Maybe our Finals hero was a bit tired from all the clapping and cheering he did for a seemingly gazillion beautiful basketball plays from his teammates—even if SGA Anunoby gave him plenty of breaks standing at the charity stripe. All in all, the Knicks definitely played at a completely different speed than the Bulls, who only a week ago had raced out to a 19-point lead against New York. Watching this game, you wonder how that ever happened.

No matter what conclusion you might come to answering that question, last night proved that it's a lot more fun to bring your A-game and best effort from the get-go than midway through the fourth, fueled by desperation. Jalen Brunson danced through the Bulls D like he was Barry Sanders playing a high school team early on and also had no mercy shooting the ball. As so often in the past, it inspired his team to follow him. The Knicks did it all the way to the highest-scoring half in their history, tying a 38-year-old franchise record. It's nice to be back doing historical things just as they routinely did last season. And just like your starters and stars diving on the floor in a lopsided game, it is a reminder that these Knicks are still those Knicks.

Whether they might be even a bit more here or there could start to show itself in games like last night's laugher. It is here where younger, unproven bench players like Mo Diawara, Dalton Knecht or James Wiseman will get their chances to make an impression on Mike Brown and the coaching staff. All of them have a ways to go, but watching last night, you at least feel a little intrigued by Mo's and Wiseman's physical profiles. Their length could give New York a different defensive dimension (5 blocks between them), and Diawara once again looked like the promising role player he teased last season. The herky-jerky is still there, yet he also flashed strong playmaking ability (tied for the team lead with 4 assists) and defensive awareness against supposedly strong wings like Caleb Wilson or Matas Buzelis. Maybe he can be a specialist for matchups like that, who knows? We've got 74 games to see more of it and figure out if it can really be a factor in important games.

We're not going out on a limb saying that it will take some time before the Bulls get to experience such games again. This doesn't diminish what our Knickerbockers did last night, playing as complete a basketball game as you dare to imagine. As beautiful as some of the passes, shots or dunks were, in the end, the foundation was the little things, many of which are exactly what a large part of the team's identity is built upon. Things like boxing out your opponent, making the right rotation or flying hard around a screen. Things like making the pass that leads to an assist or pushing the ball when it truly makes sense.

Or diving on the floor after a loose ball, for that matter.

#2965. Investors are doing just as well this year by shunning AI. How to invest if the tech trade keeps fizzling

A version of the S&P 500 without AI briefly pulled ahead of the traditional index this year and is now neck-and-neck as investors rotate into other areas of the market.

A proprietary Goldman Sachs index tracking the S&P 500 without stocks classified as "AI enablers" was up 12.4% through Oct. 22, edging ahead of the standard S&P 500's 12% gain. The Goldman index is now neck-and-neck with the S&P 500 as tech rallied in Monday trading.

Tech and AI-related companies make up about 45% of the S&P 500. That heavy weighting has helped power the index for years, but it's turned into a liability lately; the S&P 500 tech sector is down about 7% this month while other sectors have held up.

Investors have soured on debt and equity issued by companies like CoreWeave and Oracle. Shares of Meta Platforms sank almost 7% Thursday after it outlined more plans to spend heavily on capex and issue additional shares.

Companies supplying power and equipment for the buildout have been hit hard — names like GE Vernova, Bloom Energy, Vistra and Quanta Services all suffered steep declines during the recent selloff.

Goldman, which launched the measure earlier this year with S&P, uses the S&P 500 as its starting universe and excludes companies perceived to be enablers of AI before applying S&P 500 Index weighting methodology to the remaining universe of companies.

The ex-AI index lagged the S&P 500 for most of the year as investors continued to favor chipmakers, cloud-computing companies and other firms benefiting from heavy AI spending. However, that advantage has been narrowing and it disappeared last week.

Meanwhile, investors are rotating into defensive areas. ETFs like State Street Consumer Staples Select Sector SPDR ETF, State Street Health Select SPDR ETF and State Street Utilities Select Sector SPDR ETF are up 7% to 10% this month. Energy is ahead 10% too, though that's more related to the war in Iran.

Some analysts recommend sticking with non-AI companies for diversification and protection against more pain in tech. The "AI pendulum has swung back in favor of non-AI exposure over the last two weeks," Oppenheimer technical analyst Ari Wald said in a note on Oct. 24.

Two industries that Oppenheimer favors now are commercial services and insurance. The firm upgraded those industries to overweight last week, recommending Republic Services, Cintas, Verisk Analytics, Broadridge Financial Solutions, MetLife and Arthur J. Gallagher.

Berkshire Hathaway is also capitalizing on the rotation out of tech. Its Class B shares are up 8% this month, though it's still lagging behind the S&P 500 this year.

Berkshire owns a wide swath of operating companies, including insurance giant Geico and Burlington Northern Santa Fe railroad. It also has a $364 billion stock portfolio including companies like Apple, Coca-Cola, Alphabet and Bank of America. That diversification may help Berkshire continue to outperform if the tech sector drags down the S&P 500.

Trivector Research sees potential in "non-tech compounders." Healthcare giant Eli Lilly is one idea. The stock is up 22% on the strength of Lilly's weight-loss drugs. The company has more in the pipeline, including a next-generation obesity drug that Lilly plans to file for FDA approval early in 2027.

Viking Holdings, a cruise operator, is also proving durable. The company said on its August earnings call that 40% of its river capacity and 60% of its ocean capacity was already booked for 2027. "Based on recent events, we've seen our guests have proved to be resilient and are continuing to book 2027 and future seasons," the company told investors.

Viking stock is up 15% this year but it may still have upside. The average Wall Street target is $109, above recent prices around $82. Barclays recently upgraded the cruise operator to overweight with a $102 target, citing the company's "compelling risk/reward."
