#2962. Highlights: Victor Wembanyama puts on a 52-point masterclass against Mavericks

Debuting their ABA-inspired classic jerseys and classic court, the Spurs showed out for the Frost Bank Center against their I-35 rivals in the Dallas Mavericks. After taking control of the first half and extending their lead to 19 in the third quarter, the Mavericks clawed their way back in the fourth. Clutch plays from Victor Wembanyama and Dylan Harper kept the Spurs within one possession down the stretch, and Wemby drained a go-ahead free throw with 24 seconds left. Kyrie Irving missed a potential go-ahead jumper, and the Mavs failed to corral the rebound. The Spurs extended their win streak to six.

Speaking of Wemby, Holy moly, folks. Wemby channeled his inner Wilt Chamberlain against the Dallas Mavericks. Coming off a win over the Utah Jazz, Wemby looked to shake off the rust from his rough shooting night (12 points, 5-15 FG, 0-5 3PT). He responded by breaking a franchise and league record in the first half: 29 points, 15 rebounds, five blocks. He then cashed in a career-best performance: 52 points (16-28 FG, 19-23 FT), 24 rebounds, eight blocks, two steals, and two assists. From the opening tip, Wemby got to work immediately on Daniel Gafford. The Space Jam-esque leap after the crossover is otherworldly.

Besides putting it on the floor, Wemby gained the upper hand over defenders by using his height and agility. Throughout the first quarter and bleeding into others, Wemby was a lob threat every time. He spun off of Cooper Flagg, fought through contact, and finished many lobs as he dominated the paint.

Wemby kept up the aggression with countless drives to the basket. The Mavericks could not guard him one-on-one, so the result was often Wemby getting fouled on drives, lob attempts, putback attempts, and most of the time the contact did not even matter.

Here's what separates Wemby from almost all big men, he can go behind the back on one end, control and spin on the other, and leap for a wide-open jam whenever he wants. Simply ridiculous.

Oh yeah, in case you forgot, he's the reigning Defensive Player of the Year. Wemby swatted EIGHT shots, including this sequence where he blocked the Mavs THREE TIMES IN A ROW. Again, simply and utterly ridiculous.

As the aggression continued in the second half, the Mavericks transitioned to playing small ball to counteract the defensive anchor of Wemby. Wemby punished them with a handful of finishes at the rim and turning in a couple of and-ones.

He dominated the glass with nine of his 24 rebounds coming on the offensive end (which mostly resulted in putbacks). Overall, this is just another reminder of how Wemby is an MVP (and DPOY) candidate this season. With the team as a whole still figuring out their offense while running a three-guard lineup, Wemby's talent saved the day and can continue to carry this team to close out victories. Spurs fans, rejoice and continue to feel blessed every day.

Dylan Harper played Robin to Wemby's Batman with 21 points (9-15 FG) and five rebounds. Dylan, as a starter, continues to lead the league in field goal percentage at the rim. His fearlessness on drives and patience in the paint rival a prime Irving. Playing in a three-guard lineup with De'Aaron Fox and Stephon Castle, Dylan has been more of a scorer of the three. As the team's offense continues to find a consistent identity, one thing is for sure, Dylan will find a way to put the ball in the basket.

#2959. Mike Holmgren again among nominees for Pro Football Hall of Fame

A franchise legend could be Canton bound.

The Pro Football Hall of Fame announced the five final coach nominees for the Class of 2027 on Thursday, and among the quintet is former Seattle Seahawks head coach Mike Holmgren. Joining Holmgren to round out the finalists is Bill Belichick, Tom Coughlin, Dan Reeves, and Mike Shanahan.

In the new Hall of Fame selection process, only one of the five coach nominees will be selected to advance to the full selection committee, which will cast its ballots in the new year.

Holmgren has twice made it to the full selection committee, in 2020 and 2025, but was not elected either time. Last year, Belichick infamously made it to the selection committee only to be denied.

Holmgren coached the Seahawks for 10 seasons, leading the franchise to its first Super Bowl appearance, a 21-10 loss to the Pittsburgh Steelers in Super Bowl XL. He also went to two Super Bowls with the Green Bay Packers, winning Super Bowl 31 before losing Super Bowl 32. Seattle won the division five times during his tenure and made six playoff appearances. He retired as the franchise's all-time leader in coaching wins and was inducted into the team's Ring of Honor in 2021. On Monday night, Holmgren raised the 12 flag before Seattle's game against the Chicago Bears.

#2956. LIV Golf faces liquidation after BC Partners scraps rescue plan

LIV Golf could be headed for liquidation after BC Partners Credit reportedly dropped plans to finance a relaunch of the breakaway league.

The investment firm had pledged up to $300 million for a revamped version of LIV, known as LIV 2.0, but withdrew the plan after LIV Golf failed to secure enough player commitments, according to the Associated Press.

LIV is scheduled to appear in U.S. bankruptcy court Thursday, with the hearing now expected to focus on liquidation of its assets. The league's ultimate fate remains unclear.

LIV Golf and BC Partners did not immediately respond to CNBC's requests for comment.

LIV filed for Chapter 11 bankruptcy protection in September after Saudi Arabia's Public Investment Fund said it would stop funding the league after the 2026 season.

PIF had invested more than $5 billion since LIV launched in 2022, according to its Chapter 11 filing.

BC Partners' proposed relaunch would have made players majority owners of the reorganized league and featured a scaled-back schedule.

But LIV has lost several prominent players, including Jon Rahm and Sergio Garcia, as it sought commitments for the new venture.

Rahm said Tuesday that uncertainty over the league's future and proposed changes to its format influenced his decision to leave.

"There were too many unknowns at the time, and there's still quite a few," he told reporters ahead of the Abu Dhabi Championship. Rahm added that the revamped competition appeared to be moving away from the team format that initially attracted him.

Garcia, who secured a release from his LIV contract in October, has outlined plans to return to the DP World Tour and explore a possible return to the PGA Tour.

Only four players had publicly committed to LIV 2.0: Richard Bland, Cameron Tringale, Brendan Steele and Ben Campbell, according to Golf Digest.

LIV launched in 2022 as a challenger to the PGA Tour, recruiting prominent players including Phil Mickelson, Dustin Johnson and Bryson DeChambeau with lucrative contracts.

Its arrival split men's professional golf, with the PGA Tour suspending members who competed in LIV events. The disputes later spilled into the courts, with players challenging suspensions and the rival organizations pursuing competing legal claims.

The PGA Tour denied the allegations, while LIV dismissed the counterclaims.

A June 2023 framework agreement between the PGA Tour, DP World Tour and PIF ended their litigation and proposed combining their commercial businesses, although the deal was never completed.

#2955. 'You can fight Father Time': Klay Thompson's game-winning 3 seals Heat's comeback OT win over Clippers

Through his first five games as a member of the Miami Heat, Klay Thompson was going through it. The longtime sharpshooter just couldn't find the range in his new home, missing more than two-thirds of his triple tries while struggling to keep up on defense for a Heat club that opened the season scuffling despite the offseason arrival of superstar Giannis Antetokounmpo.

On one hand, it seemed at least possible that Thompson — nearing his 37th birthday, coming off a pair of up-and-down campaigns in Dallas, years removed from his peak as a Splash Brother — just might no longer be the kind of flamethrowing gamebreaker we remember from his days with the dynastic Golden State Warriors. On the other, though … the guy who's made the fourth most 3-pointers in NBA history was going to get hot and swing a game at some point, right?

That point came at Intuit Dome, in Inglewood, Calif., with the visiting Heat trailing the Los Angeles Clippers by two points with 3.3 seconds remaining in overtime of a wild Wednesday night affair:

With the Heat inbounding from the frontcourt, Thompson begins the play by cutting from the free-throw line down toward the baseline, setting a pindown screen to allow Miami point guard Davion Mitchell to flash to the top of the key. That's just window dressing, though: When the Clippers switch that screen, Thompson turns and curls off a screen by Bam Adebayo.

The Heat big man picks off both L.A. defenders in the area, Kobe Sanders and Brook Lopez, giving Klay a clean release to the short corner. From Anthony Chiang of the Miami Herald:

"Bam set one heck of a screen," Spoelstra said.

"Screeners don't usually get a lot of credit, but that's probably my best screen," Adebayo added with a smile.

"I felt like I had so much space," Thompson said. "I'm not going to say that's an easy shot for me, but I've taken so many thousands of corner 3s, especially in that left corner. It's probably the easiest shot on the perimeter for a righty like myself. I got my feet down and planted, and it felt really good coming off my fingertips."

It looked even better splashing through the net, giving the Heat a one-point lead with one second remaining in OT. With no timeouts left, the Clippers could only attempt a three-quarter-court heave that came up empty, allowing Miami to escape with a 117-116 win.

Klay Thompson (left) celebrates with Heat's guard Pelle Larsson after hitting the game-winning 3-pointer to beat the Los Angeles Clippers.

Thompson's triple punctuated a remarkable comeback for the Heat. Miami had looked dead-to-rights in the first half, trailing by as many as 20 points and conceding a mammoth 30-4 run to their hosts fueled by the inside-out scoring of veteran center Lopez and rookie guard Keaton Wagler, who outscored the Heat in the second quarter 21-19 by themselves.

As they came out of intermission, though, the Heat began chipping away.

A defense that had given up 67 first-half points on 60% shooting tightened considerably, holding the Clips to 40 points on 35% shooting with eight turnovers across the third and fourth quarters. Antetokounmpo and Adebayo flexed their muscle, combining for 31 points and 17 free-throw attempts in the second half and the Heat whittled down L.A.'s lead; after a personal 6-0 run by Giannis, they were back within one possession just over a minute into the fourth quarter, putting the game back in the balance.

After a nip-and-tuck fourth quarter that saw the Clippers rebuild a double-digit deficit, the Heat promptly erased it to take a one-point lead on an Adebayo jumper with 1:53 remaining in regulation, and the Clips took back control on a layup by Darius Garland on the ensuing possession, both teams missed golden opportunities to put the game away. Perhaps the most damaging, though: A ghastly turnover by Garland near half-court — one of six on the night for the player who leads the league in cough-ups — that eventually allowed Antetokounmpo to step to the line for two free throws, putting Miami up by one with 10.8 seconds to go:

The Clips still had a chance to win it in regulation, though, with Rui Hachimura drawing a foul on a bang-bang play that was initially called a blocking foul on Pelle Larson. Miami challenged the call, arguing that it should've been a charge; officials deemed that challenge successful, but also called a proximate foul on Adebayo on the play after review, sending Hachimura to the line with 2.7 ticks remaining. He split his pair of free throws, though, and after a missed foul-line jumper by Adebayo on the other end, the game headed to overtime, knotted at 107.

L.A. charged back in front in overtime, with Garland scoring nine of his team-high 26 points in the extra frame. But a jumper by Adebayo got the Heat back within two heading into the final minute, and Thompson's literal last-second 3 stood up as the final margin — much to the relief of the always-intense Erik Spoelstra.

The game-winner was the eighth long-ball of the night for Thompson, who finished with 30 points on 11-for-22 shooting — his first 30-point game in two-and-a-half years. Wednesday's performance continued a tear for the five-time All-Star and four-time NBA champion, who's gone 19-for-36 from long distance over the past three games as he thaws out from that frosty start.

"He's getting his legs under him, and he's getting his game legs," Spoelstra said of Thompson, according to Eric Nehm of The Athletic. "He's in shape, he works, he's really been putting in a lot of time. [...] You can fight Father Time and he's doing it. He's putting in a ton of time on his body, getting in shape, and that's allowing him to show the skill set that he's had throughout his Hall of Fame career."

Thompson, for his part, said after the win that he hasn't "felt this good in a long time."

"No pain in my lower limbs, and I'm so grateful to be here," he said, according to Chiang at the Herald. "I mean, they hold me accountable, the training staff and coaching staff, and I feel amazing. I haven't felt this good to start a season in years, so I'm just really inspired by not only my performance tonight, but our team's."

With the win, Miami improved to 3-5 heading into Thursday's date with Thompson's old friends in Golden State. The Clippers, on the other hand, have now lost seven in a row since winning on opening night, plunging to the bottom of the Western Conference standings alongside the Sacramento Kings and Utah Jazz.

#2953. Fantasy Football Rankings: Hayden Winks' WR Blueprint for Week 9

The Blueprint is my weekly column and basically everything that I use to build out the most accurate rankings I can. There will be an RB column, this WR column and a combination column on the onesie positions. You will find film notes and endless charts. Maybe enough charts to stress test the Yahoo website. I kid.

It's impossible to get every ranking thoroughly early in the week, so be sure to keep checking in on these columns through Sunday morning. As always, my full rankings at every position are available.

1. Jaxon Smith-Njigba (vs. ARI) - 92.5 proj. receiving yards

JSN has 19.3 half PPR points or more in all but one game, as the clear-cut WR1 overall in fantasy. He's had big games with Drew Lock before, including in Week 2 against these Cardinals, when he had 38.0 half PPR points on 155 yards and three scores.

2. Puka Nacua (at WAS) - 92.5 proj. receiving yards

The Rams offense has really changed recently. In two games without Davante Adams, Nacua's average depth of target is just 3.4 yards downfield. Everything is shallow. It's fine in fantasy because he will rack up catches with ease, and he can power through tackles for first downs. But it does make it a bit harder for the offense to be a threat for 35+ points like they were in previous versions. Nacua is the WR3 on WR1 usage and has a 34% target share since returning to the lineup in Week 4. He's up to 41% targets (!!!) without Adams.

The Commanders have allowed the seventh-most passing yards per game with one of the most underwhelming CB rooms. They'll muck things up in a Diet Coke version of the Brian Flores defense, but that fits with the Rams' quick-hitting approach on offense right now. Los Angeles enters as 5.5-point road favorites with a 26.5-point team total (eighth).

3. CeeDee Lamb (at IND) - 81.5 proj. receiving yards

4. Ja'Marr Chase (at ATL) - 78.5 proj. receiving yards

Chase is the WR4 on WR9 usage if we remove his Week 4 injury game. Nothing has changed with this offense, aside from now having one of the best RTs in the NFL, Amarius Mims.

5. Tee Higgins (at ATL) - 77.5 proj. receiving yards

Higgins is the WR4 on WR13 usage this season. He's been the WR1, WR2, WR71, WR7, WR8, WR19 and WR39 this season. The Bengals abandoned their under-center pass game officially and will let Joe Burrow cook as he's done since LSU.

6. Drake London (vs. CIN) - 78.5 proj. receiving yards

Last week's Michael Penix Jr. outing was concerning because of his poor decision-making, but the conditions won't ever be that bad again. It's not a complete throw-out-the-window game, but it's close to it. Before that, London had been averaging fantasy WR1 numbers with Penix dating back to late 2024.

The matchup with the Bengals is pass-first, especially with star CB1 DJ Turner (ankle) not practicing. Cincy's LB play has been amongst the worst in the NFL, and London can hammer teams on quick in cuts, something the Falcons have leaned on more recently. It's a great matchup for London, with Atlanta projected to lose while still owning a quality 24.75-point team total (eighth).

7. Amon-Ra St. Brown (at MIA) - 76.5 proj. receiving yards

8. Malik Nabers (at PHI) - 73.5 proj. receiving yards

9. Chris Olave (vs. CLE) - 71.5 proj. receiving yards

10. Justin Jefferson (vs. BUF) - 77.5 proj. receiving yards

I broke down the Vikings' pass game in detail in another column, but it really comes down to Kyler Murray, Kevin O'Connell and Jordan Addison splitting a lot of the blame for Jefferson's falloff. Over his past 17 games, he's averaging just 50 YPG. It was at 96 YPG before them. This year, Jefferson is the WR18 on WR31 usage, while seeing a lot of bracket coverage. The Lions played outside leverage at CB with a safety on the inside for a lot of the game, and Jefferson was left unprotected from it by the staff. A few inaccuracies and turn-downs from Kyler played into a mediocre game in a great matchup. That said, Jefferson still had a huge red-zone catch, was thrown the ball underneath and Murray's stats are at a career low point. They would likely to get better with more time on task, but now Murray is week-to-week with a hamstring injury. Carson Wentz will start on MNF. I don't know if the Vikings' No. 25 neutral pass rate will change, however. Jefferson is clearly more of a fantasy WR2 than WR1.

He draws an exploitable matchup against a Bills defense that ranks fourth in half PPR points allowed per game to opposing WR rooms (31.1). Buffalo allowed 29.2 points to Puka Nacua and 24.2 points to Tre Tucker in recent weeks. The Vikings are 3.5-point home underdogs carrying an implied team total of 21.5 points (19th).

11. Christian Watson (at NE) - 73.5 proj. receiving yards

12. Nico Collins (at LAC) - 68.5 proj. receiving yards

Collins ranks as the WR19 in per-game half PPR scoring on WR30 expected-points usage over his past four appearances. His recent stretch was keyed by a Week 4 masterclass against Dallas (WR3 overall), but it's tailed off since then while the Texans have limited his snap counts with more help in the offense. Over this four-game sample, he has averaged 6.3 targets and 79.0 air yards on a 21% target share. It boggles the mind that this isn't higher. Hopefully the bye week was enough time for them to figure some things out — like spamming targets to him.

The Chargers rank 17th in half PPR points allowed to wide receivers at 25.3 per game. While they have been vulnerable overall, they have yielded just 5.5 points per game on targets beyond 15 air yards (31st), which poses a test for Collins' deep-target role. Houston enters as 4.5-point road favorites with an implied team total of 22.5 points (16th). Los Angeles hasn't had SCB Derwin James.

13. Matthew Golden (at NE) - 67.5 proj. receiving yards

14. Rashee Rice (vs. NYJ) - 59.5 proj. receiving yards

Rice is the WR21 on WR39 usage, a 19% target share and 6.3 targets per game in his four healthy games. He's finished between WR12 and WR32 in all of them, though he's running pretty pure in terms of efficiency with a sprinkling of broken plays. The good news for him is the Xavier Worthy threat has closed based on his tape and Travis Kelce's YAC greatness has stalled. Rice is back to being needed, even if Kenneth Walker III's presence is going to knock Rice down from his pre-2026 peak.

The matchup is a great one. The Jets have been particularly weak against shallow WRs, allowing the eighth-most production on throws within 10 air yards. Jalen Nailor snuck by with a WR20 finish last week, while role players Caleb Douglas (WR8) and Pop Douglas (WR10) balled out in the games prior. A monster 28.75-point team total has Rice's projection looking the best it has all year.

15. Luther Burden III (vs. TB) - 65.5 proj. receiving yards

Burden played three of 21 snaps in heavy personnel. When the Bears established a lead, they went away from 3-WR sets, which is partially explaining the massive splits between halves for Burden this season. He's the WR3 in yards per route in the first half and the WR61 in the second half. When Johnson hasn't isolated him on a first read, the results are worse. That's the reality of a physically dominant yet in-development player. Burden is the WR24 on WR7 usage in games with Caleb this year, so it's no reason to really panic. He's an upside WR2/3 despite the obvious inconsistency.

16. Parker Washington (at BAL) - 60.5 proj. receiving yards

Washington is extremely volatile. On average, he's the WR20 on WR23 usage. The last time he bottomed out, Washington was the WR3 overall the following week. He's not doing anything wrong. In fact, he's the WR15 in yards per route.

This past week, the Jaguars had five explosives through the air. All of them were to TEs and RBs. That's the magic of Liam Coen's scheme, but when those plays are working and the Colts couldn't move the ball on the other side, then there aren't enough dropbacks to go around. Washington is arguably better in more difficult matchups.

The matchup against Baltimore offers a bounce-back opportunity, as the Ravens rank ninth in half PPR points allowed to WRs at 28.6 per game. Baltimore has struggled against intermediate passing in particular, ranking 10th in points allowed on targets between five and 20 air yards, where Washington has logged 30 targets this season. Jacksonville enters as 8.5-point road favorites with an implied team total of 25.5 points (ninth).

17. George Pickens (at IND) - 62.5 proj. receiving yards

Pickens had his second-lowest usage of the season last week, despite an easy matchup with the Cardinals that even went to overtime. Pickens only played 10 of 28 snaps with more than one TE on the field, allowing Ryan Flournoy to score a red-zone touchdown in a 3-TE set. These are things on the margins that will prevent Pickens from reaching top-15 status while CeeDee Lamb is healthy. Pickens will need to win his difficult assignments as the static X receiver in 3-WR sets. Dak Prescott is talented enough for spike weeks, but the consistency will be painful. Pickens is the WR28 on WR18 usage this season. He's firmly a boom-bust WR2, and I'd understand the Cowboys listening to trade offers for his expiring contract now that they trust Flournoy.

Pickens will battle with Sauce Gardner on the boundary most likely, but the rest of the defense is so overmatched here. The Jaguars moved the ball at will last week with DT DeForest Buckner gone. A monster 28.75-point team total (second) keeps the Pickens' ceiling intact.

18. Michael Wilson (at SEA) - 48.5 proj. receiving yards

I guess I appreciate Wilson's game way more than everyone else. His style is similar to Puka Nacua, although Wilson tends to be a little more out of control compared to the All Pro. How they're used and how physical they are is nearly identical. There have been too many misses by the QBs and too many DPIs/drops to treat him as a set-and-forget WR1, but Wilson is the WR5 in expected half PPR points this season.

He's no longer competing with WRs Marvin Harrison Jr., Kendrick Bourne, Jalen Brooks or Reggie Virgil for targets, and both Jeremiyah Love and Bam Knight also went down with injuries in Week 8. Wilson ended that contest as the WR4 on WR1 overall usage (30.2 expected half PPR points). Have we reached too big to fail? I think so. I'm buying, assuming his two broken ribs aren't going to derail his own season.

The matchup is obviously daunting. The Seahawks have allowed the fewest fantasy points per game to WRs, and they've been elite at stopping big plays over the middle and down the field, where Wilson has done a chunk of his damage. The Cardinals only have a 14.5-point team total with Gardner Minshew walking into an impossible task. Spamming underneath targets to Wilson has to be a big part of the plan.

19. Jameson Williams (at MIA) - 52.5 proj. receiving yards

20. DJ Moore (at MIN) - 47.5 proj. receiving yards

Week 8 was extremely encouraging for Moore. He was the WR13 on WR9 usage, and the scheme choices were great. His TD came from the slot in the high red zone on a run-away route, which suits his game best down there. He had another slot crosser, too. They connected on a deep play-action post against Marlon Humphrey, and then tested the veteran safety on a slant from the five-yard line. Moore is getting used like the most important player in the pass game, one that sits here over the past three years:

The matchup is obviously horrific with the Vikings shutting down everyone, including the Lions' pass game last week. They do really shut down TEs, however, so the Bills could lean on Moore and James Cook III a bit more than others have. Allen is also better equipped to deal with the pressure they bring than other statue quarterbacks. It's still a bad matchup, one worth knocking Moore down some, but I'm less sure Moore will be the one specifically hurt most by this.

21. Zay Flowers (vs. JAX) - 48.5 proj. receiving yards

Over the past two years, Flowers has averaged 11.6 half PPR points without Lamar Jackson. That does not include last week's goose egg, nor does it adjust for Tyler Huntley (turf toe) out for the season. Over the past month with backups, Flowers is the WR22 on WR23 usage and that's probably closer to his ceiling than his baseline with Joe Fagnano under center on a short week.

Fagnano is a big statue QB who made more plays than I thought he did on tape. There was a ton of bad luck involved on his throws to Flowers, who had a drop on a potential YAC opportunity over the middle. Another pass to Flowers was knocked down by an EDGE and another third-down rep by Flowers resulted in him falling down instead of being a first-read target.

The challenge with the matchup is the Jaguars have been very good at stopping the short pass, limiting production on throws to WRs under 10 air yards to a No. 29 ranking. Some of that is having Travis Hunter on these quick in-breakers, and that size matchup wouldn't make me feel great about Flowers here. Where they've been weaker is on deep throws, and we'll see if Fagnano can push the ball against these dynamic edge rushers. It's certainly asking for a lot. The Ravens have a sub-15.0 team total here. Flowers is a desperation WR2/3.

22. Stefon Diggs (vs. LAR) - 41.5 proj. receiving yards

In three games without Terry McLaurin (IR), Diggs is the WR30 on WR24 usage. His role isn't going to change in terms of snaps or alignment, but the Commanders may pass more without McLaurin helping to establish a lead and first-read targets are easier to justify to Diggs without their top target.

On tape, Diggs is playing this crafty slot/underneath role in ways as Travis Kelce plays it. He'll find soft spots in zone and out of structure. He's a clear red-zone threat with space getting tight. He's also not creating a ton of separation at the top of his routes in man coverage. That's okay. Diggs had 9.8 expected half PPR points (WR24) last week in a decisive win. He's a fantasy WR3 right now, while Antonio Williams is starting to break out in a full-time role.

The Rams rank 26th in half PPR points allowed to WRs at 22.4 per game, and teams routinely tilt away from passing against them, as their 47% neutral pass rate allowed ranks 30th (third-lowest). Diggs operates primarily in the short and intermediate areas, with 70% of his targets coming under 10 air yards and 57% between five and 20 yards, while the Rams sit 14th against intermediate WRs and 24th against short WRs. Washington enters as 5.5-point home underdogs with an implied team total of 21 points (23rd).

23. Tetairoa McMillan (vs. DEN) - 62.5 proj. receiving yards

The on/off splits with Jalen Coker from 2025 on are pretty glaring.

Beyond that, Bryce Young is particularly sensitive to his schedule and OL and both are getting worse moving forward. Since Coker returned in Week 6, McMillan is the WR38 on WR17 usage. He'll play better than he has over that stretch, but gone are the days of top-15 status.

Carolina brings an extreme passing approach, ranking third in neutral pass rate at 62% across 203 neutral plays. They will test a Broncos defense that has been stingy against wideouts, ranking 28th in half PPR points allowed to WRs (22.1 per game). Denver has yielded 21.5 or fewer points to the position in three of their last four games, with only Seattle cracking them for 38.0 points in Week 6. The Panthers enter as 3-point home underdogs with an implied team total of 20.25 points (25th).

24. Emeka Egbuka (at CHI) - 52.5 proj. receiving yards

25. Jalen Coker (vs. DEN) - 53.5 proj. receiving yards

26. Jaylen Waddle (at CAR) - 47.5 proj. receiving yards

27. Tre Tucker (at SF) - 44.5 proj. receiving yards

28. Adonai Mitchell (at KC) - 44.5 proj. receiving yards

Update: Garrett Wilson (concussion) hasn't practiced. That's a huge deal.

Geno Smith and Mitchell were absolutely balling in Week 8. There were three incredible timing, deep out routes that were on the money in addition to his red-zone fade ball TD and his multiple post targets. One of those posts was caught, while a 46-yard one really should've been caught by Mitchell. It was the best Geno Smith game in a very long time. That is a good sign.

Here are target shares in Weeks 1, 7 and 8 when all of the top-3 WRs were active: Wilson (29%), Mitchell (16%) and Omar Cooper Jr. (6%). I can guarantee the first-rounder's target share won't stay that low, so we're looking at a real consolidation up top. Mitchell would do well to hold onto this WR31 on WR34 per-game usage start to his season, but it's clear that his downfield role will lead to big games.

The Chiefs present a difficult draw, ranking 31st in half PPR points per game allowed to wideouts. Kansas City is stingy across all depths, sitting 27th against deep targets where Mitchell has logged a 44% target share with a 17.6-yard average depth of target. The Jets enter as 9.5-point road underdogs with an implied team total of 18.5 points (26th).

29. Omar Cooper Jr. (at KC) - 35.5 proj. receiving yards

Update: Garrett Wilson (concussion) hasn't practiced. That's a huge deal.

Cooper has a 13% target share and WR60 usage since returning in Week 6. That's the reality when there are this many quality skill guys on one team. Cooper is capable of random spike weeks because of his speed, but there is literally no floor. On tape last week, Cooper ran a lot of vertical routes out of the slot. He did not look bad at all, nor did he face that much man coverage. The ball was just out earlier to their outside targets. There were some crossing routes that could've been explosives had he been targeted, but Geno Smith was balling on sideline throws and posts. It is what it is. I'd brace for outside the top-50 finishes more times than not.

Week 9 presents a stiff test against Kansas City, which ranks 31st in half PPR points allowed to wide receivers. The Chiefs have surrendered very little through the air and allow offenses to pass against them at just a 47% neutral clip (29th). Opposing receiver rooms have regularly been bottled up, with only Jaxon Smith-Njigba's Week 7 outing clearing 60 yards against them across their past four games. The Jets head to Arrowhead as 9.5-point road underdogs with an implied total of 18.5 points (26th).

Lemon's usage without DeVonta Smith (and Dontayvion Wicks) is obviously way up. He's been the WR17 and WR15 in usage, respectively, with Tanner McKee over the past two games and had a garbage-time-filled nine targets on Sunday Night Football. There is no ceiling to chase in this offense with his forgettable long speed and short-area role, but if Smith is out, then Lemon can role-play as a fantasy WR3.

On tape last week, Lemon was used in ways that play to his skill set. His 20-yard catch was a deeper zone out route (his best work at USC). He also sat in zone coverage near the sticks countless times, breaking a LB's tackle once and making a shoestring catch on another play. Unfortunately, he didn't get his eyes around in time for a ball to the face and then couldn't come down with an outside go ball. His catch radius and forgettable speed are not those of a No. 1 WR.

32. Denzel Boston (at NO) - 43.5 proj. receiving yards

The swings from Boston have been crazy, but they're to be expected in a crowded offense with low-end QB play. It'll be Shedeur Sanders this time around, and that is likely a downgrade. To Sanders' credit, he is willing to throw it downfield and he might be a better stylistic fit for Boston than others in this offense. I tried to nail the trends in the pecking order this week:

The Saints haven't landed on CBs and Ss they fully trust, and they've been hit by big-bodied deep threats throughout the year, including Brock Bowers if we count that. In total, the Saints are allowing 250 passing YPG (ninth) while also being run on. New Orleans should be nearing full strength again on offense, so Boston's second-half numbers could come in handy here as a boom-bust WR3/4.

33. Rome Odunze (vs. TB) - 50.5 proj. receiving yards

Odunze is WR3 in catches of 15+ yards (19). He's also the only WR with 11+ explosive catches to not have a TD on them. That'll change soon. Odunze is the WR46 on WR51 usage since Williams has returned, and the signs are there for a bigger end of the season as a boom-bust WR3.

The matchup will be a battle. The Buccaneers have been great at limiting downfield shots against them, and they have size at CB1 with Zyon McCollum. In total, Tampa is allowing the 27th-most points to the position per game, recently shutting down the Panthers WR duo and Drake London. George Pickens did hit them for a WR5 finish in Week 5, but they've tightened things up since then. The saving grace is the Bears still project for 26.75 points, and the Bucs are allowing the second-fewest rushing yards per game.

35. Caleb Douglas (vs. DET) - 39.5 proj. receiving yards

Douglas is the WR30 on WR66 usage this year, if we include his Week 2 injury game. Since returning, he's been the WR8 on a multi-score game and the WR45 in a difficult spot against Christian Gonzalez. He certainly has the size/speed of a WR3/4 with some upside if we can find the right matchups for him ...

The Lions have been one of the most generous matchups for wideouts, allowing the fifth-most half PPR points per game to the position (30.5). Teams routinely attack Detroit through the air, with opponents generating a 59% neutral pass rate against them (third highest in the league). We'll see if DJ Reed can suit up, but if he can't, then it's a big deal for Douglas' downfield role. The Dolphins are 7-point home underdogs with a 21.75-point implied total (18th).

36. Malik Washington (vs. DET) - 41.5 proj. receiving yards

37. Ladd McConkey (vs. HOU) - 48.5 proj. receiving yards

McConkey survived in Week 8 because of a fluky 54-yard Hail Mary reception going into halftime that, of course, did not actually score a touchdown. Aside from that, McConkey turned 35 other routes into just four targets (11%), and 3-of-4 were screen variants behind the line of scrimmage. He dropped one of them. His other target wasn't completely out of structure, but it was a chore to complete the ball to him and he slipped after the catch. McConkey hasn't looked like the lightning-quick receiver he was as a rookie so far, and the Chargers were once again the worst offense in success rate (28%) in Week 8 off the bye. McConkey has been the WR39, WR53 and WR9 in expected half PPR points in his three healthiest games. There's a volatile WR3 case for him after 92% routes last week.

#2950. Prediction market traders say an Anthropic IPO is near-certain this year after company filed with SEC

Anthropic on Monday unveiled its Form S-1 filing, taking the company one step closer to completing its long-awaited move to the public markets, which is now seen as almost guaranteed to come this year.

Speculators on prediction market platform Kalshi place 95% odds that the artificial intelligence giant officially announces an initial public offering by the end of 2026.

Kalshi defines officially announcing an IPO as either the Securities and Exchange Commission declaring an S-1 effective, a priced IPO or an exchange assigning a ticker to a stock.

The Anthropic S-1 envisions a Nasdaq Stock Market ticker symbol of "ANT."

Anthropic is aiming to start trading the week of Nov. 16, according to a report in the Wall Street Journal. Traders on Kalshi place just a 14% chance that the company begins trading before then.

On the Polymarket platform, traders give a 69% chance that Anthropic will list on a public exchange and open for trading by Nov. 20.

#2949. The Unsung Performance of Baylor Scheierman

The Boston Celtics avoided another potential collapse of a 20-point lead last night, regaining their composure down the stretch to secure a 109–102 victory over the Milwaukee Bucks

Paul George and Derrick White spearheaded the effort, particularly in the closing minutes, while Neemias Queta also proved instrumental in the game's decisive moments. However, one player whose contributions I felt were particularly underrated throughout the contest was Baylor Scheierman.

Scheierman finished with nine points and 11 rebounds, including three on the offensive glass, while adding a steal and an assist in 28 minutes of action. Over his last three games, he has averaged 10 points and six rebounds while shooting an impressive 61% from beyond the arc.

Scheierman's impact also extended well beyond the box score. Here is a closer look at the various ways he influenced last night's game.

His impact began on the defensive end, where I thought he was particularly effective both on and off the ball. On this possession, he initially denies Herro an opportunity to receive the ball before rotating over to help on the drive, using an active swipe to force the pickup. When the ball eventually finds its way back to Herro, Scheierman stays disciplined, applies solid on-ball pressure, and closes out with a strong contest.

Here, Scheierman is matched up with Ryan Rollins, who prepares to use a wide screen from his big man, Kel'el Ware. Although Scheierman gets caught momentarily, he makes a smart adjustment by going under the screen, meeting Rollins at the other side, and recovering to get back in front. He then does a nice job staying attached through the subsequent on-ball screen, with some help from Luka Garza's hedge.

Once again, Scheierman showcases his off-ball activity by rotating over to help on another driving opponent. This time, however, we get a look at his instincts in the passing lanes. At the last moment, he reads the passer's intentions and peels back to his assignment to get a hand on the ball and deflect it.

Scheierman entered this game, averaging 4.6 deflections per 100 possessions, placing him in the 86th percentile league wide. His activity in this area makes him a valuable piece of a Celtics defense that ranks second in the NBA in deflections per game.

Garza ultimately gets the block on this next possession, but Scheierman made it possible. As the Bucks drive along the baseline, Mitchell Robinson, who is initially guarding Ware in the paint, is forced to rotate over and help on the ball. With Robinson leaving Ware, the big is momentarily left without a defender underneath the basket.

Scheierman recognizes this immediately and quickly sinks down toward Ware, reaching in an attempt to disrupt the pass. Although he doesn't come away with the steal, his presence forces Ware to adjust before going up for the layup. That split-second hesitation gives his teammates enough time to recover and get into position to contest the shot, ultimately setting up Garza's block.

Scheierman was credited with just one assist in this game, but his playmaking helped generate several quality looks for Boston. He finished with five potential assists on the night, and his ability to grab defensive rebounds and immediately push the pace (rip and run) continues to be an asset to the Celtics' early offense.

Scheierman attacks off the dribble and creates an opportunity for Robinson at the rim before quickly getting back on defense. He then does a nice job stepping in to cut off a driving lane and deter a potential three-point attempt, showcasing his ability to impact both ends of the floor in quick succession.

Staying on the offensive side of things, all nine of Scheierman's points came from downtown. He knocked down a three at the end of the half before quickly getting back on defense to contest a shot. Later, he connected on two big back-to-back threes that helped keep Milwaukee at bay.

On Rollins again here and he defends this "Iverson" cut perfectly. An Iverson cut is when a player uses two horizontal off ball screens to cut from one wing to the other.

He navigated both screens well and meets Rollins on the opposite wing, giving him no room to instantly drive. He then plays on the ball well and gets help from George who steals the pass and scores for the and one.

Anticipation and a quick reaction to the ball are obviously important traits for anyone looking to excel in the passing lanes defensively, but Scheierman has also shown an ability to incorporate deception into his approach. I first noticed this during the preseason and wanted to see another example before making the observation with confidence. This play provides exactly that.

Boston sends a double-team at Rollins near half court, forcing Scheierman into a Cover 2 situation. Watch how he deliberately leans his body toward the corner, giving the illusion that Ware is available for an easy pass in the paint. Rollins takes the bait, and Scheierman quickly changes direction to jump the passing lane and come away with the steal.

Another example of his off-ball prowess here getting another deflection.

Scheierman added several more quality possessions to the tape, giving the coaching staff plenty to be encouraged by.

Scheierman continued to make his presence felt in some of the game's biggest moments. His relentless pursuit of the basketball consistently puts him in position to make important hustle plays, and he has the skill and awareness to make those opportunities count.

He finds his way to the ball and gets his only true assist of the game to White for a huge and one then crashes for an offensive rebound that helped Boston wind down more time on the clock.

Lastly, we come back to the off-ball defense. On a play where the Bucks must get something, he communicates and performs three perfect switches before the ball is even entered.

Baylor Scheierman's impact in this game was a great example of how much value he can provide beyond the traditional box score. Whether it was his activity in the passing lanes, defensive positioning, ability to push the pace in transition, or timely shooting, he consistently found ways to positively impact the game.

Nine points and 11 rebounds are a solid contribution on their own, but his overall impact went well beyond those numbers. These are the types of winning plays that earn a coaching staff's trust, and Scheierman continues to make a strong case for a meaningful role in Boston's rotation.

#2947. Chinese AI lab Moonshot turned to Europe for Nvidia Blackwell access amid U.S. China curbs, sources tell CNBC

Moonshot AI, a Chinese artificial intelligence lab that the White House accused of accessing Nvidia advanced chips remotely in East Asia, has sought access to the chips through data centers in Europe, sources have told CNBC.

Deals have been discussed this year for Moonshot to rent capacity from neoclouds hosting Nvidia graphics processing units, or GPUs, at facilities in Europe, four people familiar with the matter said. They asked not to be named because they were discussing private information.

Nvidia's effective monopoly over the most powerful chips has made U.S. export controls a key tool in Washington's effort to preserve its advantage over China in the AI race. American AI labs such as OpenAI and Anthropic are locked in a training and pricing battle with Chinese developers, including Moonshot AI, which makes the Kimi family of models.

Moonshot was looking to lease capacity powered by Nvidia's Blackwell systems, advanced chips currently subject to those export controls which effectively ban their import into China, three of the sources told CNBC.

In July, the White House said the Chinese AI lab had accessed Nvidia's Blackwell chips via a facility in Thailand.

U.S. export controls restrict the shipment of Nvidia's most advanced chips to China, but remote access via overseas data centers offers a loophole. Current law does not give regulators broad authority to control companies' access to those chips through cloud services hosted overseas.

Moonshot, Nvidia, the White House, the U.S. Department of Commerce, China's embassy in the U.K. and its Ministry of Foreign Affairs did not respond to a request for comment.

Chinese developers have made notable capability gains in the past six months and greater access to Nvidia chips could put even more pressure on their American rivals.

To lease Nvidia computing power in Europe, a Chinese AI company would typically rent capacity from a non-Chinese neocloud or data center operator that can purchase advanced chips.

Moonshot was interested in leasing capacity on clusters for inference, which refers to serving models to end users, in Europe, two of the sources said. One of the sources said they were also aware of interest in clusters for training.

Talks for arrangements that would see Moonshot rent access to clusters of 8,000 or more Blackwell GPUs hosted in the region have taken place, one source said. They described the Chinese AI lab as "very aggressive" in its pursuit of more computing power.

One of the sources told CNBC that talks on securing Moonshot compute in Europe had taken place since the White House said it had done so via Thailand. Moonshot did not comment at the time.

Founded in 2023, Beijing-based Moonshot AI is one of China's leading model builders. It released a new model in July that Moonshot said closed the gap with leading systems built by Anthropic and OpenAI.

The model saw significant uptake as a cheaper alternative to U.S. offerings. Chinese AI models have surged in global adoption in 2026, amid capability gains and lower costs than U.S. rivals.

Around the time Kimi K3 was released, Moonshot said demand had stretched its capacity, and it temporarily paused new subscriptions to prioritize compute for current members. "We're adding capacity as fast as we can and will reopen new subscription spots in batches," the company said in a LinkedIn post.

Leasing AI compute is a booming industry, with customers, or offtakers, typically taking capacity for a fee and paying an upfront cost to secure the deal, which is a percentage of the lifetime contract value.

One source said it had been proposed that Moonshot pay 30% to 50% more upfront than a non-Chinese offtaker would.

Arrangements with Chinese customers can be more lucrative upfront, as neoclouds and financing firms look to hedge against a potential future ban of remote access agreements.

In Washington, lawmakers have proposed the Remote Access Security Act (RASA) to expand U.S. export controls to include the cloud-based access of critical hardware and software. It passed the House of Representatives in January but has not passed the Senate.

The Financial Times reported on Tuesday that the chip giant told cloud providers selling access to their chips that they must sell at least 51% of services to non-Chinese customers.

Asked about the reports earlier this week, an Nvidia spokesperson said: "America cannot win the AI race unless America's AI infrastructure wins worldwide — if the world's clouds rely on foreign-made chips, the model and application layers will be foreign-built as well."

Nvidia said in August that Washington had licensed some sales of its H200 AI chips, an older generation, but that "such sales were restricted" by the Chinese government, which has sought to boost domestic chipmakers by limiting access to Nvidia chips.

#2943. What was really behind Wednesday's monster rally? Trump quickly making peace

The stock market skyrocketed on Wednesday on the heels of a decisive sweep for Democrats in the 2026 midterm elections. But that was not necessarily the only driver of the climb.

"President Trump's acknowledgement of the Democrats' victory in the midterms and his expressed openness to collaborating with the opposition have helped markets to rally on hopes that contestation of the results and more adverse polarization can be avoided," said Thomas Hempell, Generali Investments senior economist.

Trump told The New York Times and The Wall Street Journal overnight Tuesday that he accepted the results and would work with Democrats. Those headlines coincided with a rebound in stock market futures.

In the lead-up to this year's midterms, some on Wall Street grew increasingly concerned about the prospect of Trump contesting the election results. The Department of Justice's Alaska election lawsuit led to fears over delays with the outcome that would exacerbate the uneasiness in the market that's typically removed once the elections conclude.

CNBC's Fed Survey showed that among 30 fund managers, strategists and economists, 63% believe that the extended uncertainty around the balance of power created by legal challenges to the election results posed "some" market risk. That was a slight increase from 61% in September. Additionally, 14% believed it represented "significant" market risk, which was up from 7%.

Now, investors are breathing a sigh of relief over Trump's acceptance of the results, which should "act as a break on extreme policy outcomes and bring down risk premia," so long as the president's decision remains, said George Saravelos, head of FX Research at Deutsche Bank.

Trump has a history of rallying markets, as well as driving them lower, with his announcements. Jay Woods of Freedom Capital Markets, who noted that the rebound in stocks overnight was partially due to the president's "initial tone and reaction to the results," said the focus will shift to whether Trump's latest move will actually have staying power.

"I think Trump is going to try to do and say the right thing, but at two in the morning, he may change his mind," the chief market strategist said. "He is an X factor."

Woods added that he's "encouraged" by Wednesday's massive rally, but he noted that there are still headwinds that could plague the market over the coming weeks — such as concerns about inflationary pressures as a result of higher energy prices from the conflict in the Middle East.

"How Trump reacts to it will help us navigate those headwinds," he said.

Osaic's Philip Blancato said he believes that if Trump "continues to play nice" with Democrats, the market is going to have a "very strong" end to the year.

Stocks historically tend to rally after the midterms anyway, but this time around, some were worried the solid run-up in the S&P 500 this year was going to potentially hinder those gains. After Trump's reaction, Blancato said he doesn't think the S&P 500 hitting 8,000 by year-end is totally out of the question and that there's "a lot predicated on him, but not completely."

The broad market index gained more than 1% on Wednesday, with its closing price implying a roughly 4% gain to that level.

However, investors should be prepared for any future volatility if Trump's rhetoric does not remain the same, the chief market strategist said.

"The president's always going to be a wildcard," he said. "Let's not let one day make a year. This is an instant reaction to this thing. There's still plenty of things to be hashed out."

#2942. Derrick White flipped the switch when the Celtics needed him

BOSTON — After the Celtics beat the Bucks, Derrick White sat in the locker room with ice packs on both knees and ice wrapped around his left shoulder.

The 32-year-old Celtics guard was coming off a season-high 24 points, plus 7 assists and 4 rebounds. He scored 11 points in the fourth quarter and 7 of the Celtics' final 10 points, including an and-one runner that gave the Celtics a 5-point lead with just over two minutes to go.

And, none of the late-game heroics were a surprise to his teammates.

"D-White just has an unwavering confidence — he just has a way of just being even-keeled," said Paul George, who led the Celtics in scoring with 25 on the night. "Obviously, he's great down the stretch."

This season, White is averaging 12.8 points, 5.1 assists, and 3.3 rebounds per game. For the second straight year, his shooting has slipped; he's shooting 42.5% from the field and 31% from three.

Regardless, for the second consecutive home game, White got raucous MVP chants at the free-throw line from the TD Garden. Afterward, he chatted with Jayson Tatum in the locker room, who donned street clothes after his first rest game of the season.

The two shared a laugh as White got ready to take the podium after his season-best game. Payton Pritchard exited the game in the third quarter after suffering a right eye abrasian, leaving the Celtics without two of their primary scorers in the clutch-time game against the Bucks.

That meant White knew he had to make a concerted effort offensively down the stretch.

"Every game is a little different — different lineups, different matchups, and so just kind of go out there, read what the game tells you to do, and go out there and compete, try to get a win," White said. "Obviously with JT out and then P going out too, had to [have] a little bit more aggressive mindset, and maybe next game is a little different. So, you just kind of go out there and have fun playing basketball."

The Celtics led by as many as 22 points in the game, but saw that lead turn into a one-point deficit late in the fourth quarter after a monster third quarter by Tyler Herro and multiple stints of offensive woes.

As the momentum of the game turned against Boston, White remained calm.

"He's a great defender, shot-blocker, rebounder at the guard spot — but again, with him, with Payton, like those guys just having kind of an even-keeled mentality, never get too high, never get too low, never really rattle," George said. "And when you're on the floor, you feel that you got a guy besides — he's obviously been a champion. So, he's tested. He's battle-tested. But to have someone that never gets too high, it's a level where I think when you're on the floor, you feel that, and you know, anything you're into, will get out of."

#2940. Fantasy Football Rankings: Hayden Winks' QB/TE/DEF/K Blueprint for Week 9

The Blueprint is my weekly column and basically everything that I use to build out the most accurate rankings I can. There will be an RB column, a WR column and this combination column on the onesie positions. You will find film notes and endless charts. Maybe enough charts to stress test the Yahoo website. I kid.

It's impossible to get every ranking thoroughly early in the week, so be sure to keep checking in on these columns through Sunday morning. As always, my full rankings at every position are available.

Burrow has been dealing as the season-long QB2 (26.2 PPG), generating +0.23 EPA per dropback (QB5). He is lighting up box scores through the air, leading the league in passing yards per game (302) and passing touchdowns per game (2.9). Cincinnati is leaning all the way into his arm, operating with a league-high 66% neutral pass rate (first).

That aggressive approach meets an Atlanta defense allowing 16.3 half PPR points per game to quarterbacks (23rd), where recent signal-callers like Tyler Huntley (24.0 points) and Caleb Williams (16.5 points) have put up functional days. The Bengals head in as 3-point road favorites with an implied team total of 27.25 points (sixth).

Allen's 28.2 fantasy points per game is currently ahead of the all-time fantasy record at QB (Lamar Jackson, 2024). He's done so without DJ Moore for half of the season. Moore was used properly last week with unlimited run-away routes, and then Dalton Kincaid found space downfield in multi-TE sets. It's a great formula.

The Vikings have been by far the best fantasy QB defense, however, allowing just 10.4 fantasy points per game. That's insane. Truly. They haven't faced an elite dual-threat QB this season, though.

Allen will be blitzed at a career-high rate, most likely. He's the QB5 in EPA per dropback against the blitz (+0.07), but that is far lower than on non-blitzes (+0.21, QB1). When blitzed, Allen has a 3.4% scramble rate compared to a 9% scramble rate when not blitzed. It's possible the Bills run a bit more often because of the pressure, as opposing offenses to Minnesota only pass 45% of the time in neutral situations (31st).

Buffalo enters as 3.5-point road favorites carrying an implied team total of 25.0 points (11th). It would make sense for Allen's fantasy output to take a hit here.

We'll see if Mike Evans (Achilles) can return, but it doesn't sound like Deebo Samuel Sr. will be back.

Purdy has been surgical as fantasy's QB4 (21.7 PPG), leading all qualified quarterbacks with +0.31 EPA per dropback (QB1). Over his last four starts against quality opponents, his efficiency has cooled to +0.03 EPA per dropback (16th) while averaging 17.3 fantasy points per game. His Week 7 outing without his starting receivers was his worst game of the year (16.0).

He draws a dream matchup against a Raiders defense that's allowing the fourth-most half PPR points per game to QBs (21.8). Las Vegas sits at the top right of the intersection between success rate and explosive rate allowed on dropbacks alongside the worst defenses in the NFL. It's allowed four straight QBs to finish as top-12 QBs. The 49ers enter as 9.5-point home favorites with a slate-leading implied team total of 30.5 points (first).

The Goff outlier dud happened last week, finishing as the QB21 against the Vikings' chaotic defense. Despite that, Goff is the QB4 in EPA per dropback and pacing for 4,770 yards with 36 passing touchdowns. He's the QB6 in fantasy despite not having anything to show with his legs.

The Week 9 environment is pristine against a Miami defense allowing 20.8 half PPR points per game to QBs (sixth). Opposing quarterbacks have moved the ball against the Dolphins regularly, including recent outings from Joe Burrow (24.6 points), Geno Smith (23.5 points) and Drake Maye (22.4 points). Miami is struggling on play action and throws over the middle. That's the entire Lions offense. Detroit is listed as 7-point road favorites with a 28.75-point implied team total (third).

After a very slow start, Maye is the QB2 from Week 4 on, sandwiched between Joe Burrow and Josh Allen. This past month, his +0.39 EPA per dropback leads the position. He's been the QB14 in EPA per dropback throughout the season, but timely scrambles and plenty of volume have made up for the efficiency decline. We'll see if A.J. Brown and Hunter Henry can suit up later, but Maye made it work with backups against the Dolphins last week (QB7).

He now faces a Packers defense that has kept QBs in check with a chaotic defensive style, surrendering just 14.9 half PPR points per game to the position (27th). Green Bay recently held Bryce Young to 12.4 points, Jared Goff to 18.0 and Dak Prescott to 15.0. The Patriots head into the matchup as 3-point home favorites with an implied team total of 25.25 points (10th).

Williams is simply way more accurate this year. He's the QB2 in completion percentage over expected when adjusting for his throw depth. He's middle of the pack in sack rate (7.6%). And he's the QB4 in interception rate. Williams is seeing it extremely well, despite already facing the Vikings, Seahawks, Patriots and the feisty Falcons defenses. He's the QB3 in EPA per dropback, and he'll start spiking fantasy outings once he's pushed by the opponent.

The Bucs defense will be another tough defense to beat. They're fifth-best against fantasy QBs, and from Week 3 on, they've been the best defense in EPA per play, allowing just 15.8 points per game. They slowed down Dak Prescott in Week 5, then completely shut down Bryce Young and Michael Penix Jr. in the past two weeks. They have better pass-rushing depth and have enjoyed full breakouts from CBs Zyon McCollum and especially Jacob Parrish. Tampa Bay also wants to grind out the clock with a run game that is breaking my brain. Chicago returns home as 5.5-point home favorites sporting a 26.5-point implied team total (seventh).

It hasn't been the best Prescott season, and the Cowboys' run game has been so bad that they've lost some inexcusable games along the way. The good news is Prescott has healthy WR and OL units right now, and his defense looks as pathetic as usual. He's been the QB13 in fantasy points per game this year on just QB8 EPA per dropback. It's been much higher than that for long stretches, and I can see that coming back after a recent stretch of games against BAL, HOU, TB and GB. Prescott was the QB6 and QB11 in the last two games against softer opponents.

The Colts defense is one of the worst in the NFL, possibly the worst without DT DeForest Buckner. They were smoked by the Jaguars last week after firing their GM in late October. Dallas heads on the road as 6-point road favorites with a healthy implied team total of 28.5 points (fourth). There are truly no excuses here.

Jayden Daniels has been the QB15, QB3, QB7 and QB16 in his healthiest games. Since returning from an elbow dislocation, the Commanders have yet to call a designed run for him. He'll still scramble like a madman, but more of those are resulting in slides and running out of bounds early, too. This will hurt him on the margins, though he's still the QB1 in rushing yards per game (47.2).

The Rams present a fun matchup dynamic, ranking 21st in half PPR points allowed to opposing QBs (17.0 per game) but fourth in points allowed specifically to QB rushing. Opposing offenses facing Los Angeles also feature a 47% neutral pass rate, the third-lowest rate allowed in the NFL. Washington hosts this contest as 5.5-point home underdogs carrying a 21.0-point implied team total (23rd).

Mahomes was quite good last week considering the circumstances, finishing as the QB5 against a healthy Broncos defense. Across the year, Mahomes is the QB14 on QB12 EPA per dropback. He was slowed down by the Seahawks without Rashee Rice in Week 7, but the Chiefs have been one of the best offenses in the NFL when not facing a top-three defense this year. Their weaknesses at LT and WR are hidden best against mediocre opponents.

Insert the Jets, who may not have their top-two defensive linemen here. The Jets just allowed top-five fantasy outings from Kirk Cousins, Malik Willis and Drake Maye in their last three outings. It's obviously a great spot here. Kansas City enters as 9.5-point home favorites with a 28.75-point implied team total (third).

10. Trevor Lawrence (at BAL) - 18.8 proj. points

Lawrence is up to QB2 overall in EPA per dropback. He hasn't been pushed many times this year, so he's left fourth quarters without adding to his totals. He's only the QB16 in yards per game, leaving him with QB8 fantasy points per game. That's closer to his floor while everyone is healthy on offense. His ceiling is far higher when games are tighter.

That's the issue with the Thursday Night Game against third-string QB Joe Fagnano. The Ravens are 10-point dogs with a team total below 15 points. The Ravens defense is far more talented and far better coached than their current No. 15 ranking in fantasy points allowed to QBs, too. Nice field position and the occasional 1-on-1 loss downfield with Marlon Humphrey in coverage are enough to combat these minor risks for T-Law as a low-end QB1. The Jaguars are ninth in team total (24.75).

Love is playing the sport like nobody else. His absurd aggressiveness and arm talent are amazing to watch every week.

Love has turned into the QB9 on a per-game basis, with the second-most yards per game (281). His top three pass catchers are healthy, and they're starting to figure out the best starting OL and ways to hide them on the margins. He's scored multiple touchdowns in 7 of 8 starts. Even with Josh Jacobs in the fold, the Packers have maintained their pass-first identity. That's how it should be behind this OL.

His Week 9 draw is tougher on paper against a New England defense that ranks 22nd in half PPR points allowed to quarterbacks (16.6). The Packers head on the road as 3-point road underdogs with an implied team total of 22.25 points (17th). That's lower than normal.

The Dolphins are definitely better in November than in September. This is the youngest team in the NFL, and it's a new scheme with a new QB. The team is gelling, while also getting healthier at OL and WR. Willis has been fantasy's QB7 since Week 5 on QB14 EPA per dropback. Some of that is because of a Caleb Douglas sideline YAC TD, but him being in the lineup is helpful! Miami is still 30th in neutral pass rate (44%), but they'll be forced to pass here.

The Lions have allowed the most fantasy points to QBs, and opposing offenses have the third-highest neutral pass rate allowed. Their run defense is simply better than their secondary. Detroit will miss star LB Jack Campbell over the middle (and I really hope things progress for him, beyond football), but they did get star S Brian Branch back recently. The Lions have given up the seventh-most rushing QB points this year, partially because of garbage time scrambles. Those count for us! Willis will try to continue this top-12 QB streak, after Kyler Murray (QB10), Jordan Love (QB12), Jacoby Brissett (QB7) and Bryce Young (QB7) have done just that this month. Miami carries a 21.75-point implied team total (18th) as 7-point home underdogs.

Stafford doesn't look fully healthy right now. Some of his sideline and downfield attempts lack his usual zip, and his throwing motion is feeling a little more Philip Rivers-like. I wasn't surprised to see Monday's report that his elbow is sore. That's what his throwing motion looks like right now, and the offense being extremely quick-hitting is further evidence of just that. Over the past month, Stafford's average depth of target is at just 5.4 yards downfield. The good news is the Rams have remained top-five in neutral pass rate in the two games without Davante Adams. Stafford is a lower-ceiling QB2 right now. He is fantasy's QB16 this year on QB16 EPA per dropback.

He now faces a Washington defense allowing the third-most half PPR points per game to quarterbacks (22.0). Opposing passers have found plenty of success against the Commanders, who rank sixth in QB passing points allowed per game. Jameis Winston tagged them for 326 yards and three passing scores in Week 5, while Brock Purdy posted 17.4 fantasy points the following week with backups at WR. The Rams enter as 5.5-point road favorites with a 26.5-point implied team total (eighth).

Shough is off the bye and it's possible WR Jordyn Tyson makes his debut and LT Kelvin Banks (ankle) returns after a prolonged absence. Those would be major upgrades. Shough's been the QB7 in fantasy points per game but was exposed versus the Vikings and Steelers recently while dealing with a broken non-throwing hand. He should be largely over that now, and the added rushing element has made him a low-end QB1 at times in the fastest-paced offense:

Week 9 presents a much tighter test against Cleveland, as the Browns rank 26th in half PPR points allowed to opposing QBs (15.2 per game). They haven't been tested by above-average QBs this entire month, however, with Aaron Rodgers, Cam Ward, Tyler Huntley and Geno Smith in their recent history. The Browns play man coverage more than anyone else, but the Saints have the WRs to hang in there. New Orleans is listed as a 2.5-point home favorite with a 23-point team total (14th).

1. Brock Bowers (at SF) - 76.5 proj. yards

Bowers' 17.2 half PPR points per game are on flirting with the best of all time at the position. He's barely even a tight end in this Klint Kubiak offense, one that's experimenting at RB and OG because the ground game is so broken. He peaked just last week with a season-high 24.7 half PPR points on 21.0 expected points. That rarely happens. Same with his 40% target share.

I know the 49ers have LB Fred Warner roaming the middle, but the 10-point spread is going to lead to a hilarious amount of targets on the road here. This is a good matchup on game script alone.

2. George Kittle (vs. LV) - 68.5 proj. yards

We'll see if Christian McCaffrey (hamstring) or Mike Evans (Achilles) will play later in the week, but it doesn't sound like Deebo Samuel Sr. will be back. This past month with injuries at WR, Kittle has been the TE4 on TE5 usage, commanding a 29% share in each of the past two games with Evans in and out of the lineup.

San Francisco draws a Raiders defense giving up 14.8 half PPR points per game to tight ends, the fourth-most in the league. The 49ers are 9.5-point home favorites with an implied team total of 31.25 points (first). Smash spot, duh.

3. Trey McBride (at SEA) - 59.5 proj. yards

The Cardinals no longer have the following skill players: Marvin Harrison Jr., Kendrick Bourne, Jalen Brooks, Reggie Virgil, Jeremiyah Love and Bam Knight. McBride was already the TE1 overall in usage, but his rare volume is further insulated. Gardner Minshew is known for his garbage-time dropbacks.

Of course, it's never a fun time against the Seahawks defense. They've allowed the fewest passing yards per game (169.1) and the fourth-fewest points to TEs. Colston Loveland (65 yards), Travis Kelce (44) and George Kittle (24) all had fairly quiet outings against them this month. McBride has battled against them recently, too. He was the TE9 on TE2 usage in Week 2, while finishing as the TE1, TE15, TE7 and TE2 in his other games against Mike Macdonald. A 14.75-point team total is scary for his touchdown odds, even if he'll likely see 8+ targets again.

4. Colston Loveland (vs. TB) - 50.5 proj. yards

Since Week 6, when Caleb Williams returned, Loveland is the TE3 on TE4 usage, largely competing with George Kittle for that No. 3 spot behind the true elites of Brock Bowers and Trey McBride.

#2936. Grading the Mavericks: Zaccharie Risacher’s reimagined role has been a key piece of Dallas’ success

The Mavericks went 3-2 over the last week-and-a-half, and sit at 4-4 overall. They won three in a row against Miami (113-109), Oklahoma City (120-96), and Houston (116-111). They have lost their last two against Sacramento (115-100) and San Antonio (112-111). Cooper Flagg led the team in scoring with 24.8 points per game.

Despite the sour taste that Cooper Flagg's last two games have left in everyone's mouths, the Mavericks deserve a good grade. They beat two great teams in the Thunder and Rockets, and a Miami team with multiple former All-Stars. For three games in a row, they looked like a playoff team. Kyrie Irving and Cooper Flagg's pairing made sense, P.J. Washington has been excellent all year, and Daniel Gafford has given Dallas a lot of what he gave them two years ago. Even Walter Clayton Jr., the 18th overall pick just last year, has given them a bright spot in a dark guard room.

Unfortunately, they tested fate by resting Irving on Monday against the Kings, and the basketball gods punished them by giving Sacramento their first win of the season in Dallas. The bad vibes continued for another two quarters, where the Mavericks got embarrassed in the first half against the Spurs. These six periods dropped them from an A+ to an A-. Luckily, they came out with energy in the second half and made what was looking like a stomping into a really exciting one-point loss against the defending Western Conference champions.

As previously mentioned, it is important to note how bad Cooper Flagg has looked in the last two games. He is just 10-of-38 (26.3 percent) from the floor, has not made a three, and has looked overwhelmingly disinterested on offense. Something doesn't look quite right, and they're going to need him to figure it out to take advantage of the next week of games.

Dallas has a three-game home stand against Brooklyn, Utah, and Indiana coming up.

Risacher was the first overall selection by Atlanta just three drafts ago. By no fault of his own, he was scrutinized for underperforming a draft placement he shouldn't have been taken at. When he was traded to Dallas, those expectations dissipated. It seems like a weight has been lifted from his shoulders, because Risacher has been one of the most important pieces to winning the Mavericks have.

He is just one of five players with 10+ steals and 10+ blocks this year. He is a legitimately good rebounder and has made threes sporadically. His commitment to defense has been enlightening, and his signature performance came this past week when he held Shai Gilgeous-Alexander to just 21 points on 7-of-19 shooting in a 24-point win. If Dallas is going to be a playoff team, Risacher will be in the rotation.

Yes, we are just eight games into his career. Yes, he was dealing with shin splints before the season. And yes, he was never supposed to be a flashy lottery pick. But it is impossible to ignore the tape that Morez Johnson Jr. has put together. Especially when Yaxel Lendeborg, his Michigan teammate, was picked after him and is averaging 21.5 points a game in Golden State.

Johnson Jr. was finally taken off a minutes restriction against the Spurs on Wednesday, and looked significantly more mobile. But for the majority of his minutes he has been a step slow, fouled a ton, and has given the Mavericks little to nothing on the offensive end. He is scoring just over seven points a game, grabbing just 3.1 rebounds, and fouling three times in his 16 minutes a night. Hopefully he can stack minutes as he ramps up, but he has been disappointing thus far.

It's taken a little bit of time, but Irving is starting to look like himself again. The paint touches are not quite there yet; it takes a lot of time to trust your legs to jump and move in traffic again. But the three-point shot looks good, he is moving well laterally, and he is digging in on rebounds and the defensive end. He's coming off back-to-back games of 35 and 29 points, and although the latter was not super efficient, he is starting to score in bunches like he did pre-injury. It is only a matter of time where he is doing things again that leave us in awe.
