#2933. Milwaukee Bucks NBA 2K27 simulation: Part 2

There were some wild outcomes in our first NBA 2K simulation, an AI-controlled affair that offered a glimpse of what could happen for the Milwaukee Bucks this season. Some outcomes were fun to see, some were not, and some were… tragically ironic (sorry, Scoot). This time, let's take a look at a more realistic simulation of how the Bucks' season might turn out. I'll be setting the lineups and player minutes—and trying to pull off some roster moves that I think would be realistic and beneficial for the Bucks.

The season begins with the Bucks clearing their roster issues: waiving Kevin Porter Jr., Johni Broome, Jake Stephens, and Pete Nance before welcoming back Nance on a two-way contract. That leaves their starting lineup looking exactly as it did for Taylor Jenkins and the real Bucks (save for the reversal of the forward positions):

Amid early-season play, the Bucks are twice ranked 21st in the power rankings and once 22nd. The way things are going in real life, I'm sure Bucks fans wouldn't be too mad to see this.

Roster-wise, the Bucks have Myles Turner, Caris LeVert, Gary Trent Jr., and Kyle Kuzma all on the trading block. On the flipside, Kasparas Jakučionis, Brayden Burries, and Nate Ament—a young core trio that Bucks will be looking to hold and develop over the next few years—are set as their untouchables.

In December, Milwaukee sits 7-24 on the season. One of the "leading" factors in this has been Myles Turner, who has been horrible, scoring just 7.9 PPG on 33% from the field (Editor's note: So, pretty true to life?). On a better note, Kel'el Ware looks great and has been averaging a double-double off the bench. As such, he will move into the starting position in a bid to improve the team's 27th-ranked rebounding.

TRADE ALERT: The Milwaukee Bucks are sending AJ Green and Jericho Sims to the Denver Nuggets for Marvin Bagley III, Zeke Nnaji and a 2028 second-round pick.

The Nuggets, short on options at the two-guard position, make the trade for Dairy Bird with the intention of improving their second-to-last in the league ranking in three-point attempts. Meanwhile, the Bucks acquire Bagley, who is having a strong season, averaging 10.6 PPG and 8.8 RPG on 56% shooting from the field, to bolster the big rotation. In Naji, they also add a theoretical scoring boost at power forward, a skill Jericho Sims has been unable to provide. More importantly, Milwaukee adds to its draft chest.

The Bucks roll into the All-Star Break with an 18-41 record, showing improvement after their slow start to the season. Despite starting to turn his season around, Turner remains a prominent name on the trade block; unfortunately, nothing comes together for the Bucks, even with Kristaps Porzingis, Miles Bridges, and Rui Hachimura all being floated for him. It has, however, been made clear that Turner's name will still be in trade talks come the offseason.

On a more positive note, Jakučionis makes the World Team for the Rising Star Game, averaging 10.8 PPG, 3.1 RPG, and 3.2 APG. He doesn't lead the Bucks in any category, though. Those honours go to:

As a team, the Bucks are the 28th-ranked offence in the league, with 109.6 PPG. Their defence is better, though, ranking 19th in the league (giving up 121.4 PPG). It's not quite the "Bad Boy Bucks" that fans have been hoping for, but definitely a step in the right direction in the Bucks' quest to become a defensive-minded team. As for power rankings, Taylor Jenkins' team hovers between 26th and 27th, a regression from the start of the season despite their improved play. Looking at the standings, Milwaukee sits 13th in the Eastern Conference and 25th overall.

Down the stretch, the Bucks improve slightly, finishing as the 27th-ranked offence with 110.6 PPG. Their defence fares better, ranking 19th overall, despite giving up 121.2 PPG. As far as the standings go, Milwaukee finishes 26-56 and in the 13th position in the Eastern Conference—a very realistic outcome and probably a spot where Bucks fans wouldn't be too mad to finish. On a positive note, Brayden Burries makes the All-Rookie Second Team, averaging 6.9 PPG, 1.3 RPG, and 0.8 APG. It's a pleasing accomplishment for Burries, even if the numbers are underwhelming. And on that note, the Bucks statistical leaders end up as:

On the night of the NBA draft, the Bucks are looking to make a trade and still Turner's name is ringing hot in front offices around the league. Yes, even after another down season, teams are looking to take a swing on him.

The phone rings. It's the Portland Trail Blazers…

BREAKING NEWS: The Portland Trail Blazers are trading the 20th overall pick in the 2027 NBA Draft to the Milwaukee Bucks for Myles Turner and the 45th pick in the draft.

The Trail Blazers make the move believing they are one piece away from being a top-3 contender in the Western Conference, and feel strongly that the twin towers of Myles Turner and Donovan Clingan are going to be the key to getting past the dominant bigs of the West, like Victor Wembanyama and Chet Holmgren.

With the pick, the Bucks select Babatunde Oladotun, a 6'10", lengthy, high-upside forward out of Maryland. But the surprises don't end there. In one of the biggest draft-day falls, the Bucks pick up 7'3" center Luigi Suigo out of Villanova. Suigo was projected to be a lottery pick earlier in the year, but it seems questions regarding his cardio and frame began to swirl around the league, enabling the Bucks to take a swing on the big man with the 35th pick.

Following the draft, the Bucks exercise the team options on both Ware and Jakučionis to kick off their free agency period. They then re-sign Jaquez, Herro, and Rollins to new contracts. They also add Jalen Smith and Santi Aldama on cheap deals to add some more size to their lineup following the Turner trade.

Along with the additions, the Bucks say goodbye to fan favourite Kuzma, veteran Caris LeVert, and two-way players Cormac Ryan and Kam Jones. Heading into the 2027-2028 season, the starting lineup thus looks like this:

Overall, this certainly isn't as blockbuster as the AI-controlled simulation, but I feel like it is a better look at a realistic outcome: retaining the Bucks' core and adding some key pieces to raise the team's floor.

So, did this 2K simulation tickle your fancy, or was it a fever dream a little too close to reality? Be sure to share your thoughts in the comments.

#2932. Western Christian claims program's 20th IGHSAU volleyball state title

CORALVILLE − It may feel like Groundhog Day for some Iowa high school volleyball fans, as Western Christian has claimed another state title.

The No. 1-seeded Wolfpack swept No. 6 Cherokee Washington in three sets (25-20, 25-12 and 26-24) on Thursday. Nov. 5. That's Western Christian's 20th state title, tying it with Wahlert Catholic for the most Iowa state volleyball titles. They are one of four programs to have double-digit state titles alongside Wahlert Catholic, Dike-New Hartford (16) and Tripoli (11).

Morgan Kooiman, Maddyn De Jager, Josie Hall and Hadley Dekkers led the charge for the Wolfpack on Thursday. Kooiman led the team with 17 kills, while DeJager (14) and Hall (9) combined for 23 more. Dekkers set up the trio well with 38 total assists.

Western Christian and Cherokee traded blows in the first set. Cherokee led by as much as four early on, but the Wolfpack rallied to win 25-20. Kooiman had seven kills in the set, including the final two to close on a 10-4 run. Kooiman, Hall and De Jager combined for 14 of Western Christian's 15 kills in the frame. The Wolfpack started the second set with all the momentum and took an 11-1 lead following a handful of attack errors from Cherokee Washington. Western Christian never looked back on its way to a 25-12 set victory.

Cherokee Washington refused to go down without a fight, leading by as many as four points, 19-15, in the third set. It was inches away from claiming a set for itself at 24-23 to keep its championship hopes alive. However, Hall recorded a kill, followed by back-to-back kills from Kooiman to secure a 26-24 set win and Western Christian's 20th state title.

More: How Tammi Veerbeek become the winningest HS volleyball coach in Iowa

Western Christian prepared for tight moments like that all year, situations like being tied late and needing to score, or trailing in a set and needing to rally. Because of that, it was ready for a battle to the wire even in the high-pressure state finals.

"I just felt this group had a calmness, just a, 'We can do this together, we're not going to get rattled, (attitude)'" said Western Christian coach Tammi Verbeek. "They proved it."

Cherokee Washington's stellar year wraps up at 32-9, making a deep run despite their No. 6 seed. Deidra Doeden (12), Ellana Mortenson (8) and Addy Wolfswinkel (6) combined for 26 kills in their state finals effort. This was the school's third-ever state tournament appearance.

The Wolfpack end their season at 41-5, as Verbeek continues to cement her legacy as one of the greatest Iowa high school coaches ever, with 15 state titles to her name.

"Every team is special," Verbeek said. "There's never one that you value more than another one because these girls worked hard for it. They wanted it just as bad as anyone."

#2931. Bomb threats disrupt flights across Europe for the second time. Here’s what we know

Flights across Europe were targeted by "hoax" bomb threats on Thursday for the second time this week, disrupting air travel across London, Dublin, and Helsinki, among others.

At least 27 different flights were impacted, according to Reuters, with some forced to land unexpectedly and others escorted by fighter jets.

Aer Lingus, Finnair, Wizz Air and Polish airline LOT confirmed to CNBC that some of their flights were affected.

It comes after seven passenger planes across Europe, two departing from the U.K., were targeted by suspected bomb threats on Tuesday and were described as "malicious hoax threats" by the U.K. government.

Finnair said two of its flights — one from Helsinki to Rome with 144 passengers and 7 crew members and another from Helsinki to Vienna with 177 passengers and six crew members — received "unfounded or hoax" bomb threats on Thursday, with both flights landing safely and customers disembarking the aircraft.

Local authorities in Rome and Vienna inspected the two flights and didn't find anything out of the ordinary.

"Unfortunately, air traffic is sometimes subject to threats, as we have seen this week, when other airlines in Europe have also faced hoax bomb threats," Finnair said in a statement. "Threats are almost always unfounded."

Hungarian budget carrier Wizz Air confirmed to CNBC that its flight from Milan's Malpensa Airport to Bucharest's Otopeni Airport, as well as another flight to Warsaw, were subject to bomb threats on Thursday.

The flight to Bucharest was approaching its final destination and landed safely when the threat was reported at Otopeni, with security checks subsequently clearing the aircraft. Wizz Air's Warsaw flight was diverted.

"This incident comes amid a series of similar bomb threats affecting several airlines across Europe in recent days," Wizz Air told CNBC. "Wizz Air is not the only airline targeted, with other carriers also experiencing threats resulting in flight diversions and security checks."

Irish airline Aer Lingus diverted its Dublin to Rome flight to Normandy on Thursday morning, while Polish airline LOT said two of its flights from Warsaw received security threats. Both were cleared following security inspections, the airlines told CNBC.

Air France's CEO Ben Smith said none of its flights were affected, but the coordinated threats are unprecedented. "It's unfortunate, and we hope it won't give consumers any reason to slow down or reduce their travel plans."

U.S. Transport Secretary Sean Duffy told CNBC's "Squawk Box" on Thursday that the incidents appeared to be "false attacks."

He added: "We take safety very seriously, which is why, if you see any issue in the airspace or threats in the airspace, we shut it down...and so, with all of our assets in the U.S. government and our partners over in Europe, we are tracking this together and working together to make sure the American airspace, the American traveler, is safe."

European Union Commissioner for Sustainable Transport and Tourism, Apostolos Tzitzikostas cautioned it is "too early to draw conclusions on whether the reported incidents are linked," in a post on X. "The EU-level information-sharing mechanism has been activated related to aviation security."

#2930. Rob Gronkowski All In on 49ers Making Potential Blockbuster Trade for Star WR

The San Francisco 49ers have already made one blockbuster trade ahead of the Nov. 10 deadline, acquiring defensive tackle DeForest Buckner last week.

The 49ers might not be done, as they continue to explore the wide receiver market while several injuries leave them short-handed at the position.

NFL legend and Fox Sports analyst Rob Gronkowski has one particular player in mind as a potential blockbuster addition for San Francisco.

Gronkowski appeared on the "Up & Adams Show With Kay Adams" this week, where Kay Adams asked him to say "Yay or Nay" on rumored trade pitches around the league.

One move Gronkowski loved was the 49ers acquiring New York Jets star WR Garrett Wilson. San Francisco would likely have to put a hefty offer on the table to convince the Jets to part with a player they've made clear they don't want to trade.

"Garrett Wilson… listen the Niners, they do need a wide receiver, all six of there top guys are out, the Jets they're kind of in a rebuilding process as well which is weird to say because they're a better team this year than over the last couple years but yeah it makes sense. Garrett Wilson to the Niners, I'm yay," Gronkowski said.

Despite the frustration Wilson has expressed, the Jets don't sound like they're entering the deadline as sellers, at least based on what they've said. They're 2-6 and face the Kansas City Chiefs this weekend, but they still seem intent on keeping their star receiver.

Last weekend, ESPN's Adam Schefter reported that a reliable source told him there's "no way" the Jets would be willing to trade Wilson.

That said, the Tennessee Titans said the same thing before trading Jeffrey Simmons to the Chicago Bears, who gave Tennessee two first-round picks for the star defensive tackle.

If the 49ers put an offer on the table that the Jets can't refuse, who's to say they couldn't lure Wilson out of New York by Tuesday's deadline? Adding a player of that caliber to their already talented offense would certainly put San Francisco among the top Super Bowl favorites.

#2929. Three tax proposals targeting the rich that the new Congress could revive

A version of this article appeared in CNBC's Inside Wealth newsletter, a weekly guide to the high-net-worth investor and consumer. Sign up to receive future editions, straight to your inbox.

Democrats' sweeping midterm victories have renewed momentum for taxing the wealthy on a national level. Sen. Ron Wyden, D-Ore., who is set to retake control of the Senate Committee on Finance, has already put billionaires on notice. Several proposals are already before the committee, including measures to close the carried-interest tax break and impose taxes on large trust assets.

"On taxes, the committee will redouble its efforts to ensure billionaires pay their fair share and hold tax cheats accountable. Democrats will spearhead a tax agenda that puts American families first and requires the ultra-rich and corporations to pay their fair share," Wyden said in a statement Wednesday morning.

Tax lawyers told CNBC that proposed wealth taxes and other tax hikes are unlikely to become law because the Democrats have not won enough seats to overcome a veto from President Donald Trump. However, the measures are worth monitoring as they shape the party's agenda heading into the next presidential election, they said.

"The proposals are not necessarily anything that will pass over the next two years. But if there is an administration change two years from now, they do give you an indication of what might be on the table during the next administration," said Justin Miller, partner and national director of wealth planning at Evercore Wealth Management.

Here are three tax proposals sitting with the Senate Committee on Finance that could gain traction after the blue tsunami.

In April, Wyden and Sen. Angus King, I-Maine, introduced a bill eliminating the preferential tax treatment of carried interest, a popular Wall Street tax break. Under current law, fund managers can have a share of a fund's profits taxed at the lower capital-gains rate if the investments are held for at least three years. The Ending the Carried Interest Loophole Act would tax investment profits as ordinary income and also require fund managers to recognize and pay tax on deemed compensation annually, rather than deferring taxes until the fund realizes income.

Lawmakers on both sides of the aisle have proposed changing the taxation of carried interest for nearly two decades, according to Holland & Knight partner Joshua Odintz, a former tax counsel at the Department of Treasury and the Senate Committee on Finance. President Trump repeatedly criticized the provision, saying during his 2016 presidential campaign that it was "unfair to American workers."

Odintz said he expects more scrutiny of carried interest as Congress faces pressure to shore up the Social Security trust fund, which is expected to run out of reserves by the end of 2032. However, he thinks it is more likely that the tax break will be eliminated as part of a budget reconciliation bill rather than a standalone bill.

"I think an issue like carried interest have to be addressed in the context of a larger tax bill, where it's challenging for members to say, 'No, I'm going to stand up just on this one issue and crater a bill,'" he said. "It's much easier when there are a series of trade-offs, and members can point to the different trade-offs and say, 'Look, I got this. I guess I had to give up that, but on net, this is better for my jurisdiction.''"

Fair Trusts for Fiscal Responsibility Act

Sen. Patty Murray, D-Wash., introduced a bill targeting trusts used to transfer assets while minimizing or avoiding estate and gift taxes. The Fair Trusts for Fiscal Responsibility Act sets a graduated annual tax on trust assets exceeding $50 million, with the rate rising to 3% for assets above $1 billion. It would also create a withholding-credit system: taxes paid each year would be tracked in an account and credited against estate or generation-skipping taxes when assets are later transferred or the trust ends. Any unused credit against estate taxes could be refunded.

"This is something that should be watched because if it gets traction, it would fundamentally change estate planning for ultra-high-net-worth individuals," said David Blum, partner and chair of Akerman's national tax practice group.

Miller said he is skeptical that the bill would pass given how complicated it would be to implement. He thinks it is more realistic that the U.S. would follow the examples of the United Kingdom and Canada and periodically tax assets held in trust every set number of years. In the U.K., an inheritance tax is levied every 10 years, and Canada imposes a 21-year rule on most personal and family trusts.

The Inside Wealth newsletter by Robert Frank is your weekly guide to high-net-worth investors and the industries that serve them.

Redistribution Of Billions by Instituting New High-income Obligations on Overlooked Debt (ROBINHOOD) Act of 2026

Several incoming Democratic senators, including James Talarico and Abdul El-Sayed, have pledged to curb the "buy, borrow, die" tax strategy practiced by billionaires Larry Ellison and Elon Musk. Wealthy founders and investors borrow against their stock to access cash without selling shares and triggering capital-gains taxes. If they hold the assets until death, heirs can also receive them with a stepped-up tax basis, leaving the accumulated gains untaxed.

The Senate's ROBINHOOD Act, introduced by Sen. Ruben Gallego, D-Ariz., in June, would make ultra-rich borrowers pay capital-gains taxes on selected long-term assets they borrow against. It would apply to individuals with at least $1 billion in assets or more than $100 million in annual income, and only when the assets' unrealized gains are at least as large as the loan.

A related House bill, introduced by Rep. Daniel Goldman, D-N.Y., would impose a 20% tax on certain loans backed by assets for borrowers with income over $400,000. The House bill exempts several types of loans, including mortgages and margin loans.

Miller said these bills are narrower and far easier to implement than the sweeping wealth tax proposals that dominate headlines. While these "buy, borrow, die" bills have gotten less attention, they have more realistic odds of passing, he said.

While tax policy gridlock is the likely outcome for the next two years, Odintz advised high-net-worth taxpayers to be proactive.

"I think concerned constituents should engage with Wyden and his staff if they see problems that were unintended based on the stated purpose of legislation. I think it's worth coming in now rather than later," he said. "If in 2029 there's a Democrat in the White House and Democrats control both houses, then we could see swift movement for a reconciliation bill that goes in the opposite direction of the OBBBA and TCJA."

#2928. What makes this 76ers team stacked with superstars so unique

What makes this 76ers team stacked with superstars so unique originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

They're a team of superstars that don't play superstar basketball.

That's the beauty of this 76ers team. They play a brand of unselfish team-first basketball you rarely see from a team loaded with stars.

LeBron James is one of the two or three greatest players in NBA history, a four-time MVP, a four-time NBA champion, a 1st-ballot Hall of Famer.

Joel Embiid is a seven-time all-star, a former MVP, an Olympic gold medalist and a likely Hall of Famer.

Jaylen Brown is a five-time all-star, an NBA champion, a career 20 points-per-game scorer.

Tyrese Maxey is a two-time all-star, one of only two NBA guards averaging 27.0 points per game over the last four years.

Four certified megastars on one team. And somehow they've figured out how to leave their egos at the front door and play smart, tough, disciplined team basketball, the style of ball you just never see on these superstar-laden teams.

On offense, they patiently work the ball around, always looking for the open man or a positive matchup. You've got this bunch of big-name, high-priced All-Stars who are just as happy to pass the ball as shoot it. That never happens.

The 76ers are sixth in the NBA in assists, and they're one of only four teams with twice as many assists as turnovers. Smart, unselfish, patient, disciplined.

When an all-timer like LeBron averages 7.3 assists per game that speaks volumes. LeBron is fifth in the NBA in assists per game among forwards, which is wild. You can see the joy on his face when one of his passes leads to a basket. When one of the greatest players in history has that mentality, it rubs off on everyone around him.

Embiid hasn't really got going yet on offense and still takes too many 3's, but he's also embraced the team-first mentality, and his 4.8 assists per game are more than one full assist above his career average and 4th-most in the NBA among centers or forward-centers. And still one of the most dominating defensive players in the league.

Brown is averaging 27.0 points, which you expect, but he's also averaging 4.3 assists per game, significantly above his career average of 3.8 coming into the season. He's fit in so well so fast you forget its his first season here.

And what can you even say about Maxey? Just a beast. He's averaging nearly 30 points per game but never wastes a possession, always makes the right decision with the ball in his hands and is still finding time to average 4.3 assists per game.

With James, Embiid, Brown and Maxey, the 76ers are the only NBA team with four guys averaging at least 16 points and 4 assists per game. Only the Pacers, Magic and Raptors have three.

But where we're really seeing this group playing out of their minds is on the defensive end, where this bunch of all-stars, including two newcomers, has been astounding.

The 76ers have the 2nd-best defensive efficiency rating in the league, behind only the Pistons, who they play Wednesday night, and they're allowing the 2nd-fewest points in the league, also behind Detroit. Last year, they allowed 123 points per game through eight games. This year they're at 111.

They're holding teams to 43 percent shooting from the field, 3rd-best in the league and their lowest since 2011-12. Their 9.3 steals per game are 6th-most.

All five starters are among the top 25 players in the league in defensive rating – James is fourth, Embiid 11th, Edgecombe 15th, Maxey 22nd and Brown 23rd. How is that even possible?

It's rare to see superstar-caliber guys play as hard on the defensive end as the offensive end. Everybody on this team embraces it. They hustle, they work, they communicate and most importantly they commit to it.

The biggest concern when the 76ers built this dream team was that it would be a team of individuals out for themselves, but the reality has been the opposite.

In a very short period of time, they've learned how to play together. How to play for each other.

And we haven't even mentioned V.J. Edgecomb, who at 21 years old has figured out how to assimilate into this loaded lineup, never force anything, wait for his opportunities and make huge shots when he gets his turn. Edgecombe is at 12.7 points and 3.0 assists per game, and his numbers are understandably down a bit from last year, but if that bothers him it sure doesn't show.

And the role players – Dean Wade, Anfernee Simons, Kentavious Caldwell-Pope, Jabari Walker – have each adopted the same ego-free style of basketball, supporting the starters however they need to.

Nick Nurse deserves a ton of credit for getting this bunch to play this brand of hoops. It's not easy to get any NBA player to put team goals ahead of individual goals. But he's got Hall of Famers and all-stars doing it and having a blast along the way. They all seem to love playing basketball the right way and they seem to love playing together.

The 76ers are 7-1 and looking like a real threat to win the East, and they're doing it by playing the game the right way.

By committing to each other and not themselves.

#2927. Thursday Night Football live updates, TV channel, stream, where to watch NFL game tonight

The Baltimore Ravens and Jacksonville Jaguars are heading in opposite directions as they prepare to square off on "Thursday Night Football" in Week 9.

But Baltimore's struggles have less to do with a lack of talent and more to do with a pair of injuries in their quarterback room. The biggest blow, of course, is the loss of two-time MVP Lamar Jackson, who has already missed three games this season and will now land on injured reserve (IR) after suffering a thumb injury that requires surgery.

Backup Tyler Huntley isn't riding to the rescue, either, after suffering a turf toe injury that will sideline him for the rest of the season.

That leaves undrafted rookie Joe Fagnano to make his first NFL start. The former Maine and UConn quarterback put together an impressive college career, throwing for 10,926 yards and 94 touchdowns. Still, Thursday night will be quite the introduction to life in the NFL.

At 4-4, the Ravens aren't out of contention by any stretch, and they acquired veteran quarterback Jacoby Brissett from the Arizona Cardinals via trade on Weds. Nov. 4, though the trade will not be finalized until after tonight's game. The 33-year-old will not play in today's game but he brings stability to the position and should quickly assimilate in Baltimore upon arrival.

The visiting Jaguars, meanwhile, look like one of the AFC's emerging contenders. Their defense has been downright stingy against the run, becoming the first team since the 1970 merger to hold every opposing rusher under 40 yards through its first five games. Jacksonville has also extended its streak of not allowing a 75-yard rusher to 23 consecutive games. That's a daunting assignment for Ravens running back Derrick Henry.

Meanwhile, Trevor Lawrence and head coach Liam Coen have the Jaguars' offense firing on all cylinders. Can Jacksonville make another statement in primetime, or will Fagnano and the Ravens stun everyone?

#2926. As AI becomes more fractured, one name stands out: AMD

As Anthropic rushes to takes its shares public, the AI trade is showing cracks, and they are not confined to fringe players.

We recently wrote about CoreWeave, which fell 15.5% on Oct. 14 as yields on its 2032 notes touched 15%, and its default-swap spreads nearly doubled in October. IREN is down more than 20% this year. Last Thursday, the contagion reached chipmakers, which makes sense since much of CRWV and IREN's spending is going there. AMD dropped 8.1% as Meta's financing plans and a supply warning from Lam Research exacerbated data-center fears about the pace of AI spending.

Because when you screen for factors that demonstrably generate outperformance over time, the list narrows quickly. My screen: price above the 200-day moving average; revenue growing at least as fast as the S&P 500 (5%+ per annum); EPS growing at least as fast (7%+); rating upgrades; a PEG below 3; and ROE and ROIC of at least 12%. Of the names reporting in the next two weeks, AMD is effectively the only one that makes the list. Yes, you can (and arguably should, as our staples article described) position your portfolio with slightly less tech exposure than the broad indices provide, but we can't ignore the market's biggest and most transformative sector entirely.

Especially because the growth in this case doesn't just meet my criteria; it blows past them. Consensus for Q3 calls for EPS of $1.94 on revenue of $13.06 billion, versus $1.20 and $9.25 billion a year ago. That's YoY growth of nearly 62% and 41%, respectively. The recent drawdown came from a sector selloff, not anything AMD reported, but it shows that if some names in the space get hit, even the larger players are likely to feel it. On Oct. 6, Lisa Su said AMD would substantially increase supply in 2027 because AI orders keep outrunning capacity.

So how do you balance factors we know work for a fast-growing company against a backdrop of cracks in the AI trade? Options.

Owning the stock outright means absorbing a roughly 9% to 11% implied earnings-related move (day of). Earnings are about a week out, and over the past four quarters the smallest move over a similar window was more than 9%, and the largest move, after the company reported its Q1 26 results, was 38%! Of course, that kind of volatility means options aren't cheap, and that rich premium is also a reason not to simply buy calls.

A call spread risk reversal does both jobs: it reduces risk if the cracks widen, and the short options offset the elevated volatility we would otherwise pay for. The bottom chart shows that, by sacrificing about $1,700 of upside for $5,000 in outperformance during a severe downside move, and given how jittery this trade has been lately, this is a way to stay in it with considerably less risk. $5,000 less, specifically. Of course, selling that out will tie up some cash, but less so than simply buying the stock. And selling both the out and the call reduces long call purchase by 66%

#2925. Las Vegas Raiders owner Mark Davis says he's 'not a fan' of sports gambling amid prediction market boom

Sign up today for the CNBC Sport Newsletter

Las Vegas Raiders and Aces owner Mark Davis may run his business in America's gambling capital, but he's not a fan of sports betting.

In an interview with CNBC Sports' Michael Ozanian, Davis expressed his concerns about the rapid expansion of sports gambling, particularly proposition bets, which are wagers on specific events during a game that aren't necessarily tied to the eventual outcome. Davis said that the growing sports gambling industry could threaten the integrity of pro sports.

"I'm not a fan per se of gambling on sports, especially the one-on-ones, the proposition types of betting," Davis said.

Davis said protecting the integrity of the game should remain the top priority for professional sports leagues, even as gambling partnerships generate significant revenue.

"I think the key thing is the integrity of the game," he said.

The comments come as professional sports leagues grapple with the rapid growth of prediction markets, which allow users to trade contracts tied to the outcomes of sporting events.

The NFL has taken a more cautious approach than some of the other pro sports leagues. While the league has embraced sports betting, it has declined to enter into official partnerships with prediction market operators amid concerns about regulation and the integrity of competition.

"We think that there needs to be stronger regulations into the predictive markets," NFL Commissioner Roger Goodell told CNBC Sport in September. "We don't feel like we have to be first in this. We feel like we're going to be right. And the best thing to do is to be patient and make sure that you keep the integrity of the game number 1."

The league filed an amicus brief with the Supreme Court in October, arguing that prediction markets are gambling and should be regulated by states.

"People have to believe in the game," Davis said. "Is it worth the risk? I don't know. I can't answer that."

Davis helped bring the first NFL franchise to Las Vegas when he moved the Raiders there from Oakland in 2020. He said he remembers a time when the NFL took a much harder stance against gambling.

"I remember growing up that there was a big sign in the locker room as you walked out that door that said, 'No gambling whatsoever,' or there's suspension or you're out of the league," he said.

The longtime sports owner has not completely shied away from sports gambling. Both the Raiders and Aces are partners with BetMGM and Davis said MGM Resorts' CEO Bill Hornbuckle was instrumental in his purchasing the Aces.

He acknowledged the financial benefits of gambling but said leagues must weigh the revenue against the potential risks.

"The revenue that is coming from gambling and those types of entities is large so there has to be a balance between integrity and what the good things that it can bring to the league," he said.

As gambling and prediction markets continue to proliferate, Davis said his focus remains on athletes and competition.

"I'm more worried about the game on the field," Davis said. "People ask, 'What do you think you're going to win by,' I said, 'I just want to win by one point.'"

#2924. Powerhouse LA high school football coach mired in controversy fires back at peeved parents: ‘I will not apologize’

The California high school football coach mired in controversy over potential recruiting violations said bluntly this week he has no intentions of apologizing for the way he's running his program.

Just after Westlake head coach Rick Clausen was called out by several of his own players' parents in a scathing letter to the school, the 44-year-old told the California Post on Thursday he's not fretting about the complaints one bit.

Westlake head coach Rick Clausen instructs the offense during football practice at Westlake High School, Tuesday, Aug. 11, 2026. USA TODAY Network via Reuters Connect

"It's comical, to be honest with you," Clausen said. "But with what goes on in today's society, nothing shocks me in any way, shape or form."

Clausen took over a floundering Westlake program in 2025 and immediately turned the team around, going 10-1 in his first year and 7-3 so far in Year Two. That came on the heels of an 0-10 season the year before he took over coaching.

However, last week, a group of parents sent a memo to Westlake High School stating they've enlisted the help of a private investigation company to probe into whether or not Clausen has committed recruiting violations in order to turn the school into a winner.

Westlake head coach Rick Clausen talks to his team after defeating Ventura 21-3 in a football game on Friday, Oct. 16, 2026, at Ventura High. USA TODAY Network via Reuters Connect

The letter was five paragraphs long, and it stated it was authored by "Concerned Westlake High School Parents."

Clausen didn't seem concerned about it at all during an interview with The Post, chalking everything up to "kind of the world, unfortunately, that we're living in now."

"This is kind of what we have done historically with programs," the football coach said. "We've inherited programs that have been down on their luck and we turn them around. Not going to apologize for it. But it doesn't fit everybody."

In a separate statement, Clausen, the Los Angeles Rams Don Shula Coach of the Year Award winner in 2025, added that "everything in our program is earned through competition."

"For some kids and families," he said, "competition is a foreign concept and they feel they are 'entitled' to certain things. That is the old Westlake and that does not fit with the direction of this program going forward."

He also said, "I will not apologize for building a program built on good families, and good kids who have goals and aspirations to go to college."

Clausen and Westlake are slated to play next in a postseason game against Dana Hills on Friday night.

#2923. Barkin sees interest rate hikes ahead, but at a gradual pace

Richmond Federal Reserve President Thomas Barkin said Thursday that he expects the central bank will have to raise interest rates further, though he's not sure by how much or at what pace.

In a CNBC interview that was part of the Richmond Fed's 2026 CFO Summit, the policymaker expressed concern over inflation and said monetary policy will need to adjust.

"Unemployment's near record lows, and inflation is too high. That means the path is pretty straightforward. The question, of course, is pace," Barkin said in the talk with CNBC's Steve Liesman.

"Gradual makes sense if you think there's uncertainty in the world, and if you think that there's information you're going to get in the period. And I think that's still a pace that makes sense to me," he added.

Barkin's comments come a little more than a week after his colleagues voted to keep the Fed's benchmark overnight borrowing rate anchored in a range between 3.75% and 4%. While Barkin is not a voting member on the rate-setting Federal Open Market Committee now, he will be in 2027.

In its post-meeting statement, the FOMC said it is awaiting the "accumulation of more information" as it deliberates where to go next. When the committee OK'd a quarter percentage point hike in September, officials largely agreed that another increase was likely before the end of 2026.

Markets are pricing in a better than 80% chance of a hike in December, then one more move in 2027, according to the CME Group's FedWatch tool.

Barkin said he considers current policy close to a point where it neither restricts nor promotes growth, which officials consider a neutral rate.

"If you're restricting the economy, presumably you're restricting consumption, which is you know roughly two-thirds of the economy, and that's relatively elevated," he said. "So that would make the case that we're a little bit accommodative."

Inflation, he added, "is the most important risk right now ... and I hope that our move in September and whatever else we might choose to do in the future will have some impact on inflation."

Artificial intelligence is one source of price pressures, though he said it eventually will spur production and help ease inflation further down the road.

In a related matter, he said the debt issuance to hyperscalers, estimated this year at better than $500 billion, is "worth watching" for potential credit issues.

"It's pretty hard to convince yourself that some of these big hyperscalers who are generating so much cash from their core businesses aren't good credits," he said. "So it's more who are the intermediaries in this world who are taking on risks that you don't know about. ... You do wonder where in the intermediary sector is the biggest risk."

#2922. Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double

Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers stayed unbeaten Saturday night at Xfinity Mobile Arena, earning a 127-116 win over the Bucks and improving to 3-0 on the season.

Joel Embiid had his 10th career regular-season triple-double, tallying 28 points, 11 rebounds and 10 assists.

On his 30th birthday, Jaylen Brown posted 27 points and five assists. Tyrese Maxey had 26 points. VJ Edgecombe scored 17 and grabbed 13 boards.

The Sixers' absences on the first night of a back-to-back included LeBron James (low back/sciatica management). Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said pregame that he did not know yet who would be available for his team's matchup with the Pistons on Sunday.

Here are observations on the Sixers' victory Saturday:

Dean Wade made his Sixers debut and started in James' place. He'd missed the first two games of the regular season with a left ankle sprain.

The 3-and-D forward scored the night's first points by a sinking a corner three-pointer on the opening possession.

Embiid also had a sharp start. He moved well, made good decisions and served as the main hub of his team's offense in the early going.

The Sixers' seven-time All-Star center has often enjoyed facing Myles Turner. He entered the night with an average of 29.4 points in 18 previous games against the Bucks big man.

Embiid did a little bit of everything in the first quarter, posting eight points, four assists and four rebounds over nine minutes. After snatching an offensive board, Embiid drained a fadeaway jumper to give the Sixers a 10-1 lead.

One of Embiid's assists was quite stylish. He found Edgecombe open beyond the arc with a behind-the-back bounce pass. Edgecombe knocked down the shot and wound up going 3 for 4 from three-point range in the first quarter.

The Sixers added rookie Labaron Philon Jr. to their rotation. Jabari Walker stayed in the mix, though he didn't enter until the 7:36 mark of the second quarter.

Philon played four minutes in his NBA debut and missed his lone shot attempt, a corner three. On defense, Philon allowed a couple of blow-bys off the dribble. He generally looked like he belonged on offense, albeit without any production in his brief run.

Maxey banked in a half-court heave at the first-quarter buzzer to put the Sixers up nine points. The Sixers couldn't carry forward any momentum, however, and began the second quarter poorly.

Adem Bona had another rough stint amid a subpar start to the season. After Anfernee Simons missed a transition layup try, Ryan Rollins' three lifted the Bucks into a 40-37 lead.

The dreadful stretch for the Sixers on both sides of the ball called to mind how they'd fallen into a 29-point deficit Thursday night vs. the Cavaliers before clawing out a comeback win.

The Sixers mustered just seven points in first six minutes of the second quarter and had many stagnant possessions that concluded with contested jumpers. The team's transition defense was dismal for much of the second period. Milwaukee had the game's first 19 fast-break points.

The Sixers regained the lead late in the second quarter. Kentavious Caldwell-Pope made two three-balls in the second and nabbed three first-half steals. Embiid assisted a go-ahead, and-one Walker layup.

Brown hit a three with 11.8 seconds left in the second quarter. Unsurprisingly, his variety of shotmaking skills were useful without James in the lineup.

Even when James or Embiid sit, the Sixers should have a simple advantage in terms of volume of stars over most opponents this year. None of Brown, Embiid or Maxey had the sort of off night that would have improved the Bucks' odds of a road win. They'd all passed the 20-point mark by the end of the third quarter.

Maxey notched another final-possession bucket, this time with a more conventional make. He canned a jumper from the right elbow with 1.7 seconds left in the third to extend the Sixers' lead to 97-87.

The Bucks made a late push in the fourth quarter. Kel'el Ware's put-back, and-one layup cut the Milwaukee's deficit to 117-114.

The Sixers ultimately handled business. Embiid reached his triple-double by assisting a second-chance Brown jumper and and Caldwell-Pope soon drilled a dagger corner three. Embiid even threw down a last-minute dunk for good measure.

For the second consecutive season, the Sixers have a 3-0 start. They got to four wins last year before dropping their first game.

#2918. HSA-eligible health plans in 2027: What to know about tax savings vs. higher deductibles

Healthcare affordability was part of the midterm election cost-of-living debate.

It is uncertain how a new Congress might try to address that pain point. Now, as workers choose their 2027 health insurance coverage, millions face a more immediate decision: whether to pick a plan that lets them contribute to a health savings account, or HSA.

The question is whether an HSA-eligible plan with lower premiums and tax benefits is worth the higher deductible and potential out-of-pocket costs.

One noted advantage of an HSA is its tax benefits.

"You have a triple tax advantage," said Kalei Cadinha-Pua'a, vice chair and CEO of Honolulu-based Cadinha & Co., which ranked No. 21 on this year's CNBC Financial Advisor 100 list.

Employer and employee contributions go into the fund before taxes, earnings in the account aren't taxed while they remain in the HSA, and withdrawals for qualified medical expenses are tax-free.

Read coverage of CNBC's 2026 Financial Advisor 100 list, including stories featuring ranked firms and their advisors:

Financial advisors say employees often overlook another potential tax savings. HSA contributions made through an employer's Section 125 cafeteria plan generally aren't subject to Social Security and Medicare taxes.

That can make payroll contributions more tax-efficient than contributing on your own and claiming a deduction later, said certified financial planner and enrolled agent Andy Panko, the founder of Tenon Financial in Metuchen, New Jersey.

Workers should check how their employer processes contributions and ask if they receive the Social Security and Medicare tax break on HSA contributions.

A worker who chooses to contribute to an HSA outside of payroll may be able to deduct eligible contributions on their federal tax return but generally won't get the break on Social Security and Medicare taxes.

HSAs are typically paired with a high-deductible health plan, so consumers generally have to be enrolled in a high-deductible health plan in order to contribute to the HSA.

Such a plan may have lower premiums relative to others available to consumers, but workers can face higher costs on the back end when they need care.

For 2027, the IRS says an HSA-eligible plan must generally have a deductible of at least $1,750 for individual coverage or $3,500 for family coverage. The plan's annual out-of-pocket expenses, excluding premiums, cannot exceed $8,700 for individual coverage or $17,400 for family coverage.

More employers are offering health plans with an HSA option. Across all U.S. health plan sponsors with at least 50 employees, 63% offer a high-deductible plan that qualifies for an HSA, according to a recent Marsh employer survey. More than a third, 37%, of all employees covered by an employer health plan are enrolled in an HSA-eligible plan.

"Employers often offer an HSA plan as a low-cost option, and then because of the higher deductible and the overall cost-sharing structure, these plans are typically costing less for the employer as well as the employee," said Frank Easley, senior vice president of Aon Health Solutions.

An HSA's tax advantages don't by themselves make an HSA-eligible plan the right choice. Workers should consider how the plan's premiums, deductible and out-of-pocket maximum align with expected healthcare needs before enrolling, financial advisors say.

They should also factor in any employer HSA contributions and the cash they have available to pay an unexpected medical bill before they reach their deductible, says Nathan Lochstampfor, CFP, an associate consultant at Harrisburg, Pennsylvania-based Conrad Siegel Investment Advisors, which is ranked No. 45 on CNBC's Financial Advisor 100 for 2026.

If consumers can't afford to pay the deductible of a high-deductible health plan, it may make sense to choose a plan with higher premiums and lower out-of-pocket costs if they need care, he said.

Lochstampfor recommends clients ask themselves: "If I got in a car accident going home today from work, could I afford that deductible? Or would I be better off maybe paying a little bit of higher premiums until I build up those cash reserves?"

HSA contribution limits for 2027 are $4,500 for individuals and $9,000 for families. People age 55 and older can make a $1,000 catch-up contribution.

You can use an HSA to pay for qualified medical expenses now or later. Any money left in the account generally carries over from year to year, and earnings aren't taxed while they remain in the account. HSA balances can also be invested. As of midyear, 11% of HSA accounts held invested assets, according to Devenir, an HSA investment firm.

Ideally, financial advisors say, you keep some of the balance in cash to cover short-term needs and invest the rest for retirement.

"If you have a long time and you can begin to develop a strategy for savings, it can really work and benefit you in the future when you don't have that earned income to pay for your healthcare needs," Cadinha-Pua'a said.

Some HSA owners pay current medical expenses out of pocket and leave money invested in the account so it can grow tax-free. They can use their HSA funds to reimburse themselves years later for those out-of-pocket expenses. Some conditions apply: It must be a qualified medical expense and incurred after the HSA was established, for example.

That strategy can be smart — but it requires good records.

The IRS wants to make sure expenses aren't double counted. Account holders need to keep records showing that any HSA distributions paid or reimbursed qualified medical expenses weren't also claimed as an itemized deduction.

"Be really honest with yourself," Cadinha-Pua'a said. "Are you a good record keeper?"

She recommends making digital copies of receipts, bills and proof of payment, organizing them in an electronic folder and backing up that folder to the cloud. That can help preserve the documentation if a computer fails or the account holder needs to substantiate a reimbursement years later.

#2917. Ripple strikes deal with Robinhood to expand its RLUSD digital dollar

Ripple has struck a deal with Robinhood to expand the reach of its digital dollar, RLUSD, strengthening trading and financing links between their institutional businesses and improving Robinhood's internal money management.

The partnership involves using RLUSD to settle institutional trades, automate Robinhood's treasury operations and expand access to digital asset trading and financing through Ripple Prime and Bitstamp, the global crypto exchange acquired by Robinhood last year.

It also helps meet Robinhood's ambition of becoming a global financial superapp, centralizing its customers' financial lives while expanding the underlying infrastructure that can serve both retail and institutional clients.

"Utility is what moves stablecoins forward. Working with Ripple, Bitstamp and Robinhood will have access to RLUSD for institutional settlement, Ripple Treasury for our operations and Ripple Prime for financing," Robinhood Crypto General Manager Johann Kerbrat said in a statement shared with CNBC. "That is what a connected, global financial system looks like."

Stablecoins have long been a workhorse of the crypto world, giving traders a way to move money among exchanges, buy and sell crypto assets, and park funds between trades without having to convert back into traditional currency. But as the crypto industry evolves beyond trading and markets move toward 24/7 liquidity, stablecoins are becoming essential financial infrastructure because of their ability to enable near-instant settlement and move dollar-denominated value at any time, regardless of traditional banking hours.

As firms look to do more with stablecoins beyond simply listing them on exchanges, stablecoin issuers are betting they'll favor a one-stop-shop approach that bundles the technology and services needed to move money, settle trades and manage cash.

"Stablecoins have evolved from a crypto trading tool into a critical piece of financial infrastructure," Ripple President Monica Long told CNBC. "The clearest evidence of this is where they're now being embedded — from corporate payments to capital markets. Our partnership with Robinhood reflects that evolution."

"This is about much more than trading," she added. "It's about fundamentally changing how money moves and is managed — giving institutions the ability to operate 24/7/365, unlocking liquidity and putting capital to work more efficiently."

Circle Internet Group, a Ripple competitor, is positioning itself for a similar future, where winning platforms, in addition to issuing stablecoins, also provide the infrastructure to put them to work. Earlier this year, for example, it launched the Circle Payments Network, connecting financial institutions and payment providers using its flagship USDC stablecoin and other digital assets for cross-border payments.

The network is Circle's answer to the 8-year-old Ripple Payments business, a service that helps businesses move money across borders, using any of several assets moving through that system, including USDC, Ripple-created XRP and, as of its debut in December 2024, RLUSD.

RLUSD's market cap has grown to more than $2.5 billion. Tether's USDT and Circle's USDC still dominate the total stablecoin market, which now exceeds $300 billion.

RLUSD will also be listed on Robinhood's trading platform, and is currently available to traders on Bitstamp as well as Robinhood Chain, the company's home-grown blockchain for tokenized stocks and other assets.

Robinhood, which reports third-quarter results for the three months ended in September after the market closes, rose about 2.5% in early trading Tuesday, snapping a three-day decline.

Correction: A previous headline misspelled RLUSD.

Observations after Sixers beat Bulls to reach East-leading 7-1 originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers kept up their hot start to the season Monday night at Xfinity Mobile Arena, reaching 7-1 with a 126-107 win against the Bulls.

Adem Bona missed a dunk early in the second quarter, which stung a bit more when Jalen Smith drilled a three-pointer seconds later.

Bona remained the Sixers' main backup center. He had a poor first-half stint overall amid a largely negative start to his third season. The 23-year-old big man played well in the Sixers' victory over the Bucks, posting six points, 13 rebounds and three blocks. Outside of that, his season has been sparse on bright moments so far. It's reasonable to wonder when Nurse might turn to alternatives like Jabari Walker, who played a good game Saturday in Charlotte, or try James again at the five.

The Bulls outscored the Sixers by eight points in Bona's first run, cutting their derficit to 40-38. Chicago went in front with a three by former Sixer Buddy Hield.

The Sixers regained the advantage late in the second quarter. Maxey and James each were excellent as passers in the first half, combining for 14 assists and three turnovers. James fired the ball to Wade for a corner three that gave the Sixers a 60-52 lead.

Following a third-quarter Embiid fadeaway jumper and two James lay-ins, the Sixers were up 17 points.

The Bulls cut the Sixers' lead down to 11 points by the start of the fourth quarter. With Bona still struggling and on four fouls, Wade began the fourth at center. Bona then briefly spelled Wade before being whistled for his fifth foul and taking a seat. Embiid ultimately wasn't needed for any of the fourth period and only logged 25 minutes.

Embiid said postgame that he was not available to return to the action in the fourth and has been feeling his plantar fasciitis on the court. The seven-time All-Star's health is clearly not close to 100 percent.

"I was on a minutes restriction," he said. "(The foot) has been going like that for the past few weeks. It's painful. Just got to manage it.

"If it flares up pretty bad, got to take a step back. We've been able to manage it, but it has its ups and downs."

The Sixers' lead got as low as eight points, but Chicago did not shoot well enough from three-point range (26.3 percent) or have sufficient firepower to sustain a meaningful comeback run. The bottom line is the Sixers are in a different class than the Bulls, who fell to 1-6.

As for the Sixers, they'll wake up Tuesday morning at the top of the Eastern Conference standings.

"You've got some of these nights where you kind of call them professional wins. …You look at the records and all that kind of stuff, but they have a lot of talent," Nurse said. "A lot of these teams play a way that's a little scary. It's fast and it's furious, lots of threes. … They're going to keep coming at you, high-possession games. So they're not easy games and I'm glad that we handled it professionally tonight."

#2914. Blue Origin pushes launch to January 2027 as it races to rebuild damaged launchpad

Jeff Bezos' Blue Origin has delayed a return to flight for its flagship New Glenn rocket to January 2027, later than its earlier target of the end of this year, as it rushes to recover from a major explosion in May.

Blue Origin CEO Dave Limp told employees in a memo Wednesday that the company's launch timing slipped as a result of delays with reconstructing its Cape Canaveral launchpad, including due to bad weather over the summer.

"This small adjustment doesn't diminish what we've accomplished or the importance of the work still ahead," Limp wrote in the memo, which was viewed by CNBC. "Returning New Glenn to flight remains our top priority — it's worth it."

The Financial Times earlier reported the memo. The January 2027 time frame is about 10 days later than Blue Origin's earlier launch target, a person familiar with the matter told CNBC. The person asked not to be named because the matter is internal.

The New Glenn explosion in May, which occurred during a hot-fire test, badly damaged Blue Origin's Florida launchpad. The company was slated to deploy 48 satellites for Amazon's Leo internet-from-space service just a few days later.

Since then, the company has moved swiftly to repair the launch facility and reworked its pad configuration. Bezos told Fox News last month that the company was still expecting New Glenn to fly again in December.

Limp said Blue Origin has made great progress on the recovery, but he noted that "rebuilding at this pace hasn't been easy."

"Your collective work on our return to flight has been nothing short of remarkable," Limp wrote. "In just six months, Team Blue has accomplished what many thought would take years."

The mishap was inconveniently timed, as Blue Origin has been working to ramp up its launch cadence in an effort to compete with its chief rival, Elon Musk's SpaceX, for commercial and government customers.

The New Glenn delays have hampered Amazon's Leo constellation buildout, for which it relies on Blue Origin for a portion of its launches. Amazon has turned to other providers, including United Launch Alliance and Arianespace, in the interim, especially as SpaceX has phased out Falcon 9 launches to prioritize testing its enormous Starship rocket.

NASA has also tapped Blue Origin for several contracts in the agency's Artemis return-to-the-moon program, including a lunar lander.

The company announced new investments in its Florida launch facilities last month with the goal of supporting multiple flights per week by the end of the decade.

#2913. ‘I'm finding my footing': Jaylen Brown's role taking shape with Sixers

'I'm finding my footing': Jaylen Brown's role taking shape with Sixers originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Jaylen Brown has spent the opening weeks of the season describing the Sixers as a work in progress.

He offered a similarly humble perspective Monday night at Xfinity Mobile Arena. His performance, however, provided the clearest look yet at how he can influence this group while sharing the floor with the club's other established stars.

Brown finished with 25 points, 11 rebounds and five assists in the Sixers' 126-107 win over the Chicago Bulls. He shot 9 for 15 from the field, made four three-pointers and did not commit a turnover.

Five of his rebounds came on the offensive end, an area Brown has emphasized while the Sixers continue piecing together their frontcourt rotation.

"Just a mindset," Brown said. "Just trying to create extra opportunities for my team. As we're trying to patch together our fives and our front line, I try to make an extra contribution and emphasis on the glass."

Brown has also been careful about how he describes his place on a roster that already belonged to Joel Embiid and Tyrese Maxey.

Maxey scored 15 of his 26 points in the fourth quarter Monday, continuing his torrid start to the season. He has led the Sixers in scoring while being the only starter to appear in all eight games.

Brown is not interested in interrupting that.

"Maxey's been leading the way, 100 percent," Brown said. "He's been here. This is his team. Him and Joel have been here for a while, so the mindset has been to complement those guys. I'm finding my footing, but Maxey has been leading the way for sure."

Brown's footing looked firm against Chicago.

Beyond his scoring, he helped the Sixers control the glass, moved the ball within an offense that generated 23 assists during the first half and supplied the physicality Nick Nurse expects from him.

"The dude is tough and he wins," Nurse said. "I'll take that all day long. The degree of difficulty on some of the shots he hit was really amazing. I thought he pounded the glass and got some key rebounds. Makes a lot of winning plays. Man, he's a winner."

Brown spent the first 10 years of his career in Boston. He won a championship there, reached another NBA Finals and grew from a 20-year-old prospect into one of the league's premier two-way wings.

Moving into a new system alongside James, Embiid and Maxey was never going to be seamless.

"I mean, he's fitting in along with all of us," James said. "We're getting better and better. It's not going to happen overnight. He spent his first 10 years in a system in Boston, in the city, so it's going to take some adjustment.

"It takes adjustment for him. It's going to take adjustment for the rest of the guys that joined this franchise as well. But tonight was a great step."

James pointed to Brown's "pressure, his shot-making ability, his rebounding ability" and his professionalism.

Brown's early influence has extended beyond his production.

The Sixers entered Monday with one of the NBA's best half-court defenses. Brown has been one of the group's most persistent voices, stressing communication, preparation and attention to opposing players' tendencies.

"That's the part that I care about the most as a group, not individually," Brown said. "Having a mentality night in and night out, respecting your opponent, respecting the game, making sure that we are executing our coverages, we're being clear in our communication and then we're also taking away tendencies on a night-to-night basis.

"Those things I just explained are over 50 percent of the battle."

Brown understands that maintaining those details across an 82-game schedule will require more than reminders.

"A little bit of accountability," he said. "You learn different guys require different things. How you communicate with each guy may be a little different, but accountability for sure and confrontation if need be.

"The offense is going to be what it's going to be, but our defense has got to be non-negotiable. That's something I say all the time. Non-negotiable. You want to be a good team, defense is non-negotiable."

The Sixers showed both sides of their defensive potential Monday.

They built a double-digit lead during the first half, allowed Chicago to score 36 points in the second quarter and then created separation after halftime. Philadelphia held the Bulls to 20 points in the third quarter before pulling away in the fourth.

Brown did not celebrate their ability to raise their intensity after it slipped.

"I don't like that we have that," he said. "It's good that we have shown the potential, but we've got to keep that switch on. It doesn't work like that where you just flip the switch.

"It can catch up to us and it can bite us against a better team or on a night that we're not being as crisp on offense. It can't be a switch just because we're playing a lesser opponent or whatever the case may be. We've got to make sure that mentality is from the beginning to end. Can't skip any steps."

That urgency accompanies considerable optimism.

Brown said the Sixers enjoy playing together. He loves when James grabs a rebound because "he's like a quarterback," capable of launching an outlet pass or immediately pushing the pace.

The group is learning when to attack, when to move the ball and how to avoid hesitating in its effort to play unselfishly.

"It's a lot of talent, man," Brown said. "We like each other, and we're just kind of building that chemistry of how we can get this thing to a well-oiled machine. I think we're taking steps forward every single day."

The Sixers are 7-1 despite Brown believing those steps remain incomplete. They have won close games, erased sizable deficits and received important contributions from different players.

Brown's performance Monday showed another version of how their pieces can fit.

"We still have a long way to go to being a really, really solid, great team," Brown said. "But we've been able to win some games. We started out the season great, 7-1, and you haven't seen our best version yet.

#2911. Voters say the economy drove them to the polls — and blame Trump for their troubles

As President Donald Trump campaigned for the nation's highest office in 2024, he regularly blamed Democrats for the country's economic anxiety.

During campaign stops across the U.S., Trump frequently asked voters if they were better off than they were four years prior. At the time, his message appeared to land: In an election centered on inflation and the cost of living, Trump won every swing state and the nationwide popular vote.

Two years later, voters grappling with those same kitchen-table issues took away much of his party's power in Washington and in states across the country.

Democrats rode a blue wave of historic proportions in Tuesday's midterm election to flip everything from battleground congressional seats to long-held Republican governorships. The Democratic Party will now have control of both the House and Senate, ending the Republican Party's trifecta of power in the federal government.

Nearly nine out of 10 voters in the CNBC Voter Poll said the cost of living was a crucial or major factor in their vote for Congress. Participants in the poll ranked the economy as the most important issue facing the country during the midterm election, ahead of topics like the state of democracy and immigration. Three out of every four survey participants rated the economy as "poor" or "not so good," compared with just 2% who described it as "excellent."

And perhaps most telling of all: Fewer than one out of every five voters said their family's financial standing was better than it was two years ago.

Much of their dissatisfaction was centered on Trump himself, with about two out of every three voters saying his policies worsened economic conditions in the country.

"It was all about the economy," said Mark Zandi, chief economist at Moody's Analytics. "Most Americans are struggling with the high inflation and have been for quite some time, and they're just angry."

Tuesday's election played out against the backdrop of a war with Iran that has been politically unpopular and exacerbated the inflationary pressures facing everyday Americans. The CNBC Voter Poll found nearly three out of every four people said the war was a crucial or major factor in their vote for Congress.

Trump told Fox News that voters were presumably unhappy with the surge in gas prices, even if they generally agreed with the need for military action against Iran. Vice President JD Vance said on social media that some voters who backed him and Trump in 2024 likely supported Democrats in the midterms as a result of the war.

U.S. drivers paid about $4.15 for an average gallon of gas on Tuesday, the highest of any midterm or presidential election day since at least 2000, according to AAA. Gas prices have surged about 37% over the past year as the Middle East conflict crippled the global crude trade.

"America sent a very clear message," said Patrick De Haan, head of petroleum analysis at GasBuddy, a fuel price tracking platform. "Going into an election with prices that on Election Day have never been higher is something that garners a lot of frustration from consumers."

Higher prices for gas and other energy products have cost the average American household nearly $1,230 since the start of the Iran war, according to an analysis from Zandi. The financial pain felt by U.S. consumers from the conflict has spilled into other areas. Rising borrowing costs and expanded military services roughly cost households more than $900 in addition, according to Zandi.

The consumer price index, a broad basket of goods and services tracked by the Bureau of Labor Statistics, reaccelerated during the war with Iran. Increases in consumer prices have outpaced wage gains in recent months, leaving consumers with less purchasing power.

Prices for diesel, the fuel type used by truckers and trains, climbed to records that transportation executives have said feel "like science fiction." In the latest producer price index report, the BLS said that rising diesel costs pushed up prices for many goods.

The CNBC Voter Poll found more than three out of every five people believed that Trump's policies led to higher prices. Almost nine out of 10 said they were very or somewhat concerned about the prices for a variety of goods such as utilities, food and oil. The poll was a collaborative effort across several news organizations that drew on about 100,000 interviews conducted by phone, online and in-person at polling locations with a margin of error of 0.6%.

Trump took over an economy coming out of the pandemic that was caught in a "vibecession," the term used to describe the disconnect between sour consumer confidence and positive economic fundamentals. With consumer prices now on one of their longest streaks of higher-than-preferred inflation readings on record, the average American's economic sentiment is even worse today.

The University of Michigan's consumer sentiment survey in 2026 fell to the worst levels in its seven-decade history. The Conference Board, meanwhile, said its consumer confidence gauge in September slid to 14-year lows.

Inflation pushed voters to a "breaking point," said Francesco D'Acunto, a Georgetown University professor who researches behavioral economics. "If consumers become angry voters, they vote against the status quo — in this case, the Trump administration."

Before the U.S. began its war with Iran in late February, economists and monetary policymakers were already sounding the alarm about what Trump's policies could mean for the economy.

Trump pitched his plan for broad and steep tariffs as a way to revive domestic manufacturing, but the policy sent financial markets reeling and raised the prospect of a potential recession. Researchers at the New York Federal Reserve said that several consumer prices would have fallen if not for Trump's tariffs, many of which were later struck down by the Supreme Court.

In the CNBC Voter Poll, 64% said Trump's tariff policies hurt the economy in their area. Just 14% said they helped.

Trump's White House also oversaw mass deportations in major cities that gave way to large-scale protests and the death of two U.S. citizens by federal immigration agents. A University of Pennsylvania study found that the cities experiencing immigration enforcement surges collectively saw decreasing foot traffic and billions of dollars in lost consumer spending.

About three out of five voters said they disapproved of how Immigration and Customs Enforcement handled its job and that the Trump administration had gone too far on the issue, according to the CNBC Voter Poll. Still, voters were more likely to say the Republican Party was better at handling immigration.

To be sure, some policies under Trump's White House have been better received. The Internal Revenue Service said in October that the average tax refund grew by 11% following the passage of Trump's "big beautiful bill." So-called Trump Accounts are allowing parents to tap into government and donor funds to build nest eggs for children.

But on Tuesday, voters showed that affordability remained a leading concern as the Iran war continues and inflation pinches pocketbooks. What changed compared with the 2024 presidential election was who they believed could fix it.

In 2024, more voters said they thought Trump was better equipped to handle the economy than former Vice President Kamala Harris. This week, voters said Democrats were better suited than Republicans to manage the economy.

But the party will have a lot of work to do. Seven out of 10 respondents in the CNBC Voter Poll said they felt the U.S. economic system generally favored the wealthy.

"We know pretty clearly what is making Americans upset," said Zandi, who has advised politicians from both parties. "There's another election coming up in two years. Hopefully, policymakers are listening to all this."
