#2909. Bill Belichick nominated for Pro Football Hall of Fame 2027 class after first-ballot snub

The Pro Football Hall of Fame announced coach nominees for the Class of 2027 on Nov. 5, with Belichick headlining the list of five. It marks the second consecutive season for the former New England Patriots head coach, who oversaw one of the greatest dynasties in NFL history.

Belichick's snub on the first ballot sparked outrage across the football world in January, leaving many to question the Hall of Fame's voting process. Now the path to enshrinement in Canton, Ohio, looks a little different as sweeping changes were enacted in September.

The Hall slashed its voting body from 50 members to 25 as part of the change. It will also have three non-voting members that will make up a 28-person selection committee. The three non-voters are set to rotate each season.

JARRETT BELL: I'm a Pro Football Hall of Fame voter. We blew it with Bill Belichick snub | Opinion

Subcommittees initially debated a list of 14 coaching candidates for enshrinement in 2027 before settling on the list of five that includes Belichick, Tom Coughlin, Mike Holmgren, Dan Reeves and Mike Shanahan.

All five nominees found themselves in the same position last year, with Belichick being chosen as the category's representative.

And, just like last year, only one will advance out of the group again to be among the 15 finalists that will include 13 modern-era Players, one coach and one contributor.

Belichick didn't receive the necessary votes for induction as part of the Class of 2026. Under the previous voting rules, the coach would've needed at least 40 of 50 votes to be enshrined in Aug. 2026.

Since voting for the Pro Football Hall of Fame isn't public, it's unclear how many votes Belichick received.

Belichick spoke about the snub during an interview with Fox News' Sean Hannity on the May 19 episode of the "Hang Out with Sean Hannity" podcast.

"Well, it's out of my control," Belichick said. "And honestly, I've been very focused on my job at the University of North Carolina and the players and the university there. So, you know, whatever happens, happens. But I'm proud of what we accomplished as teams at New England and certainly at the Giants, and proud of those relationships. So that's what matters most."

Belichick said appreciated the outpouring of support for his Hall of Fame case in the immediate aftermath of the 2026 snub.

"It's great to hear that support from so many people and from so many different fields – not just football, but many others," he added. "And so, you know, I was very appreciative of that support and the positive comments that they made."

Belichick wasn't willing to speculate on why he wasn't voted in, saying you'd have to ask the voters.

Belichick headlines the list of coach nominees for enshrinement as part of the Pro Football Hall of Fame Class of 2027. The Hall provided a brief bio on each as part of the announcement:

Bill Belichick: NFL coach from 1975-2023 with head coaching jobs with the Cleveland Browns (1991-95) and New England Patriots (2000-2023). Career record: 333-178 (including playoffs) with six Super Bowl titles as a head coach and two others as an assistant.

Tom Coughlin: Spent 20 years as an NFL head coach with the Jacksonville Jaguars (1995-2002) and New York Giants (2004-2015). Including playoffs, posted a 182-157 record and led the Giants to two Super Bowl titles. Also won a title with Giants as an assistant coach.

Mike Holmgren: Head coach of the Green Bay Packers from 1992-98 and the Seattle Seahawks from 1999-2008; won a Super Bowl ring in two Super Bowl trips with the Packers and appeared in another Super Bowl as head coach of the Seahawks. Career record: 174-122 (including postseason). Also won two Super Bowls as an assistant with the San Francisco 49ers.

Dan Reeves: Head coach of the Denver Broncos (1981-1992), New York Giants (1993-96) and Atlanta Falcons (1997-2003). Career record: 201-174-2 (including postseason). Led Broncos (three times) and Falcons (once) to the Super Bowl. Won a title as an assistant with the Dallas Cowboys.

Mike Shanahan: Head coach of the Los Angeles Raiders (1988-89), Denver Broncos (1995-2008) and Washington Redskins (2010-13). Won back-to-back Super Bowls with the Broncos while compiling a 178-144 career record (including playoffs). Also won a title with the San Francisco 49ers as an assistant coach.

Given Belichick's snub last year, it would appear likely that the voting body would avoid a repeat this time around. That would seemingly make the coach a heavy favorite to be inducted.

The Hall announced the list of contributor nominees on Nov. 4, which is headlined by Patriots owner Robert Kraft. Like Belichick, Kraft fell short of receiving the necessary votes for induction in 2026.

Here's a look at the full list of nominees, with the bios provided by the Hall:

Ralph Hay: Owned the Canton Bulldogs from 1918-1922; hosted the NFL's formational meeting in his automobile dealership in downtown Canton.

Robert Kraft: Owner of the New England Patriots since 1994 and chairman and CEO of the Kraft Group. His teams have won six Super Bowls and played in five others – 11 appearances overall in the past 31 seasons. Also key member of several influential NFL owners' committees.

Virginia McCaskey: Principal owner of the Chicago Bears from 1983 until her passing in 2025 at age 102. Daughter of team founder George Halas. During her tenure the Bears won Super Bowl XX during a stretch of five consecutive division titles.

Doug Williams: Nine-year NFL career was punctuated with a Super Bowl title and game MVP honors following a 340-yard, four-touchdown performance against the Broncos to cap the 1987 season. Has spent the past 13 seasons in the front office of the Commanders' franchise. Other stints as a scout or front office executive in the NFL came with the Jacksonville Jaguars and Tampa Bay Buccaneers.

John Wooten: An offensive lineman in the NFL for 10 seasons, he transitioned to scouting and player personnel roles with the Cowboys, Eagles and Ravens for nearly three decades. In 2003, became chair of the Fritz Pollard Alliance, a group working to advance opportunities for minorities in coaching and front office positions across pro football.

#2908. What Wall Street billionaires are saying about Democrats' midterm sweep

America's billionaires woke up Wednesday to a political reckoning — and a Wall Street rally.

As Democrats celebrated a sweeping midterm victory, some of the country's wealthiest donors traded barbs over record election spending. Others warned the results could threaten the artificial intelligence boom.

Wall Street, however, cheered the outcome. The Dow Jones Industrial Average soared 1,569 points, or 3%, while the S&P 500 gained 1.6% and the Nasdaq Composite rose 1.4%, as investors welcomed political gridlock, reduced uncertainty and President Donald Trump's willingness to accept the results.

Virgin Group founder Richard Branson took aim at the billions spent influencing the election.

"I have felt embarrassed by the billions that tech entrepreneurs and hedge fund managers have spent," Branson wrote Wednesday on LinkedIn, calling for campaign finance reform. Branson did not give donations that met the reporting threshold in the 2026 election cycle.

His comments came as Elon Musk, the election's largest individual donor, watched his preferred Republicans lose the Texas Senate and governor races despite pouring $224 million into the state — nearly half his record $474 million in political giving nationwide, according to a CNBC analysis of campaign finance records.

Early Wednesday, the Tesla and SpaceX CEO suggested on X that data centers in space were looking increasingly appealing before joking that his "election-interfering space lasers" had failed.

LinkedIn co-founder Reid Hoffman, another major political donor, took a sharper tone.

"We should get money out of politics," Hoffman wrote late Tuesday on X, accusing Musk of spending "outrageous amounts to keep authoritarian clowns in power" while defending his own contributions to Democrats.

Other Democratic megadonors celebrated. "The world changed yesterday," Alex Soros wrote Wednesday on X, hailing the results as a generational victory after his family contributed roughly $103 million to Democratic political efforts.

But beyond the political sparring, some of the country's richest investors were focused on a potentially bigger consequence of the Democratic sweep: the future of the AI boom.

Even before polls closed Tuesday, Pershing Square founder Bill Ackman warned on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" that slowing AI development could jeopardize America's technological edge. "Practically speaking, we can't slow down," he said.

As results rolled in Tuesday night, billionaire investor Chamath Palihapitiya sounded an even starker warning.

Palihapitiya initially questioned whether the early results would hold, but warned that if they did, the implications for the AI trade could be severe.

"And the only way to sink the AI trade is to sink the data centers," Palihapitiya said during an election night livestream of the "All-In" podcast.

Texas offered an early test of those fears. Democratic Gov.-elect Gina Hinojosa, who has proposed a moratorium on new data center construction until stronger safeguards are established, defeated three-term Republican incumbent Greg Abbott.

During the same livestream, Altimeter Capital CEO Brad Gerstner warned the Democratic wave could mean at least 10 to 15 gigawatts of expected AI data center capacity would not come online in 2027. Wall Street analyst Dan Ives estimated Wednesday that 10% of planned U.S. data centers would no longer go ahead.

Advanced Micro Devices CEO Lisa Su acknowledged the growing resistance to AI's expansion, urging technology companies to demonstrate the benefits of data centers to local communities.

"At the end of the day, what we need to do is ensure that everybody wins from AI," Su told CNBC on Wednesday.

Yet Wall Street may have found opportunity in the same slowdown that worried AI's biggest backers.

Fewer new data centers could increase the value of existing facilities while easing the enormous spending burden on technology giants. Big Tech stocks surged Wednesday, adding more than $750 billion in market value at one point, even as smaller, debt-dependent data center operators tumbled.

#2906. Not much has worked for the Houston Texans on the ground. Here's what has: Woody Marks and run right

The Houston Texans entered the season hoping for a revamped running game after struggling on the ground in 2025. That hasn't happened.

Entering Sunday's game against the Los Angeles Chargers, the Texans rank fourth-worst in the league in yards per carry and have the second-fewest carries through eight weeks.

"I want us to move the football," coach DeMeco Ryans said. "Run, pass, it doesn't matter. I want us to move the football and score points."

Houston has mostly done that through the air, with most of its total yardage coming from the passing game. But even with the run game less involved, some trends have emerged.

The Texans' 27-21 overtime loss to the New York Giants on Oct. 25 ended two streaks that had lasted through the first two years of Woody Marks' career.

Houston had been 10-0 when Marks carried the ball at least 11 times in a game since the Texans drafted him in 2025. The team had also been 11-0 when he rushed for at least 44 yards.

Marks rushed 19 times for 66 yards against the Giants, but it wasn't enough to help the Texans win or keep either streak alive.

"He sees the hole and hits it hard," Texans center Evan Brown said. "It's been fun blocking for him. We've got to do a better job giving him a little bit more of a crease to hit sometimes, and we'll keep working at that as we go."

David Montgomery, acquired in an offseason trade with Detroit, got off to a fast start in his first game with the Texans. He scored three touchdowns, and Houston rushed for 124 yards in an overtime loss to Buffalo in Week 1. But the Texans followed with three straight games of 70 yards or fewer. They had just one regular-season game with fewer than 70 rushing yards last season.

The rushing attack has been better over the past three games, especially in the win over the Jacksonville Jaguars, when Marks found success running to the right side of the offensive line.

{ "__type": "devHubFreeformEmbed", "__id": "Datawrapper", "__fallbackImage": "datawrapper.dwcdn.net/oGyBn/mobile.png", "__data": { "datawrapper_id": "oGyBn" } }

Nearly 74% of Marks' rushing yards over the past three games came on runs inside the right side of the line or to the right. The Texans' more experienced starters on that side are right tackle Trent Brown and right guard Ed Ingram.

"I'm not sitting there intentionally trying to run right every single time," second-year offensive coordinator Nick Caley said. "Like, I can promise you that."

Marks' longest run of the season has been 12 yards, though he has had longer runs called back by penalties. A 27-yard run against the Giants was negated by a holding penalty, and his 24-yard touchdown against the Tennessee Titans was nullified by another holding call.

"When you get them big runs and it finally opens up and you turn around and it's a flag, it's just like, dang," Marks said.

It's not just a problem for Marks. The Texans' longest run of the season so far was a 25-yard touchdown by Jawhar Jordan against the Jaguars.

Every team in the league except the Cincinnati Bengals has had at least one run longer than 25 yards. The Bengals and Texans are the only teams with fewer than two runs of 20 or more yards.

#2905. The Packers are asking Jordan Love to do something historically difficult

Jordan Love is putting together one of the best seasons of his career. That's good timing, because the Packers aren't making things particularly easy on him.

Through eight games, Love leads the NFL with 2,246 passing yards and has thrown 16 touchdowns. He's on pace for a franchise-record 4,772 passing yards, potentially surpassing Aaron Rodgers' 2011 season. But there's a caveat: Love is being asked to overcome problems that few quarterbacks could reasonably be expected to sustain.

The Packers are averaging just 60.6 rushing yards per game, the lowest mark in the NFL over the past decade. They're also responsible for 23 dropped passes, eight more than any other team.

Against Carolina, Green Bay managed just 66 rushing yards on 26 carries. Opposing defenses continue to make Love beat them through the air. So far, he has. His development into a quarterback capable of carrying an offense is encouraging, and the team's 5-3 record reflects that resilience.

But there's a difference between having a quarterback who can overcome adversity and building an offense that consistently puts him in that position. The Packers don't need Love to do more. They need the rest of the offense to do more for him.

Unfortunately not much! The trade deadline is approaching, and I suppose there's a possibility that the Packers acquire a new running back. But given the run blocking the team has displayed this year, how much difference might a better back make? Speaking of the line, it's possible that their run blocking could improve and that could alleviate some of the issues. But don't hold your breath there. As for pass catchers… anyone can receive a pass, but it's catching of the pass. That's the key. Can the Packers receivers turn that around?

In the meantime, I'm choosing to simply marvel at the fact that, through 8 games, Jordan Love seems entirely capable of keeping me entertained on Sundays. It's extremely impressive given the circumstances, and that will have to do.

#2904. AI debt fears ease ahead of Oracle, Alphabet and Amazon updates. Here's what to watch.

Fears over artificial intelligence debt have eased after a bout of volatility. But more tests for AI debt are coming.

Oracle holds its Investor Day on Wednesday. And hyperscalers Alphabet and Amazon.com are scheduled to report quarterly results after the market close on Wednesday and Thursday, respectively.

Credit markets are on edge over a flood of AI debt, though fears have eased up a little lately. Meta Platforms' plans for a $95 billion stock offering, announced on Monday, helped bring down its credit-default swap spreads. Those spreads had risen last week when the company reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter. Other CDS spreads had moved higher too, though they have since backed off.

Investors had expected Meta to bring a large bond deal to market shortly after its earnings announcement, said Richard Cheng, head of Nuveen's investment-grade corporate sector team. The fact that it wasn't announced came as a relief.

"Just the absence of the bond deal itself is a direct catalyst for AI-related spread tightening," he said.

A credit spread is the difference in the yield between the corporate bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. When spreads tighten, it means investors are becoming more confident about the bond issuer.

The market action over the last few days shows that investors are recognizing that "credit fears may have been overblown," said CNBC contributor Paul Meeks, head of technology research at Freedom Capital Markets.

"I expect that these better feelings, maybe not good but better, may continue as companies announce their quarters over the next few weeks."

Still, spreads on CDS remain wider over the past year due to a surge of debt issuance and free cash flows turning negative across the AI space. Meta's CDS spread, for instance, is still up 177% year to date, while Amazon's spread has gained 129%.

"It's not necessarily fundamentals," said CreditSights analyst Jordan Chalfin. "It's more, I'd say, 80% driven by technicals and sentiment."

Alphabet and Amazon pose the next big hurdle for tech bonds as they update their capex plans and free cash flow forecasts. Microsoft's earnings are also due Wednesday, but the company has yet to issue any bonds to pay for its AI spending.

Both Amazon and Alphabet reported negative free cash flow in the second quarter and analysts expect that trend to continue.

Analysts expect Alphabet to report free cash flow of -$2.3 billion while spending $55.2 billion on capital expenditures in the third quarter, according to FactSet consensus estimates. Amazon is expected to report -$9.9 billion in free cash flow against capex of $58.7 billion.

S&P Global Ratings estimated in an August report that the hyperscalers will have negative free operating cash flow in 2026 and 2027.

More critical will be their outlooks, Cheng says. "The singular focus on the investors' minds is really what the capex guidance trajectory looks like across the hyperscaler space."

Meeks believes elevated capex is a sign of strong AI demand. The hyperscalers have "decided that with all the evidence they have — and they have more than you and me — that this is worthwhile spending, and not just worthwhile; it's required," he said.

CreditSights' Chalfin doesn't expect the hyperscalers to announce major new bond issuance around their earnings reports. Oracle's updates may be more critical for credit markets. The company has issued $117 billion in long-term debt and is clinging to an investment-grade credit rating of BBB-, the lowest level of investment grade above junk, according to S&P.

"It's the most important IG tech credit, just given how it's a bit more stressed relative to the A-rated names," he said. "It's trading more like a high-yield credit, even though it's IG, and so there's just a lot of investor concern on that one."

One wrinkle to the AI debt story: balance sheets don't reveal the whole picture. The companies are using complicated off-balance sheet arrangements for data centers and chip financing, and they aren't fully transparent, S&P Global said in a September report.

"There are commitments they are entering into, for example, into data center leases or purchase commitments on chips that aren't necessarily treated as debt," Mark Habib, digital infrastructure sector lead at S&P Global Ratings, said on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" Monday.

S&P is trying to provide the market with more visibility on those commitments "to give a fuller picture of what kinds of obligations they're actually entering into," he added.

Oracle aside, hyperscaler credit ratings are all comfortably above A levels — reflecting the fact that they have broadly diversified revenue streams, ample coverage for interest payments, and capacity to add leverage if they choose.

Nuveen's Cheng isn't concerned, pointing out that while there has been some credit repricing, there is no credit crisis.

"They continue to have exceptional credit profiles, strong cash generation liquidity," he said of the largest hyperscalers.

"The market is really not concerned about credit quality at this point," Cheng added. "It's really just the trajectory of the capex needs and the supply of debt to fund that. Both of those are pointing in one direction — and that's up."

Correction: This story has been revised to reflect that Oracle's investment-grade credit rating is BBB-, according to S&P. A previous version mischaracterized the company's credit rating.

#2903. California’s billionaire tax faces defeat, but Democrats plan higher taxes on the wealthy

A version of this article appeared in CNBC's Inside Wealth newsletter, a weekly guide to the high-net-worth investor and consumer. Sign up to receive future editions, straight to your inbox.

The likely defeat of the California billionaire tax may energize Democrats in Congress to propose federal tax hikes on high earners along with a potential federal wealth tax, according to tax experts.

While the votes in California are still being counted, 56% of the state's voters have voted "no" on the billionaire tax while 44% voted "yes." The ballot measure requires a majority of votes for approval, so election experts say a defeat is likely. The tax, known as Proposition 40, would have imposed a one-time levy of 5% on the net worth of Californians worth over $1 billion.

Two ballot measures designed as poison pills against the billionaire tax are garnering strong support. Proposition 41 and Proposition 42, which limit revenue use and prevent any state taxation of personal assets, are both showing majority "yes" votes. If either gets more votes than the billionaire tax, the billionaire tax is defeated.

Service Employees International Union-United Healthcare Workers West, the healthcare union that sponsored the billionaire tax proposal, all but conceded defeat Tuesday night, saying they will try again in 2028 unless the legislature takes action to "make billionaires pay their fair share."

Proposals to raise income taxes on the wealthy fared better in other states. In Washington State, voters supported a new "millionaire tax," which would impose a 9.9% tax rate on household income of more than $1 million a year. In Colorado, the passage of a plan to replace the flat tax of 4.4% with a graduated income tax with a top rate of 8.4% on income over $1 million, is too close to call.

With Democrats now set to control both the House and the Senate, tax experts expect a flurry of new tax proposals aimed at raising taxes on high earners to cut taxes for lower- and middle-income taxpayers.

"It is clear Democrats will try to lower taxes for middle-income households and raise taxes on certain businesses and high-income households," said Howard Gleckman, nonresident fellow at the Tax Policy Center. "But it is much too soon to know how."

The proposals could include plans like those floated last year by Senators Chris Van Hollen, D-Md, and Cory Booker, D-N.J. Van Hollen's plan calls for a new graduated surtax on those earning more than $1 million, starting with a 5% surtax for those making between $1 to $2 million and rising to 12% for those earning more than $5 million a year. The new top rate would be 49% under the existing 37% top marginal rate.

The hikes would be offset by a tax cut on middle- and lower-income taxpayers, creating a new exemption equal to $46,000 for single filers and $92,000 for joint filers. Van Hollen has also proposed increasing the capital gains top tax rate from 23.8% to 35.8%.

Booker's plan is similar, but would increase the top rate to 43% and more than double the standard deduction to $37,500 for single filers and $75,000 for joint filers.

While taxing the wealthy has proven popular among Democrats, pairing the tax hikes with tax cuts for lower or middle-income taxpayers could prove challenging given the deficits, tax experts say.

"A key question is the debate about how much to prioritize further cuts at all, given the revenue needs for the deficit situation and the Democrats' spending priorities," said Garrett Watson, vice president of federal tax policy at the Tax Foundation. He said those priorities include reversing Trump-era tax cuts as well as expanding spending.

Plans for a federal wealth tax or tax on unrealized gains could also get more attention. Congressman Ro Khanna, D-Calif., who campaigned for the California billionaire tax and whose Silicon Valley district is home to substantial tech wealth, teamed up with Sen. Bernie Sanders, I-Vt, on what they call the "Make Billionaires Pay Their Fair Share Act."

The plan would impose a 5% tax on total net worth of those worth more than $1 billion. Yet unlike California's tax, which was a one-time levy, the Khanna-Sanders plan would be an annual tax.

Of course, President Trump would likely veto any Democratic tax plan, especially if it takes aim at the Trump tax cuts passed in 2017 and extended and expanded last year. But tax experts say any Democratic plans introduced in the next two years could influence tax policy in the 2028 election and highlight the party's priorities.

"Proposals made in 2027 will shape the direction of policy in the future," the Tax Foundation said in a report. "While their ambitions to raise taxes will be limited by President Trump's veto, these proposals will serve as messaging and discussion vehicles over the next two years on the direction of the tax code."

The Inside Wealth newsletter by Robert Frank is your weekly guide to high-net-worth investors and the industries that serve them.

#2902. Democrats to probe JPMorgan's relationship with Jeffrey Epstein when they take Senate control

Senate Democrats are gearing up to investigate JPMorgan Chase's banking relationship with the late sex offender Jeffrey Epstein once they take control of Congress early next year.

Democratic staffers on the Senate Finance Committee intend to home in on how high-ranking executives at JPMorgan handled Epstein, a source familiar with the panel's thinking told CNBC on Thursday. The person spoke on condition of anonymity because the probe of the nation's biggest bank has not been announced.

The Finance Committee is likely to be chaired beginning in January by Sen. Ron Wyden, an Oregon Democrat who currently is the panel's ranking member. Wyden has made clear he plans to make full use of the subpoena powers that committee majorities wield.

Democratic staffers aim to focus in particular on decisions about Epstein made by Mary Callahan Erdoes, the CEO of JPMorgan's asset and wealth management division, the source said.

JPMorgan declined to comment. The bank has previously said it regrets any association with Epstein but that it did not assist him in committing crimes.

In a 2023 deposition, Erdoes said she was aware as early as 2006 that Epstein was accused of paying cash to have underage girls brought to his home. Epstein had been a client of the bank since 1998.

Epstein became a registered sex offender after pleading guilty in 2008 in Florida to a state criminal charge of procuring an underage girl for prostitution.

JPMorgan CEO Jamie Dimon in his own, later deposition in 2023, said Erdoes had authority to cut off Epstein as a client. She did not take that step until 2013.

Epstein, a former friend of President Donald Trump, ex-President Bill Clinton and many other high-profile people, died in a jailhouse suicide in August 2019, weeks after being arrested on federal child sex trafficking charges.

A lawyer for the U.S. Virgin Islands told a federal judge in New York in 2023 that JPMorgan "was a full-service bank for Jeffrey Epstein's sex trafficking," and that it waited until after he killed himself to notify the Treasury Department of $1 billion in suspicious transactions related to trafficking by Epstein.

"This was a CYA [cover your a--] reporting after 16 years of all of the monies flowing in his JPMorgan accounts after he was dead," the lawyer, Mimi Liu, said.

JPMorgan has blamed former investment banking chief Jes Staley for concealing or failing to report criminal activity related to Epstein, with whom he had a close relationship.

Staley, who left JPMorgan in 2013 and later became CEO of Barclays before stepping down in 2021, has denied knowledge of Epstein's crimes.

House Oversight Chair James Comer, R-Ky., after a closed-door interview with Staley in July, said that the former executive had encouraged JPMorgan to keep Epstein as a client.

In mid-2023, JPMorgan agreed to a $290 million settlement with victims of Epstein without admitting wrongdoing. That same year, JPMorgan agreed to pay $75 million to settle a separate lawsuit by the U.S. Virgin Islands alleging that the bank facilitated and benefited from the sex trafficking of young women by Epstein.

In August, Wyden released a report accusing top officials at multiple banks of "looking the other way" as Epstein moved hundreds of millions of dollars to finance a yearslong sex trafficking operation.

"The report exposes how top bankers shielded Epstein from federal scrutiny by delaying the reporting of Epstein's suspicious financial activity to the U.S. government, likely in violation of federal anti-money-laundering laws," Wyden's office said in a statement that month.

"It includes new details about the extent of suspicious activity reported by Deutsche Bank after Epstein's death, new details from bank reports filed about suspicious transfers to Epstein through accounts at Bank of America, and names top officials at JPMorgan Chase and other banks who were responsible for reporting Epstein's shady activities to the government but instead protected Epstein in order to gain access to Leon Black and other billionaires."

Black, former CEO of Apollo Global Management, was revealed to have paid Epstein $158 million for financial advice in the years after his 2008 conviction.

In September, Black sued the House oversight panel, seeking to block subpoenas it had issued him for his testimony and for documents related to its Epstein probe.

On Wednesday, Black sued Rep. Robert Garcia, D-Calif., who is poised to become chair of the Oversight Committee in the next Congress, alleging Garcia defamed him in statements regarding Epstein. The lawsuit focuses on Garcia's claims in a TV interview that Black "is at the center of Jeffrey Epstein's crimes" and "was one of the last people to actually interact" with Epstein before his death, among others.

The Department of Justice's mountain of files related to Epstein became a major focus in late 2025 and early 2026, after Congress passed a bill ordering the DOJ to make those records public.

Trump's name is referenced thousands of times in the files that have been released.

The president has not been charged with any wrongdoing in connection with Epstein, with whom he fell out in the early 2000s.

#2899. Here's what the Democratic election rout could mean for the U.S. economy

The Democratic midterm election sweep could result in changes in economic policies related to trade, spending priorities and artificial intelligence. But the biggest forces shaping the economy aren't going to change just because the party in power did.

Affordability pressures, heavy levels of government spending and growth that is dependent on the artificial intelligence tail wind are likely to persist, economists say. And all of those factors pose risks to the economy.

"It's in a very kind of fragile place, and I don't know that it would take a lot to push the economy off the rails, and recession becomes a much greater risk," said Mark Zandi, chief economist at Moody's Analytics. "That isn't the case at the moment, but I don't think that's gone away."

Zandi said he had been worried before the election that the economy was under threat from inflation, an underwhelming labor market and geopolitical dangers, and remains concerned now.

Democrats romped to upset wins in not only House and Senate races but also governor and other state races. But economic and foreign policy is largely set in the executive branch, and President Donald Trump remains far apart from his Democratic adversaries, though he said Wednesday he anticipates working with them.

"I don't expect any change in economic policy. The Iran war, the tariffs and trade policy, the heavy-handed immigration policy, all are done under executive order. They're not legislatively driven," Zandi said.

"The president thought they were good policies before the election. I don't know why he'd change his mind after the election," Zandi added. "So I think they will continue to be policies that restrain growth and add to inflationary pressures and interest rates."

Zandi sees the AI hyperscaler buildout as a key to economic growth, but also a source of potential peril.

"The risks are decidedly to the downside because the growth is so dependent on AI, and the AI growth effects are so dependent on the equity market, and the equity market is so dependent on the valuations," he said. "It just feels like kind of a very fragile place, and the labor market is not well functioning."

Local opposition to data centers was a fairly significant issue in the election, particularly for Democrats, according to CNBC polling, so that could be a further economic headwind, though the issue ranked below cost of living, corruption and several others.

The election happened amid a dichotomy in economic views.

Top-line numbers such as gross domestic product, consumer spending and the unemployment rate tell the story of an economy growing briskly while consumers spend and most Americans who want jobs are at work. GDP accelerated at a 3.4% annualized pace in the third quarter while consumer spending grew 3.8%. The unemployment rate is at 4.2% while layoffs are around their lowest level since 1969.

However, rising prices have overshadowed those otherwise solid metrics.

The inflation rate in September as judged by the consumer price index was 3.7% — up sharply from 2.3% in April 2025 when Trump announced an aggressive round of so-called reciprocal tariffs against U.S. trading partners. The Iran war has only aggravated those conditions. The CPI was at just 2.4% when the U.S. and Israel launched attacks on Iran in late February, a war that has caused gas prices to soar.

And wages aren't keeping up with rising costs. Wage growth when adjusted for inflation has turned negative, down 0.7% from a year ago in September, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics.

One area where the two sides of the political divide could come together is tariffs.

Congressional Democrats haven't been reflexively opposed to tariff efforts aimed at leveling the playing field. But the Trump tariffs have been criticized for their sweeping nature.

Gary Cohn, who served as director of the National Economic Council during Trump's first term, said Wednesday that he sees room for movement on trade issues.

"There's become a much more universal view on tariffs and what they're good for," Cohn said during a CNBC interview. "Protecting U.S. workers and protecting manufacturing, protecting jobs in the United States, specifically from China, more than anything, is good and has been good policy."

While Cohn noted that "shutting down the borders between Canada and Mexico and the United States has probably not been so good for policy," he thinks "there's a compromise that could be reached where we actually have tariffs put through the legislative system and they become law. They wouldn't become executive orders that can be rescinded the minute the next president walks off the podium."

Democrats would also likely look to roll back spending cuts to SNAP food stamp benefits and Medicaid that were approved by the Republican-controlled Congress under the One Big Beautiful Bill Act, Morgan Stanley analysts said in a client note. But other policies that Democrats oppose would be harder to reverse.

"We think policy uncertainty since the beginning of President Trump's second term has been tied to executive, not legislative, action," the firm said. "Policies on tariffs, geopolitics, trade ... deregulation, and export controls are all likely to persist, meaning we should expect a certain level of policy uncertainty to continue."

High levels of government debt and budget deficits also are likely to remain.

The national debt currently stands at $40.5 trillion, up more than $4 trillion since Trump took office in January 2025. Net interest on the debt totaled $1.05 trillion in fiscal 2026, higher than spending on any other category except Social Security and Medicare.

The U.S. ended the year with a deficit just shy of $2 trillion, or about 6.1% of GDP. Morgan Stanley expects that level to persist even with some Democratic officials preaching fiscal discipline.

While there are concerns that the change in power could result in standoffs over the debt ceiling, "Democrats are likely to leverage [an impasse] to win policy concessions," Jan Hatzius, chief economist at Goldman Sachs, said in a note.

"It is not yet clear what Democrats will seek in return, but limiting the war with Iran, tariff rollbacks, the reversal of spending cuts, and expanded health benefits appear to be high on the list of priorities," he said.

Despite the various headwinds and political uncertainty, most of Wall Street expects the growth in the economy to continue — if not without hiccups and lingering issues over the cost of living.

"My sense is that the economic expansion will continue to roll on, featuring above trend growth near 2½ percent," said Joseph Brusuelas, chief economist at RSM.

Brusuelas also sees the opportunity to "reset" the trade agreement between the U.S., Mexico and Canada after a year and a half of tension from the Trump tariffs and reprisals.

"We only have a 15% probability of recession over the next 12 months," he said. "The outcome of the election was immaterial to that estimate."

#2898. Dale Jr. Says NASCAR Teams Taking Investors ‘Don’t Pull All the Strings Anymore’

Dale Earnhardt Jr. says NASCAR teams bringing in outside investors give up some control, a trend he called "kind of scary."

Earnhardt talked about the push for investors on The Dale Jr. Download this week, after co-host TJ Majors brought it up.

"I mean, there's all these freaking teams talking about wanting investors. That's wild to me," Majors said.

"That's the way this, the sports landscape as a whole, not just racing, is going," Earnhardt said. "It's kind of scary because as soon as you bring in investors, you lose control, a little bit of control."

"Those investors are going to want results. They're going to want control. They're going to want to, you know, know what's going on," he added. "And now this team that you were pulling all the strings on, you don't pull all the strings anymore."

His comments come days after Brad Keselowski reached an agreement with RFK Racing on Oct. 31 to give up his management role. Keselowski tried to buy RFK, but Jack Roush and Fenway Sports Group turned down his offer.

Keselowski is leaving the team after this season and will stay a passive investor for now while he plans to sell his 20% stake. His roles in team governance, competition oversight and the board have ended. He has said the split was not adversarial. Keselowski joined RFK as a driver and co-owner in 2022, alongside Jack Roush and Fenway Sports Group.

Several Cup teams have brought in outside partners in recent years. Legacy Motor Club announced more members of its ownership group this week, and Joe Gibbs Racing and Spire Motorsports also have outside investors.

Part of the reason is value. NASCAR's settlement with 23XI Racing and Front Row Motorsports made charters permanent, which made Cup teams a more secure investment.

Earnhardt knows the ownership side well. He co-owns JR Motorsports with his sister, Kelley Earnhardt Miller, and Rick Hendrick. The team races full time in the O'Reilly Auto Parts Series and has entered the Daytona 500 the last two years. Earnhardt has been open about wanting to run full time in Cup.

Running a Cup team takes millions each year. Sports Business Journal has reported that charter values are expected to reach $50 million to $100 million.

Earnhardt spent 18 seasons racing at NASCAR's top level and now runs a team in motorsports himself, so he has seen both sides of ownership.

His comments came the same week Dodge confirmed its Cup return with Kaulig Racing. Legacy is one example of how outside investment can work. Co-owner Jimmie Johnson has said new investment diluted his original stake, though he still controls the company.

#2896. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney

#2895. Observations after Sixers earn strong road win over Pistons, Brown scores 30

Observations after Sixers earn strong road win over Pistons, Brown scores 30 originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers remain the Eastern Conference's sole leader.

They earned a 112-106 road win over the Pistons on Wednesday night to move to 8-1 on the season and stay at the top of the standings. Detroit fell to 6-2.

The Sixers last started 8-1 in the 2023-24 season, which was Nick Nurse's first as the team's head coach.

Jaylen Brown scored a season-high 30 points and pulled down seven rebounds.

Joel Embiid had 26 points and a season-best 15 rebounds. LeBron James posted 13 points, 10 rebounds and eight assists. Tyrese Maxey added 19 points and seven assists.

The Pistons' leading scorers were Duncan Robinson with 27 points and Cade Cunningham with 26.

The Sixers were down Dominick Barlow (lower back stress reaction) and Ariel Hukporti (right Achilles tendon tear). The Pistons' injured players included Jalen Duren (right hamstring strain) and Isaiah Joe (right hip contusion).

Nurse told reporters in Detroit he thinks "it'll be another 10 to 12 days before they kind of clear (Barlow) for getting on the court to see where he is," according to Sixers Wire's Ky Carlin.

The Sixers will visit the Cavaliers on Friday night. Here are observations on their victory over Detroit:

Embiid assisted the Sixers' first basket with a sharp pass out of a double team. He saw Brown's cut along the baseline and dished to him for a layup.

Detroit ran several double drag screen actions early, forcing Embiid to defend on the perimeter. The Pistons started 7 for 11 from the floor and took a 17-11 edge on a Robinson three-pointer.

The Sixers then made a 10-0 run and Embiid was right in the middle of it. He converted a put-back layup after a pretty high-low feed from James. Embiid hit two foul shots to give the Sixers the lead. The team raised its defensive level and Detroit's offense grew frigid.

Embiid was very good on the glass in the first half, grabbing eight rebounds. The Sixers had an excellent rebounding game as a team, limiting the Pistons to four offensive boards over 48 minutes.

Embiid didn't break out of his early-season three-point shooting slump, going 0 for 3. He's 3 for 26 (11.5 percent) on the season and hasn't made a three since the Sixers' second game.

Late in the second quarter, Embiid got into a dead-ball dust-up with Tolu Smith. After an officials' review, Embiid was assessed a technical foul for "initiating" the altercation.

Playing on his 26th birthday, Maxey missed his first four field goals and was scoreless until he hit a floater with 31.5 seconds left in the first quarter.

Brown picked up the scoring slack, tallying 10 points in the first on 4-for-6 shooting and hitting a deep, buzzer-beating three to put the Sixers up 29-21. He produced at all three levels, chose smart spots to attack and looked comfortable in the rhythm of the Sixers' offense.

Meanwhile, James did his usuall stellar work as a passer. He assisted two Adem Bona slams in the second quarter and constantly spotted open teammates. Nurse rewarded Bona's strong opening stint, playing him for a lengthy stretch.

Brown swished a corner triple to extend the Sixers' lead to 48-31. With a 3-for-5 night, Brown is at 45.7 percent from long distance so far this season. It wouldn't be surprising at all if Brown's three-point shooting improved from last season's 34.7 percent clip. With such an abundance of star power, the Sixers' hope will be that everyone gets regular open looks beyond the arc throughout the year.

As for Maxey, he sat with 3:26 left in the second quarter after being whistled for his third foul. Even with a mere two points from Maxey, the Sixers entered halftime holding a 14-point advantage.

The Pistons played better basketball to begin the third quarter.

Following a John Collins alley-oop jam, Nurse called timeout with his team's lead down to single digits. The Pistons' offense sustained some success, but the Sixers prevented a sustained run.

Embiid played an especially physical, aggressive third quarter. He tipped in his own miss and Maxey then sunk a three to make the Sixers' lead 84-68. They were up as many as 17 points in the third.

Embiid sat out the fourth quarter Monday night for the Sixers' win over the Bulls and said he was on a minutes restriction (25) because of his right foot plantar fasciitis. He played the fourth period against the Pistons and logged 33 minutes. Bona went back to the locker room early in the fourth. Nurse told reporters postgame that Bona had "neck soreness."

Detroit did not let the Sixers coast to victory.

Robinson drained a three. Cunningham made a pair of free throws and drove in for a layup that cut the Pistons' deficit to 106-102.

The Sixers halted the Pistons' run before anything disastrous was on the table. Brown blocked a Cunningham three-point try, Embiid knocked down two foul shots, and the East's No. 1 early-season team improved to 4-0 on the road.

#2893. Wall Street's fear gauge surges the most since April. Why traders think it should be even higher

Whether it's a referendum on the midterms or just a continuation of a bear market in semiconductors that dates back to the group's peak in June, one thing's clear from the options action Thursday: few traders saw this coming.

That's evident in the surge in Cboe Volatility index (VIX), which jumped as much as 5.7 points to above 28 about 90 minutes after the opening bell on Thursday. It's the highest reading on VIX since March 31, the day after the S&P 500 bottomed after selling off as tensions between Iran, Israel and the U.S. reached fever pitch.

From its intraday low to high Thursday, VIX moved more than 11 points, the biggest one-day move since April 9. Yet with the S&P 500 down 3%, there's an argument to be made that volatility should be higher. Traders use a rule of thumb that 16 points for the VIX equate to a 1% move in the index.

The S&P 500 was headed for its worst decline since April 2025.

"My data says to de-risk, but my gut says this is a blowout that could create a dip-buying opportunity" said Noel Smith, "I do not see macro stuff causing this, like oil or rates. Those things are relatively unchanged. The tape looks like a liquidation and that's exactly what it is according to what I'm hearing with regards to a hedge fund."

Looking at Wednesday's response to the selloff in semiconductors adds a wrinkle for traders that pointed to a too-calm VIX despite Wednesday's tech-stock wreck. With the VanEck Semiconductor ETF down 3%, it was the first day in market history that the VIX concurrently fell more than 4 points, according to data from the X account "OddStats." As of midday, nearly 800,000 calls traded on the VIX, 3.5 times higher than 30-day average, according to Cboe data.

#2892. Trump's energy deals are about to face a wave of investigations by Democrats

President Donald Trump is expected to face a wave of investigations when Democrats take control of Congress in January. His sweeping deal-making in the energy sector is likely to become a central focus.

Democrats have pressed the White House for months on the legality of its intervention in Venezuela's oil sector and its cancellation of renewable energy projects funded by the Biden administration. They have raised conflict of interest concerns over numerous deals in the minerals sector.

But, until now, the opposition party could do little more than send strongly worded letters to administration officials. When the next Congress convenes in January, the Democrats will wield the subpoena power that comes with committee control, giving them the authority to compel testimony and the disclosure of documents.

The House Energy and Commerce Committee in the next Congress intends to pursue an aggressive oversight agenda to root out corruption, Rep. Frank Pallone, the New Jersey Democrat who will likely chair the panel, said in a statement.

With comfortable majorities in the House and Senate, Democrats will likely pass a war powers resolution that demands an end to the conflict, which has hiked fuel prices in the U.S. and around the world.

Crude oil prices sold off sharply Wednesday as the market expected the election defeat to push Trump to cut a deal with Iran. Vice President JD Vance blamed the Republican loss directly on the war, raising questions about whether there is support inside the administration to continue the conflict.

House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, who will likely take over the House speaker's gavel in January, said Wednesday that Democrats will "end this reckless and costly war of choice that has skyrocketed gas prices all across the country."

The new Congress will try to rein in the administration by passing legislation to stop the conflict, said Rep. Ted Lieu, vice chairman of the Democratic caucus. Jeffries has said Democrats will pass a war powers resolution, though Trump will almost certainly wield his veto. It is unclear whether Democrats will try to restrict funding for the war as well.

Nevertheless, tumbling crude prices could push gasoline below $4 per gallon in the next week and diesel under $6 by Thanksgiving, said Patrick De Haan, head of petroleum analysis at GasBuddy, in a social media post Wednesday.

But the odds still favor military escalation in the Middle East, warned Bob McNally, president of Rapidan Energy and a former energy advisor to President George W. Bush.

"Iran is hardened in its uncompromising position," McNally said. "Trump, having now paid a huge price for his determination to prevent Iran from obtaining a nuclear weapon, is unlikely to let go of this legacy goal and walk away."

Democratic lawmakers have pressed the Trump administration for months to provide Congress with an accounting of how Venezuela's oil revenues have been spent — to no avail.

Washington seized control of Venezuela's oil exports in January after ousting former President Nicolás Maduro in a military raid. The Pentagon took a stake in an obscure oil company, North American Blue Energy Partners, or NABEP, that is supposed to develop Venezuelan oil reserves. The deal supposedly gives the U.S. control over 65 billion barrels of oil.

Democratic House members warned the oil industry in January that entering into an agreement with the U.S. on Venezuelan oil could expose the industry to "legal risk, including civil liability from creditors who hold nearly $200 billion worth of debt to Venezuela and their various state-owned entities."

Democratic senators subsequently sent a letter in September to Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth and Energy Secretary Chris Wright questioning the legal basis for the U.S. government's deal with NABEP.

Energy analysts warned when the NABEP deal was announced in August that it might not survive if Democrats take power in Washington.

Democrats have also raised conflict of interest concerns over the Trump administration's series of deals in the critical minerals sector.

The White House has justified the deals, often involving government equity stakes in private companies, as necessary to support a segment of the mining industry that's critical to U.S. national security and that has been undercut by competitors in China.

But Democratic senators have pointed to the involvement of Cantor Fitzgerald in a number of those deals as cause for concern. Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick ran Cantor before joining the Trump administration. He personally divested from Cantor after joining the Cabinet and turned over control to his sons.

The Democratic senators demanded in a July letter that the federal government preserve any records related to deals involving 14 companies.

For his part, the Commerce secretary dismissed the allegations. "If the Democrats send me letters, I will respond to the letters but they don't matter, since when you do nothing wrong, you don't worry about it and you don't care," Lutnick told CNBC in a September interview."You just answer their silly questions and you move on," Lutnick said. "This is a waste of time trying to create something that doesn't exist."

Others are less cavalier. USA Rare Earth, one of the companies named in the senators' letter, has warned investors that its deal making the U.S. government one of its stockholders has few precedents.

As a consequence, the company could be subject to litigation, political scrutiny and a change in federal priorities, it said in a March filing with the Securities and Exchange Commission.

The Trump administration has favored fossil fuels and waged a relentless campaign against renewable energy, particularly wind power, since taking office.

Democrats are unlikely to restore tax credits phased out by Trump's big tax bill due to the president's veto power. But they are certain to press the administration on the nearly $8 billion of energy projects it has canceled in states that supported Vice President Kamala Harris in the 2024 presidential election.

Energy Secretary Chris Wright has told Congress that the projects were not canceled based on political considerations. Several House Democrats have accused Wright of giving false testimony, an allegation the Energy Department has denied.

Democrats are also scrutinizing the administration's efforts to kill offshore wind power in the U.S. The Interior Department has tried to buy out leases for several such projects, for example, paying TotalEnergies nearly $1 billion to cancel an offshore wind project and stop investing in any similar projects in the U.S. in the future. Total agreed to invest in natural gas instead.

Reps. Jared Huffman and Jamie Raskin, the ranking Democrats on the natural resources and judiciary committees, respectively, called the TotalEnergies deal unlawful and launched an investigation in April.

#2891. LeBron James is having fun in Philly and one fan's wine can prove it

LeBron James is having fun in Philly and one fan's wine can prove it originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

LeBron James is one of kind in so many ways. What he did Monday night might be the cherry on top.

In the third quarter of the Sixers' 126-107 victory over the Bulls, James decided he'd take fan interaction to a whole new level.

James looked for a transition pass from Tyrese Maxey, who snagged a rebound. James was behind the pass and his momentum took him into the stands, where he disappeared for a few moments.

It wasn't until another angle was shown on replay that we could see what actually went down in the darkness of the seats. James didn't hold back giving reporters a play-by-play.

"I spilled some of his wine," he said postgame. "I crashed into him, spilled some of his wine. So I wanted to see if it was worth it on my arm and how good it was. It was alright."

Troy Clemons – the Sixers fan whose drink spilled – explained his perspective on the interaction with James to Kris Rhim of ESPN.

"[I was] walking to my seat … LeBron crashed into me, he grabbed me, he said 'My bad,' my drink spilled everywhere. He took my drink from me and he basically just took a sip of it, gave it back and ran back on the [court]."

"At the end of the day he's another man like me," Clemons said. "I mean he's LeBron, but at the end of the day, I threw that cup in the trash. And it's nothing up with him, I just got a fresh drink though."

It should be noted that players are not supposed to drink from fans' cups, but it's rare for the NBA to fine someone for a spontaneous interaction such as this. In 2019, a similar thing happened when Mike Scott tumbled into the Bucks' court-side seats. The Sixers star jokingly took a sip of a fan's beverage and the NBA opted not to fine him. Time will tell if James has the same fate.

If nothing else, an interaction like this just shows how much fun James seems to be having.

The 24-year veteran noted the importance of finding joy in the game as he grinds through the season.

"It's a long marathon. And if you don't find ways to have fun throughout it, even though you're working and even though you're locked in, then it could wear on you. So for me, especially at this point in my career, being able to just appreciate the moments, the small moments, these fans are great to us. We try to be great to them as well. By playing the game the right way, playing the game hard and then also just being showmen as well, it was a great game for us."

Head coach Nick Nurse also weighed in on the 41-year-old's mindset coming into the season.

"This summer I think he probably stated publicly that he wanted to go some place and have some fun playing. He loves the game, [it's his] 24th year. It's a group of guys, like I keep saying, that have been super focused in the intent of playing the right way. I think he's a big part of that."

The vibes are high. It's difficult for them not to be.

With Monday's win against the Bulls, the Sixers are 7-1 and lead the Eastern Conference. They've won four in a row and the only team they've lost to is the Pistons. The Sixers will face them on Wednesday to start a three-game road trip.

#2889. IRS announces new federal income tax brackets for 2027

The IRS has announced new federal income tax brackets and standard deductions for 2027.

In its announcement Thursday, the agency raised the income thresholds for each bracket, which apply to tax year 2027 for returns filed in 2028.

The IRS also increased figures for other key provisions, including long-term capital gains brackets, the estate and gift tax exemption, and eligibility for the earned income tax credit, among others.

For 2027, the top rate of 37% applies to individuals with taxable income above $661,375 and married couples filing jointly earning $793,650 or more.

Here are the federal income tax brackets for 2027:

Federal income tax brackets show how much you owe on each part of your taxable income, which you calculate by subtracting the greater of the standard or itemized deductions from your adjusted gross income.

The standard deduction will also increase in 2027, rising to $33,200 for married couples filing jointly, up from $32,200 in 2026. Starting in 2027, single filers can claim $16,600, a bump up from $16,100.

The new tax brackets come as Americans wrestle with affordability issues amid the rising cost of housing, groceries, gasoline and other living expenses.

The consumer price index climbed 3.7% in September 2026 from one year earlier, up from a yearly pace of 3.4% in August, the Bureau of Labor Statistics reported in October.

The U.S. federal income tax system is progressive, which means tax rates increase as earnings rise.

For 2027, the federal brackets include seven income tiers, with rates ranging from 10% to 37%. The tax rates apply to each band of income rather than a flat percentage for all earnings.

The IRS adjusts the income ranges for each tax rate yearly based on the consumer price index.

Your federal tax brackets depend on your filing status, which you choose on your tax return. The options include single, married filing jointly, married filing separately, head of household or qualifying surviving spouse.

You can find your 2025 taxable income on line 15 of your individual federal income tax return, known as Form 1040.
