#2888. 10 takeaways from the Celtics’ wild win over the Bucks

I had already started thinking about what a comfortable Celtics win over the Bucks might tell us about this team when Milwaukee decided to rewrite that narrative.

Boston led by as many as 22 points Wednesday night, held the Bucks to 14 in the first quarter and went into halftime up 62-41. Considering Milwaukee entered the game at 1-6, and the Celtics were playing without Jayson Tatum for the first time this season, things were looking awesome.

Then Tyler Herro caught fire, Boston's offense went missing and the Bucks somehow found themselves leading in the fourth quarter. Payton Pritchard had also left with an eye injury, leaving the Celtics without two of their most important offensive players.

Thankfully, Derrick White remembered how to score, Paul George continued being wonderful and a few Celtics made the kind of plays that deserve their own place in the box score.

Boston survived 109-102, improving to 6-2 on the season. Here are ten takeaways from a game that was significantly more stressful than it needed to be.

As noted NBA insider Jerry Seinfeld once observed, anyone can take a reservation. It's holding the reservation that's really the important part.

The same logic apparently applies to 20-point leads in Boston.

Against Chicago last Friday, the Celtics led by as many as 32 before winning by 14. Wednesday against Milwaukee, Boston was up 21 at halftime, had led by 22 at its peak and looked well on its way to a stress-free evening. By the middle of the fourth quarter, the Celtics were losing.

Now, the circumstances matter. Boston was missing Tatum, lost Pritchard during the second half and had to figure out some unproven lineup combinations on the fly. Milwaukee also shot the lights out in the third quarter, with Herro doing most of the damage.

Still, the Celtics helped create their own problems. Milwaukee scored 38 points in the third after managing just 41 in the entire first half. Boston's offense became stagnant, turnovers gave the Bucks opportunities to run and suddenly a team that had looked completely overmatched was playing with all the confidence in the world.

Joe Mazzulla pointed to the offensive struggles as a major part of the defensive problem afterward. Boston wasn't executing well enough to put consistent pressure on Milwaukee, and the Bucks took advantage.

Now, to Joe's credit, the Celtics are 139-5 when leading by at least 20 points under his watch. That's a 96.5 percent winning percentage, for anyone keeping track. But after watching Philadelphia erase significant deficits during last year's playoffs, and now seeing these early-season scares, I understand why Celtics fans aren't particularly comforted by a 20-point advantage.

The good news is that Boston found a way to win anyway. I'd just appreciate fewer opportunities to practice that particular skill.

For the first time this season, the Celtics had to figure out what their offense looked like without Jayson Tatum. It was a useful test, especially considering how much of Boston's early success has come from his scoring and playmaking.

Hugo González moved into the starting lineup alongside Derrick White, Sam Hauser (his first start as well), Paul George and Neemias Queta. George took on more offensive responsibility, and Joe Mazzulla adjusted his substitution pattern to accommodate the change.

One of the more interesting decisions was George playing the entire first quarter. He returned midway through the second, then had his minutes managed differently after halftime so he could play the entire fourth.

Mazzulla explained afterward that he wanted George on the floor to close, particularly after some of Boston's third-quarter lineups became stagnant. And that was before Pritchard's injury complicated things further.

The absence of both Tatum and Pritchard became obvious when Milwaukee began mounting its comeback. Boston struggled to create clean looks, and there were stretches when every possession seemed to require George to manufacture something on his own.

But the Celtics also got meaningful contributions from players who might not normally have as much responsibility. Queta made plays with the ball in his hands, White took over late, and Baylor Scheierman helped create second chances when the offense desperately needed them.

Nobody should expect Boston's offense to look equally comfortable without Tatum. The encouraging part was seeing Mazzulla find different ways to get contributions out of the rest of the roster.

I wrote after the Bulls game that Paul George looks like he was meant to be a Celtic. Wednesday versus the Bucks only strengthened that belief.

George finished with 25 points, five assists and four steals, shooting 5-for-9 from three. He scored 10 points in the first quarter, helping Boston establish an early lead, and then spent much of the second half trying to keep the offense alive.

The responsibility looked different without Tatum. George was asked to initiate more possessions, create his own offense and operate as Boston's primary scoring option for long stretches.

For the most part, he looked perfectly comfortable doing it.

There were some mistakes, including six turnovers and a pair of missed free throws in a tie game late in the fourth. But George also hit a massive step-back three with 1:23 remaining to put Boston ahead 107-99 and give the Celtics some desperately needed breathing room.

What continues to impress me is how easily he seems to move between roles. When Tatum plays, George can spend stretches operating without the ball, finding open space and making the extra pass. When Tatum sits, he's more than capable of taking the wheel.

Mazzulla praised George's defense after the game and talked about how important his versatility has become to the team's identity.

"We need the best version of him for us to be a great team," Mazzulla said.

I think we're getting a pretty good look at that version already.

George has been an excellent teammate, a disruptive defender and a much more natural fit than I expected this quickly. If this keeps up, I'm going to run out of new ways to say how much I enjoy watching him play basketball.

The version who looks completely comfortable taking over an offense when the moment calls for it. The guy who doesn't hesitate when the ball finds him late in a possession, and who occasionally seems to decree that he's had enough of the other team scoring.

White finished with 24 points, seven assists, four rebounds and two blocks against Milwaukee. Eleven of those points came in the fourth quarter, when Boston needed them most.

After the Bucks tied the game at 97, White helped settle things down. He converted a crucial and-one after Queta and Scheierman kept a possession alive, then followed with a driving floater that pushed the Celtics' lead to seven.

He also went 9-for-9 from the free-throw line.

The performance was especially encouraging because White's offensive start to the season has been fairly underwhelming. Through eight games, he's averaging 12.8 points and shooting around 29 percent from three.

In the offseason, White talked about improving his outside shooting after last season, and Wednesday's 1-for-7 performance from deep didn't do much to suggest he's found the answer yet. But I've been waiting to see the confident version of White who could become Boston's second offensive option when Tatum or Jaylen Brown missed games in previous seasons.

The Celtics will need more of this version of White if they're going to make a deep playoff run. He doesn't have to score 24 every night, but the willingness to attack and create offense when things get uncomfortable is going to matter.

Hopefully the jumper comes along for the ride.

Think about where Neemias Queta was a few years ago.

In 2023, the Sacramento Kings waived him after two seasons in which he struggled to establish a consistent NBA role. Boston brought him in on a two-way contract, and he spent much of his early Celtics career trying to prove he belonged in the rotation.

Now we're talking about him making critical plays down the stretch of NBA games, getting praised by his coach for his development and playing on a four-year, $56 million contract.

Queta finished Wednesday with nine points, 12 rebounds, three assists and two blocks in 29 minutes. His impact was especially important late, when Boston desperately needed someone besides George or White to make something happen.

With the score tied at 97, Queta scored on a driving layup off a feed from George. A little later, he battled Myles Turner for an offensive rebound after a White miss, helping keep the possession alive before Baylor Scheierman found White cutting for an and-one.

Those are enormous plays in a game that was slipping away.

Mazzulla pointed to Queta's continued improvement after the game, particularly his ability to contribute as a playmaker and deliver defensively in important moments. He also noted that Queta has been showing those improvements consistently since the season opener against Detroit.

It's easy to look at the Celtics' current center rotation and start arguing about minutes and matchups. But I think it's worth occasionally appreciating what Queta has built for himself. Boston's player development program deserves credit, and Queta deserves even more for putting in the work.

The Kings decided they didn't have a place for him. Three years later, the Celtics are counting on him to close games.

I'm starting to think Baylor Scheierman's greatest skill might be convincing basketballs to find him.

He grabbed 11 rebounds against the Bucks. Eleven! From a wing coming off the bench.

Three came on the offensive glass, and he added nine points on 3-for-4 shooting from deep. Boston was plus-11 in his 29 minutes, the best mark among the Celtics' reserves. But the thing that jumps out when watching Baylor is how frequently he seems to be involved in possessions that have already become a complete mess.

A missed shot bounces toward the baseline? Baylor's there. Someone gets a hand on a loose ball? Baylor's already moving down the court. A rebound starts ricocheting between players? There's a decent chance Scheierman ends up with it.

Noa Dalzell made a good observation after the game that some of Baylor's most valuable hustle plays didn't even register as rebounds. He was constantly involved in securing loose balls and keeping possessions alive.

The best example came late in the fourth, when Queta and Turner battled for a rebound and the ball eventually found Scheierman. Baylor immediately located White cutting toward the basket for the and-one that helped Boston retake control.

That's the kind of sequence Joe Mazzulla dreams about.

There's still a crowded group of young wings competing for minutes, and I don't think we've seen enough to declare a permanent rotation hierarchy. Hugo got the start Wednesday, while Baylor came off the bench and played significantly longer.

But Scheierman keeps finding ways to make his presence useful.

I described him recently as a dog who just heard the cheese drawer open. Watching him chase rebounds this year, I'm beginning to think someone has left the drawer open all season.

After many were disappointed with his early performances, I'm happy to report that Mitch gave us considerably less to complain about Wednesday.

Robinson finished with 10 rebounds in just under 20 minutes, including six on the offensive glass. He also had a steal and a block, and Boston was plus-five during his minutes.

He scored only three points on 1-for-8 shooting, so this wasn't exactly a complete offensive performance. But the rebounding looked much closer to what Celtics fans expected when Boston signed him over the summer.

Mazzulla has talked repeatedly about the adjustment Robinson is making to Boston's system. In New York, his offensive responsibilities were more narrowly defined. With the Celtics, he's being asked to screen farther from the basket, handle the ball more and contribute in ways that take him away from his traditional rebounding spots.

After Wednesday's game, Joe said Robinson's rebounding process had been consistent even when the results weren't there.

He also called Robinson one of the best rebounders of all time, which is quite a thing to hear your coach say when you've spent the first two weeks wondering why you aren't grabbing more rebounds.

I'm still curious about Robinson's fit alongside Queta and how Mazzulla plans to divide those minutes. But I thought Wednesday was a meaningful step in the right direction. This looked much more like the player Boston was hoping to get. Now the question is whether he can make it a regular occurrence.

There's a faint rumbling coming from somewhere beneath TD Garden. Or is it above? My coffee is trembling on the table. A few pigeons just took flight outside my office.

First, I owe Luka Garza an apology. During my offseason analysis of Boston's roster, I identified him as a break-glass-in-case-of-emergency option, assuming most of the meaningful center minutes would belong to Queta and Robinson.

Well, apparently Garza is the reason the other team needs to break the glass. He is the emergency.

Garza scored 11 points against Milwaukee, shooting 3-for-4 from three and grabbing five rebounds in 18 minutes. All three of his made threes came in the first half, when Boston's offense was humming.

He's now shooting 52.4 percent from deep through eight games, including nine made threes across his last three outings. That's an admittedly small sample, but it's becoming impossible to ignore.

Garza's shooting gives Mazzulla something different from his other centers. Defenders have to respect him away from the basket, which creates some fun possibilities when he's sharing the floor with a traditional interior presence.

We saw that against Milwaukee when Mazzulla paired him with Robinson, allowing Garza to operate farther from the basket while Mitch attacked the glass.

I still don't know exactly how many minutes Garza should play when everyone is healthy, or how sustainable this shooting will be over an entire season. But the man is currently shooting better than half his threes, and I feel obligated to take that seriously.

The villagers outside of Boston have been warned. Garzilla is loose.

Payton Pritchard was getting treated like a crash test dummy last night.

First, he took a shot to the face that left his nose bleeding during the first half. Somehow, he kept playing and even managed to score while dealing with the aftermath.

Then, in the third quarter, he suffered another blow to the face and left the game with what the Celtics officially described as a right eye abrasion. He didn't return.

Pritchard finished with 10 points and five assists in 18 minutes, shooting 4-for-7 from the field. He had entered the night dealing with a right shoulder contusion, too, so this was hardly the most relaxing evening for a historically durable player.

Afterward, Mazzulla said Pritchard seemed okay but acknowledged that he didn't know the exact extent of the injury. Boston will have to monitor his status heading into Friday's NBA Cup game against Charlotte.

Before exiting, Pritchard also found time to reprise his role as Smooth PP, the jazz pianist who has recently taken to using opposing defenders as unwilling dance partners.

Kyle Kuzma found himself involved in the latest performance.

The man is apparently capable of improvising an entire musical number while his face is being repeatedly rearranged. All jokes aside, hopefully Pritchard is okay. Boston missed his ballhandling and ability to create offense during the fourth quarter, especially without Tatum available.

One of the more intriguing developments Wednesday was Mazzulla's willingness to experiment with lineups that we haven't seen much of this season.

The Robinson-Garza pairing was particularly interesting.

Before the Bucks game, the two had shared the floor for only about two minutes. Against Milwaukee, Mazzulla gave them a longer look, and the early results were encouraging.

Robinson attacked the offensive glass while Garza provided spacing and perimeter shooting. By halftime, Robinson had nine rebounds and Garza had scored 11 points, making all three of his three-point attempts.

The combination made sense against Milwaukee's first-half approach. Boston could control the boards without completely sacrificing the ability to space the floor. But things changed after halftime.

The Bucks began playing faster, spreading the floor with shooters and attacking Boston off the dribble. They shot 7-for-8 from three in the third quarter, and suddenly the Celtics' bigger combinations weren't nearly as comfortable.

Mazzulla acknowledged afterward that the double-big pairing worked better in the first half than the second. Milwaukee's adjustments forced Boston to reconsider its lineups, particularly as the Celtics struggled to generate offense without Tatum and, eventually, Pritchard.

I actually appreciate Joe experimenting this early in the season. There's a lot to learn about this roster, especially with so many new players being asked to take on unfamiliar responsibilities.

BOSTON, MASSACHUSETTS – OCTOBER 30: Joe Mazzulla of the Boston Celtics high-fives Mitchell Robinson #4 during the first half against the Chicago Bulls at the TD Garden on October 30, 2026 in Boston, Massachusetts. NOTE TO USER: User expressly acknowledges and agrees that, by downloading and or using this photograph, User is consenting to the terms and conditions of the Getty Images License Agreement. (Photo by Brian Fluharty/Getty Images) | Getty Images

But Wednesday also showed how quickly a promising lineup can become a problem when an opponent changes its approach.

Boston won the game because its players made the necessary plays down the stretch. White took over, George hit the big three and the Celtics' frontcourt helped secure some enormous possessions.

The win is what matters. But so does understanding why the team needed late-game heroics after leading by 22.

The Celtics are 6-2, and I still think we're only beginning to understand what this version of the team can become. Wednesday offered plenty of reasons to be excited about its depth, even if the road to discovering that depth involved nearly losing to a 1-6 Bucks team.

Maybe next time we can conduct the experiment without involving everyone's blood pressure.

#2887. IRS announces bigger estate and gift tax exemption for 2027

The Internal Revenue Service has announced a higher estate and gift tax exemption for 2027.

In its announcement Thursday, the agency increased the federal "basic exclusion amount" to $15.48 million per person for 2027, up from $15 million in 2026. The exemption applies to tax-free transfers during life and at death.

State estate and inheritance tax exemptions can be lower.

The IRS also increased figures for dozens of other provisions, including federal income tax brackets, the capital gains tax brackets and eligibility for the earned income tax credit, among others.

The inflation adjustments for 2027 come as Americans wrestle with affordability issues amid the rising cost of housing, groceries, gasoline and other living expenses.

President Donald Trump's "big beautiful bill" increased the estate and gift tax exemption to $15 million per person. Without the change from Congress, the exemption would have reverted to 2017 levels — $5 million plus an inflation adjustment — after 2025, which spurred proactive estate planning from ultra-wealthy families before the legislation.

Under current law, few families are subject to federal estate taxes. For 2026, families will file about 6,890 federal estate tax returns, and roughly 3,900 will be taxable, according to estimates from the Tax Policy Center, a think tank.

#2886. Coinbase net income surges 66% in third quarter, benefiting from bitcoin investment gains

Coinbase Global earned third-quarter adjusted net income of $719 million, up 66% from $433 million a year ago, largely due to a recovery in the cryptocurrency market.

Coinbase said it earned $2.51 a share in the latest quarter, although it wasn't immediately clear if that compared with the 6-cent-per-share profit that Wall Street analysts had estimated. Revenue of $1.20 billion trailed the $1.233 billion expected by analysts polled by LSEG.

Net income at the San Francisco-based platform surged thanks to the value of its volatile cryptocurrency investment portfolio, which can swing wildly even when the composition of the underlying holdings are little changed.

The third quarter value of Coinbase's crypto investment portfolio climbed due to a resurgence in the digital asset market. The price of bitcoin — often used as a gauge for all crypto assets — rose 39% in the three-month period ended Sept. 30 and the flagship cryptocurrency recently changed hands at roughly $80,809.

In the third quarter, Coinbase deepened its push into non-crypto products and services, including artificial intelligence integrations for traders, tokenized real-world assets and prediction markets.

In July, VP of product Max Branzburg told CNBC that Coinbase would debut an "everything exchange" spotlighting tokenized stocks and other real-world assets, derivatives trading, prediction markets and early-stage token sales. The trading platform also introduced Coinbase for Agents in early October, supporting users that want to use ChatGPT, Muse or other AI agents to manage their portfolios or research and trade various assets.

Coinbase also expanded its prediction market offerings, making a wider range of event contracts available to users.

In the third quarter, prediction markets revenue grew to more than $200 million on an annual basis, while the number of traders on those markets increased 60% compared with the second quarter.

#2885. Elon Musk's Tesla and SpaceX lose over $420 billion in combined market cap after midterms

SpaceX and Tesla shares declined by about 9% and 11%, respectively, on Thursday afternoon with investors concerned that Elon Musk could face new congressional probes — and his companies stricter regulation — following the U.S. midterm elections.

The declines, which correlated with a broader tech sell-off, brought aggregate market cap losses for SpaceX and Tesla to over $420 billion since Tuesday's close.

Musk's wealth, which had topped $1 trillion after SpaceX's initial public offering in June, is now down below $800 billion, having dropped about $90 billion in the past week, according to Forbes. He is still the richest man in the world.

Musk was the biggest donor to GOP candidates and causes in the midterms, with his financial contributions exceeding $470 million, the Financial Times first reported.

Despite his bolstering the GOP war chest, most of the candidates Musk backed lost their races.

Democrats won control of both the House and Senate. And in Texas, where both Tesla and SpaceX are headquartered, Democrats Sen.-elect James Talarico and Gov.-elect Gina Hinojosa beat longtime Musk allies, state Attorney General Ken Paxton and Gov. Greg Abbott.

Deutsche Bank analysts wrote in a note out on Wednesday, "While policy is naturally hard to predict, we could envision SpaceX facing some incremental headwinds in this type of backdrop."

Gov.-elect Hinojosa "made data centers a major campaign issue," the analysts noted, calling for "a moratorium on new approvals until the legislature passes stronger protections on water use, electricity costs, local control, and incentives," and criticizing tax breaks for large facilities.

Tesla and SpaceX are both building out AI infrastructure in Texas, and are planning to build Terafab, a massive facility, which will include a data center and sprawling semiconductor factory, in Grimes County, Texas.

SpaceX also plans to build out a "supercomputer" at its Starbase, Texas, launch facility and company town, according to company job listings.

Deutsche Bank analysts also wrote that SpaceX's Texas compute capacity could now be at risk of delays. Capital expenditures by the company may need to increase to meet the requirements of tighter, anticipated regulations around data center buildout, the analysts wrote.

Musk and SpaceX have been instructed to preserve records concerning restrictions imposed on a military version of the Starlink satellite internet service in Ukraine, CNN first reported, citing an aide to a Democratic senator. The restrictions hampered the country's ability to defend against attacks by Russia.

Additionally, regulatory scrutiny could block chances for Musk to merge SpaceX and Tesla, an idea he has promoted, while acknowledging shareholders' approval would be required first.

SpaceX and Tesla had lost about $456 billion in market cap by the close of business on Thursday.

#2884. The Pistons go from owning to getting owned in the paint

During a tumultuous playoff run and a prolonged offseason contract negotiation, Detroit Pistons fans were ready to say goodbye to Jalen Duren. Well, Duren has been out for six of eight games, and it turns out that Duren is the most indispensable member of the Pistons aside from Cade Cunningham.

A hamstring strain has knocked Duren out for six straight and counting, and boy, do you feel that every moment of every Pistons game.

Pundits and fans alike had easy diagnoses for what ailed the 60-win team during a disappointing second-round exit last season. Detroit needed a second scorer and much more perimeter shooting to succeed in the postseason. Its second-best player, the All-NBA big man Duren, couldn't effectively operate in a congested, constipated offense when opponents had so much extra time to prepare their defense. That had a trickle-down effect on Cade Cunningham and on offensively limited players the Pistons relied on, like Ausar Thompson, Javonte Green, and Ron Holland.

This offseason, the Pistons seemingly tried and failed to add their second scorer (a planned run at Austin Reaves fizzled, and trade interest in players such as Kyrie Irving and Kevin Durant went nowhere). But they certainly addressed the team's lack of perimeter punch. Detroit added guys such as Isaiah Joe, John Collins, and Taurean Prince. They re-signed midseason acquisition Kevin Huerter. They reformulated their offensive game plan to create more movement, space, and offensive responsibility among their non-Cade players. So far this season, the perimeter offense has hummed. Detroit is a top-10 team in 3-point shooting and almost middle of the pack in attempts despite Isaiah Joe missing most of the season to injury.

Detroit is 6-2, losing to the New York Knicks and on Wednesday night to the East-leading Philadelphia 76ers. Calls after both games from fans and casual watchers immediately turn to the team's lack of that second star as the biggest issue. While that still might be a deal-breaker in the players, I would argue that the actual biggest issue in the season's first eight games is Detroit's collapse in its former ability to absolutely own the paint.

In other words, it's not the lack of a Kyrie or a Durant or a Devin Booker. It's the lack of Duren.

Last season, Detroit led the NBA in points in the paint, mostly behind Duren's elite rim gravity and chemistry with Cade Cunningham and Detroit's other ball handlers. Last season, the Pistons led the NBA with 57.9 points in the paint and were fourth in opponents' paint scoring. They had a scoring differential inside of 14.2 points per game. This season, Detroit ranks just 22nd in the paint at 44.8, down 13.1 points per game.

Rim gravity, it turns out, is just as important as perimeter shooting in creating the space needed to run a successful offense.

Paul Reed is a dynamic scorer inside, and he has some pop to his game. But he isn't a rim runner like Duren. You give it to him on the block, and you let him work his magic. John Collins is opportunistic in transition, but those opportunities are dried up when he is the only big on the floor applying rim pressure, usually trying to duck in from the corners.

Ausar has the ball in his hands more, and it is working to create opportunities from others on the perimeter, but it is taking away a lot of those dunker-spot opportunities and back cuts that Thompson feasted on last season.

The lack of inside pressure is manifesting in other ways. Detroit has gone from a top-5 team in second-chance points to 22nd. They are 28th in offensive rebounding. Last season, they were third.

This team needs Jalen Duren back ASAP. And I think fans need to start reckoning with how crucial he is for this team to succeed.

#2883. Neocloud IREN says $4 billion in annualized revenue being delayed due to customer request

Following a 26% post-election plunge in its stock price, neocloud IREN said in its earnings report on Thursday that over $4 billion in annual recurring revenue projected to land in 2026 has been pushed out to at least January.

IREN said the move, which could extend payment to February, is due to "a customer-requested change and supplier timing."

On the company's earnings call, IREN co-CEO Daniel Roberts said Microsoft is the customer.

"They made a request to reconfigure and upgrade some of the network infrastructure and the purpose of that was really to enable the 200 megawatts of capacity to service these latest-generation frontier models," Roberts said.

Shares of IREN rose in extended trading, but that comes after a 55% drop over the past month.

The company said revenue climbed 66% from the prior quarter to $228 million, beating the $221 million average analyst estimate, according to LSEG. AI Cloud revenue more than doubled to $200 million. IREN gets the rest of its revenue from bitcoin mining, which was its primary business before the company pivoted to AI.

IREN's net loss narrowed to $263 million from $684 million the prior period. Year-ago figures weren't included in the earnings release.

The company is being closely watched by investors as the AI trade shows signs of unwinding, particularly following the Democratic sweep in Tuesday's midterm elections. Concerns are mounting that cash-burning companies reliant on debt are going to have a hard time meeting their obligations as interest rates rise and lenders back out of the market. The Nasdaq had its worst day on Thursday since April 2025.

"Capital markets have become more selective in the last few weeks, and the questions have undoubtedly moved from how big the contract is to whether it can be delivered, financed, and run at a profit," Roberts said on the call.

CoreWeave, which is set to report earnings on Tuesday, has plunged 60% in the past month. Nebius, which reports next Thursday, is down 48% over that stretch.

IREN said revenue backlog increased 22% from the prior period to $20.3 billion, with "approximately two-thirds with investment-grade counterparties." The $4 billion ARR target is fully contracted and funded, the company said.

"There is a disconnect between what we're seeing in public market and what we're seeing at the customer level," Roberts told CNBC's Seema Mody.

Still, Iren said it planed to shell out $15 billion in capital expenditures the rest of this fiscal year, including $9 billion in cash and committed financing, and $6 billion in "target" financing, which is "subject to market conditions."

The neocloud said that prices for its compute continue to rise and that it expanded an existing contract with a leading frontier AI lab. It said it has contracts with Nvidia, Microsoft and 13 other customers.

Roberts said that some data center construction in the coming year would be backed by Nvidia and Microsoft, but about a third would be to companies that are willing to offer large prepayments for the capacity.

"We just had a customer agree to fund 90% of the GPU capex," Roberts said, referring to graphics processing units.

IREN said that community opposition can be addressed by tapping local suppliers, using water efficiently and creating jobs.

"I think whether it's Texas, whether it's Australia, whether it's Spain, everywhere, all we're seeing is people wanting to ensure that data centers pay their own way and do the right thing, and it it feels completely reasonable," Roberts said.

IREN said it deliberately hadn't yet bought all of the GPUs it needs for next year to reduce risk.

—CNBC's Isabel O'Brien contributed to this report.

#2880. Knicks Bulletin: ‘I said it for you, I hope I don’t get in trouble’

New York was so good on Wednesday that the Knicks made me hate them for effectively finishing my 2-hour night plan in 20 minutes.

I'm cool with the 4-1 in the last five, but let's make it a bit more competitive for entertainment's sake, please and thank you (I know I'm in the minority here. Alas)

Here's everything said before and after yesterday's bullfighting.

On the entire Knicks roster contributing to the victory over Chicago:

"I take my hat off to everybody on the roster, because everybody contributed in some way, shape or form."

On the Knicks delivering their most complete performance of the season against Chicago:

"That was as close to a 48-minute game as our group has had yet."

On the Knicks' franchise-record-tying 84-point first half:

"It was a really good half, in terms of doing what we believe, what we should be doing offensively as our staples. Everybody was really good in those areas."

On the Knicks' spacing, ball movement and offensive execution against Chicago:

"I really liked how we started out. Spacing the floor well, touching the paint, and then re-spacing too. Our re-spacing was really quick and really decisive. We had some great, great looks early on. And everybody played well offensively. Our pace was pretty good, it's something we've been talking about. Our offensive rebounding, for shooting a high percentage, was pretty good. Just a lot of good things."

On Brunson finally making the shots he had been missing earlier in the season:

"He's obviously missed some shots but he took the same shots tonight that he's been taking. And, we felt we've gotten some good shots in previous games that just haven't gone down. But the good part about it is, they went down tonight, and we defended. A great game by him as well as everybody else."

On Jalen Brunson's unusual endurance and how the Knicks track player fatigue:

"Our analytics guys [chart] how guys play in two-minute increments. Some guys, after six minutes, their three-point field-goal percentage drops. They also can track their movement — how quickly they're still moving out on the floor, if that slows down or not. Jalen's just seems to go up every two minutes."

On Brunson's toughness and why he must avoid overworking him before the playoffs:

"He's about as durable as anybody I've been around. He's mentally tough, physically tough, and he can push himself to go… But at the end of the day, it's going to impact him, in my opinion, come playoff time, so I have to be conscious of not going to the well too much, especially at this point in the season."

On why he did not let Brunson continue playing after his 22-point first quarter:

"No, because he played 12 straight minutes."

On Brunson's ability to maintain his production over extended playing time:

"Jalen's numbers just seem to go up every two minutes and there's really no time when it's down. So, can he play 12 straight minutes? Yes. Can he play 36-38 minutes? Yes. But, at the end of the day, it's going to impact him, in my opinion, come playoff time. I have to be conscious not to go to the well too much, especially at this point in the season."

On Landry Shamet earning playing time through his performances:

"The tough part about it is I don't remember when that happened last year, but it's more so the reality [that] Landry is playing well. Landry, he played well in the last game. As you guys can see I'll try to go with who I think is playing well. I'll try to find minutes for them . . . Sometimes the other guys are playing good and that's who you rock with to try to get the win at times."

On Tyler Kolek replacing Jose Alvarado as the Knicks' backup point guard:

"I said this before, every position coming off the bench is a competition, all the time. Nobody's minutes are set in stone. … Jose had the first opportunity in our last game. And then I gave Tyler an opportunity in the second half [against the Nets] and Tyler played well. So I'm gonna go with Tyler. And if I think I need to make a change because Tyler's not getting it done in my eyes, then I'll go back to Jose."

On limiting the Knicks' starters' minutes during the first games of the season:

"I don't want to kill these guys because they played more games — if you're talking about last season — than most of them had in their career. And the offseason was a lot shorter than what it's usually been. And it takes a toll. Playing (19) playoff games, it's a lot different than playing (19) regular-season games. It might be a game-and-a-half just because of how physical the games are and the mental stress that goes into it, the attention to detail in each individual possession."

On the physical and mental toll of the Knicks' championship season and balancing rest with winning:

"It can be a mental and physical toll that can impact everybody differently. I have to be careful of not overreacting and trying to lay the hammer, especially early, on every little thing. I got to let our guys breathe a little bit and get their feet underneath them, not just from a physical standpoint, but a mental standpoint as well, because we played a lot of basketball last year and we're right back to it. I also need to be careful in making sure that I'm not overextending all of our guys early. I wanted to use a 10-man rotation. There were a couple of games I think I went to nine.

"I hate saying this — I want to win, I'm going to try to win, but I'm not going to do that to the detriment of our group later on. So, I've got to find the balance of how to use my bench a little better to give these guys some more needed rest during the course in games, so they're not at 35, 36, 37 minutes right now. I thought that helped us out a lot last year. We'll up their minutes as the year goes along. I haven't been too good with that so far this year."

On adjusting the rotation after the Knicks' slow start:

"We experimented with different stuff and our players not getting it done caused us to start off a little slow. So, to get us on track, we're going to hold the fort down with a veteran group of guys."

On managing the Knicks' practices and playing time:

"I have to be conscious of how we practice. And I have to try to be conscious of guys' minutes."

On calling out his players for losing track of the game at times:

"We hit a three, we celebrated the three and they got a layup on the other end… that's one of the little things that we talk about embracing playing with next play speed, playing the right way no matter what the score is… (by not doing that), that's how you build bad habits."

On Dalton Knecht's offensive versatility and defensive development:

"He's a scorer. He's not just a shooter. A lot of people think he's a shooter because he's white. I said it for you, I hope I don't get in trouble. He can go get it. He can shoot from range, he's got a medium game, he can get to the rim, and he can pass. He's got to work defensively and I thought tonight, he tried to play within himself and contributed the best way he could.

"There were a couple things defensively that he can do a little better and that's what he really has to concentrate on but I thought he gave us a nice little lift, especially offensively when he played towards the end of the first half."

On the Knicks' improvement despite their early-season inconsistency:

"You want it, but you can't live and die by trying to have everything be great and-or perfect in a matter of a week or two weeks. We know we can play better. The crazy part about it is we're 3-1 in our last four. Knowing that we're hungry for more, and that strive for perfection is going to help us out when it really counts at the end of the day.

"So we just can't let go of the rope, we have to keep trying to get better on both sides of the ball and make sure that we're embracing the little things so that we can hit a groove so we'll be where we want to be at the end of the season — but knowing that we're going to go through ups and downs throughout the course of the year."

On giving starters more opportunities to overcome their struggles than bench players:

"Our starters are going to get a lot more rope than the guy's coming off the bench, you know? And that's kind of how I look at it. Again, this is a process and I have to always be careful of not trying to want [perfection]. You want it, but you can't live and die by trying to have everything be great and/or perfect in a matter of a week or two weeks."

On finding his rhythm and creating offense through defense:

"Obviously, making 3s, but I think making our two-pointers are really important as well. When you have it going, it feels good. Just trying to play off that and use your gravity to make plays. I feel like it really started on the defensive side of the ball, we were able to get stops and get out in transition."

On using his scoring gravity to get teammates involved:

"When you have it going, it feels good. And then just trying to play off that and using my gravity to get teammates involved."

On whether he would ask Mike Brown to leave him in a game to pursue an exceptional scoring performance:

"Possibly, but it has to be the right moment, right time. I don't want to say no because you never know when you're in this situation, but I just want to respect the game as much as possible."

On the Knicks' defensive effort setting up their offensive breakout against Chicago:

"I feel like it started on the defensive side of the ball. We were able to get stops and get out in transition, create a comfortable lead, and I think we played well with the lead for the most part."

On the Knicks' familiarity with their Eastern Conference rivals and the advantage of roster continuity:

"I think everyone in the East knows each other. This is our third year now with each other, so you know, it's rare. That's one of our advantages."

On comparing the current Knicks to LeBron James and the Miami Heat's Big Three era:

"At the end of the day, people knew, when LeBron and the Big Three were there, you had to go out there and beat them. Everyone brings their best against the New York Knicks. You've got to protect the jersey, the city at all costs."

On handling the distractions and pressure of playing in New York:

"I mean, we did it all last year. You're in New York, you've got to play through distractions; that's what comes with playing here. There's a lot of pressure, a lot of noise."

On the Knicks' ability to accomplish something special by staying united:

"But as long as this locker room, which has shown it can do, stays together and continues to just believe and fall into each other when things get tough, we can do something special."

On how the Knicks grew closer during their difficult stretch after winning the NBA Cup:

"We didn't look our best version, and we heard about it. This team continued to fall into each other instead of splitting away and … separating. We got closer and closer."

On the pressure of playing in New York and how quickly fans' expectations can change:

"I guarantee you, I'm going to be sitting here rock bottom. I don't think I'm ever going to be on my high horse, either. We're in New York. Everything rides on the next game. We go on a 10-game winning streak and the next game we lose, they're ready to burn the place down. We know what it is. It's the tough part of being in New York."

On the Knicks' short offseason and the physical toll of the championship celebrations:

"We gotta go out there and win games. And we gotta get those championship habits back, because we were away from it for I don't know how many months. I think it's a tough spot. It's kinda like a little conundrum, because obviously we played into mid-June. Then in the summer obviously you're working out, you're doing that, but you also are enjoying the fruits of the labor of winning in terms of going to brands, going to appearances. Off the court, it's kind of booming for you. So, you're doing all of that, so that takes a little bit of a wear on you. And then you get into it. So sometimes, the body has to kinda catch up."

On the Knicks' open communication and willingness to have difficult conversations:

"It's open. Anybody can step up, whether it's the [bench] guys or starters. That's the thing about this team, our chemistry is so good and we have a lot of trust because we see each other putting forth a lot of work."

On accepting a reduced role and contributing energy even when he isn't playing:

"[Brown] knows I'm always ready. My teammates know that I'm always ready and am going to try to bring the energy. If that's not on the stat sheet or paper, just trying to do the little things, that's something that I've grown in since I've been here. That really took priority for me."

On being ready when the Knicks activated him for his debut against Chicago:

"I wasn't surprised, I just came in ready to go. The coaches said you're active so that just means be ready."

On his first Knicks points coming on a putback dunk and making his debut:

"It was great. Honestly I didn't hear much just because I'm so locked in, but it was good. Pretty much my first play was a putback dunk so it's cool, it's great."

On wanting to contribute as a scorer and defender:

"I think I just go out there and impact the game on all three levels. And I just wanna show on the other side of the ball, getting the ball pressure and stuff like that and going in and grabbing defensive rebounds."

On staying prepared for opportunities and learning from teammates on the bench:

"Be ready so you don't have to get ready. Just watching on the bench and talking to my teammates is really helpful and it was easy to get in there and play confident. All the guys out there gave me a good look."

#2879. Nvidia's Huang is asked to brief Congress on reported Chinese spy relationship

Three House Republican committee leaders asked Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang to sit for a closed-door briefing on his relationship with a tech executive reportedly identified as a Chinese intelligence asset.

"Recent reports raise significant national security concerns regarding the PRC's access to sensitive U.S. technology and information," the GOP lawmakers wrote in a letter to Huang unveiled Thursday, using the acronym for the People's Republic of China.

They are seeking information on Huang's interactions with Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, as well as Nvidia's "security protocols against foreign adversary espionage," they wrote in the brief message, which is dated Wednesday.

The letter does not specify which reports it is referring to. But The New York Times reported last week that U.S. intelligence found Alice Huang, a Chinese citizen determined to be funneling information to Beijing, had "developed a close relationship" with Jensen Huang.

President Donald Trump, who himself has forged close ties with Jensen Huang as he champions the U.S. artificial intelligence industry, was briefed about a year ago on those intelligence findings, the Times reported.

Trump "chose not to do anything with the information," according to the newspaper, which cited interviews with people briefed on the surveillance and other sources.

The letter to Huang was signed by House Foreign Affairs Committee Chairman Brian Mast, R-Fla., Select Committee on China Chairman John Moolenaar, R-Mich., and House Permanent Select Committee on Intelligence Chairman Rick Crawford, R-Ark.

Nvidia did not directly respond when asked by CNBC to comment on the letter and confirm whether Huang would attend the lawmakers' proposed briefing.

Nvidia instead provided a statement that largely mirrored the one it previously sent the Times, in which a spokesperson suggested Jensen Huang's interactions with Alice Huang were no different from his meetings with other executive clients.

"Our regional cloud customers receive ordinary business information — not sensitive NVIDIA trade secrets—and zero information relevant to national security," the spokesperson said in the statement.

"Meetings with customers are part of a CEO's job; Jensen talks with thousands of customer executives every year," the spokesperson said. "We're confident that if the U.S. government believes that any person is a threat to American industry, it would let us know."

A spokeswoman for Mast did not immediately respond to CNBC's request for additional information. Megaspeed did not immediately respond to an email seeking comment.

The lawmakers invited the Nvidia CEO to appear for an in-person joint briefing on Nov. 18. Nvidia is scheduled to report its third-quarter earnings on Nov. 17.

"China's access to that sensitive U.S. tech is core to the investigative work of our three committees this Congress," the committee leaders wrote. "It is vital that Congress determines the extent to which U.S. national security has been placed at risk."

#2878. $6.3 billion Binance hack is largest crypto theft on record

Binance said Friday that a security incident impacted about $6.3 billion in client assets, with around $3 billion frozen on-chain, marking the largest crypto hack on record.

The company later added it began recovering assets impacted by the breach after pledging to cover losses using its Secure Asset Fund for Users, which the company created in 2018 as a protection for clients in the event of a hack.

It wasn't clear how the attack took place. The world's largest crypto exchange initially said the hack may involve "a compromised DevOps engineer's laptop, which allowed an attacker to gain access to a service container and submit unauthorized withdrawal transactions." However, Co-CEO Richard Teng later said the attack involved a more complex process rather than the misuse of a single engineer's permissions.

The FBI said Friday it was aware of the incident and was in touch with "relevant public and private partners to understand the scope of the activity, evaluate ​potential impacts, ​and ⁠coordinate appropriate investigative steps."

It wasn't clear either who was responsible for the breach. But TRM Labs, a blockchain intelligence and AI investigations company, pointed to North Korean hackers as the potential culprits. "Several features of this attack match earlier North Korea-linked heists, including the Bybit hack and the KelpDAO hack in April 2026: small Tornado Cash withdrawals funding an operator wallet, staging that began well before the theft, and signatures obtained in advance then executed in one burst," the company said in a post.

Blockchain data platform Chainalysis also said this appears "be the work of North Korean cybercrime group TraderTraitor. This group has repeatedly targeted the cryptocurrency industry, exchanges, DeFi protocols, and investors, to raise funds for the regime. We base our intelligence assessment on on-chain activity observed in the hours following the hack."

Binance temporarily suspended withdrawals while it overhauls its withdrawal security systems and has revoked the affected address authorizations, Teng said. The exchange is also working with law enforcement and blockchain intelligence firms to trace and recover the stolen funds, while deposits remain unaffected.

Binance co-founder Yi He said deposits remained unaffected and continued to operate normally, while the exchange was working on enhancements to its withdrawal service and related address controls. It expects to begin resuming withdrawal services within the next 48 hours.

"We have found no evidence of any wallet private key leak. We will share a detailed technical post-mortem once the investigation is complete and the facts are fully verified," Binance said.

The announcement followed moves by several crypto firms, including Circle Internet Group, Tether and Ripple, to freeze funds tied to the hack.

Exploits are not uncommon in the crypto space. In 2025, Bybit suffered roughly $1.5 billion in losses due to a hack. More recently, Bitget lost more than $350 million worth of digital assets in a cyber theft incident. Over the past several years, bad actors have also drained billions of dollars in crypto from decentralized exchanges.

This week's breach "proves our exact point in proposing regulations for crypto markets earlier this month," Michael Selig, chairman of the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, said Friday in a CNBC "Squawk on the Street" interview. The U.S. derivatives regulator on Oct. 5 proposed a framework that would effectively put crypto exchanges under federal government oversight — after Congress failed to approve legislation to regulate the asset.

"The real issue here is that these are unregulated offshore exchanges, and they don't have the same sorts of safeguards that we have in our financial markets here in the United States. We want to offer a federal option to these exchanges to come and register with the CFTC, and they would be subject to the same sorts of cyber security safeguards that we have in our markets today," he said.

Bitcoin fell more than 4% to $77,187.60 on Friday as the hack weighed on crypto markets.

Circle CEO Jeremy Allaire said Thursday evening in a social media post that the company had frozen more than 800 million in USD Coin associated with the incident. USDT stablecoin issuer Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino also said in on X that his firm had frozen more than 1.5 billion of its stablecoin that may have been related to a potential breach.

Jack McDonald, Ripple's senior vice president of stablecoins, said it was actively freezing affected funds held in RLUSD, its U.S. dollar-backed stablecoin.

#2876. Fired Baylor HC Dave Aranda in Baton Rouge as LSU reportedly eyes reunion

The LSU Tigers have given up 17 points combined in their last two games, and they are still trying to bring another defensive mind into the building before Saturday's game against the Alabama Crimson Tide. Dave Aranda, who ran LSU's defense from 2016 to 2019 and won a national title there, has been out of work since the Baylor Bears fired him on Oct. 25.

CBS Sports' Matt Zenitz reported Thursday that LSU is working to add Aranda in an "analyst-type role" and that he is already in Baton Rouge. After seven seasons of running a program in Waco, where he finished 40-40, Aranda would go back to spending his days on game plans.

Oct 24, 2026; Lawrence, Kansas, USA; Baylor Bears head coach Dave Aranda reacts during the second half against the Kansas Jayhawks at David Booth Kansas Memorial Stadium. Mandatory Credit: Jay Biggerstaff-Imagn Images

The Tigers are tied for fourth nationally in total defense at 258.1 yards per game and allow 4.27 yards per play under defensive coordinator Blake Baker. LSU has held all six teams it beat to 17 points or fewer. The other two games were road losses, 32-24 to the Ole Miss Rebels and 35-34 to the Kentucky Wildcats, and a 6-2 team chasing a playoff spot has little room left for a third.

Alabama comes first at 3:30 p.m. ET on Saturday, followed by the Texas Longhorns at home and trips to the Tennessee Volunteers and Arkansas Razorbacks. No analyst arriving this week is going to remake the plan for Alabama, but a coach with Aranda's background would have three more game weeks to work with before the regular season ends.

Lane Kiffin has also seen Aranda's work from the other sideline. His offenses scored 49 and 35 in their first two meetings with Aranda's defenses, at the USC Trojans in 2010 and at Alabama in 2015. The next two produced 17 points combined: a 10-0 Alabama win over LSU in 2016 with Kiffin as offensive coordinator, and seven points for his Ole Miss team in a 21-7 Sugar Bowl loss to Baylor to close the 2021 season.LSU is now trying to put the coach behind those defenses on Kiffin's staff.

Oct 24, 2026; Auburn, Alabama, USA; LSU Tigers head coach Lane Kiffin looks on during the second quarter against the Auburn Tigers at Jordan-Hare Stadium. Mandatory Credit: John Reed-Imagn Images

Baker turned down head coaching offers from the Tulane Green Wave and Memphis Tigers last December and stayed on a three-year, $9.3 million contract.

"There's not a better defensive coordinator job, and so let's just make sure that when the time is right, I leave for the right head job," Baker told The Advocate that month.

Baker has said he wants to follow the path Kirby Smart took, going from defensive coordinator to head coach at a power-conference program that can win a national title. With LSU's defense among the five best in the country, more head coaching offers could come in December.

If Baker takes one, LSU will need a new defensive coordinator, and Aranda has already held that job at LSU and won a national title in it. Chris Kiffin, Lane Kiffin's brother, would also have a claim to the job as LSU's co-defensive coordinator and linebackers coach.

#2875. JJ Redick was impressed with the Lakers’ defense after flat start vs. Kings

For the second straight game, head coach JJ Redick praised his team's defensive performance following a sluggish start. In the Los Angeles Lakers' win over the Miami Heat on Monday, Redick complimented their second-half showing, when they limited Miami to 45 points and a 95.7 defensive rating.

On Wednesday, he was pleased with how they responded after the Sacramento Kings raced out to 16 points in the opening 4.5 minutes. Ultimately, the Lakers bounced back for a comfortable 116-103 victory and moved to 7-2 on the young season.

"I think when the game started, we were a little flat. I called an early timeout. They had 16 points. They scored 87 for the last 43:27 seconds or whatever it was [after] I called that timeout," Redick said postgame. "The promising thing on that side of the ball is the effort and the intent to execute, I think, is really high. Second efforts were really good. We had a number of veer switches and peel switches and scrambles, had a number of late shot clock possessions for them, some shot clock violations. We did a lot on that end. I think the activity overall was really good, save for the first four minutes or whatever it was."

Against the Kings, Los Angeles posted a 105.1 defensive rating, per Cleaning the Glass, which marked its third-stingiest effort of the season behind an Oct. 28 win over the Los Angeles Clippers (90.9 defensive rating) and an opening night romp of the Golden State Warriors (96.5).

The Lakers' defensive anchor, Walker Kessler, is pleased with certain aspects of their play but still sees areas to refine as the year progresses, particularly in the open floor.

"We got to clean up some stuff on the defensive end," Kessler said Wednesday. "Our half-court defense, once we get set, is pretty good. Got to do better in transition as a collective."

While the Lakers allow the seventh-fewest points per 100 transition possessions (113.2), they allow the sixth-highest transition frequency (16.9%). Thus far, that inability to slow teams is really bogging them down. Despite being seventh in transition defense and eighth in half-court defense (91.3 points per 100), they're just 16th in overall defensive rating, per Cleaning the Glass.

Despite Ja Morant's absence, they'll face a stiff test in their rematch Friday against the Portland Trail Blazers, which own the league's top-ranked offense and sit top five in both half-court and transition efficiency. Tipoff is scheduled for 7 p.m. inside Crypto.com Arena.

#2874. The October jobs report is out Friday. Here's everything to expect

The U.S. economy isn't expected to have produced much job growth in October, with employers reluctant to hire workers but also not cutting staff amid a shrinking labor force and a growing problem of long-term unemployment.

Economists are looking for a meager nonfarm payrolls gain of 59,000, a slight uptick from the 29,000 jobs produced in September, according to the Dow Jones consensus. That result would continue a pattern of sparse growth.

But that's kind of OK: The forecast also is that the unemployment rate will hold at a very low 4.2% rate, the product of a climate where a stagnant labor force reduces the need for outsized job increases to keep the jobless level steady.

"Long-term, we're not going to need as many jobs added to the economy to have stable employment because we have fewer people who are available to work," said Nicole Bachaud, labor economist at jobs site ZipRecruiter. "An aging workforce is pushing a lot of people into retirement. We have had decades of low birth rates, meaning we don't have a lot of people who are coming in to replace those retiring workers. And immigration is now down pretty significantly, which is reducing some of that prime-age employment."

Evaluating the health of the labor market in such a climate becomes a moving target, Bachaud said.

More clues will come Friday when the Bureau of Labor Statistics releases the October nonfarm payrolls report at 8:30 a.m. ET.

"Do we have an economy where we have a lot of productivity but not as many jobs? Do we start focusing on things like wage growth? Is that more of a signal, especially for lower-wage workers?" she said. "I think that's the situation we're in right now because we're not going to go back to 200,000-plus jobs being added a month. That's just not the the scenario that that we're sitting in."

Indeed, payroll growth from January 2025 through September 2026 averaged just 35,000. There has been just one month during that period when gains topped 200,000 and seven months have seen losses.

Yet the unemployment rate has barely budged, up just 0.2 percentage point despite the marked slowdown.

During the period, though, the labor force has contracted by 434,000 and is down by just shy of a million since September 2025. The share of the labor force either working or looking for employment, known as the labor force participation rate, also has pulled back sharply, down to 61.8%, or a 0.8 percentage point slide.

But while hiring has been weak, other aspects show strength.

The level of layoffs has been meager, with initial jobless claims sliding to 189,000 last week, the lowest since July and hovering around the lowest level since 1969. Job openings also picked up by 333,000 in September, while the level of workers quitting their jobs — a proxy for confidence in being able to find new work — hit 3.36 million, its highest level in a year and a half.

Conversely, long-term unemployment has been a problem. The total number of workers out of a job for more than 25 weeks is up by 495,000 since January 2025, while the average duration of unemployment is at 24.8 weeks, an increase of 2.8 weeks, according to BLS data through September.

Federal Reserve officials generally consider the labor market as solid, but questions remain.

"If you have a job, you're fine, right?" ZipRecruiter's Bachaud said. "There's not a lot of movement happening, but at least you're employed. For people who are out of work because of the slowdown in turnover, people aren't vacating their jobs, employers aren't expanding and adding new jobs. That makes it very difficult to kind of break back in."

#2873. Eagle River boys, girls swim teams are primed to achieve lofty goals at state meet

Nov. 5—For the past two years, the Eagle River High School girls swimming team has been the cream of the crop in Alaska, winning back-to-back state championships.

This year, the Wolves boys team hasn't just been on the rise; it's been the gold standard.

Last weekend, both Wolves squads claimed Region IV team titles. While the girls narrowly edged past Service by nine points, the boys team had nearly 200 more points than runner-up Chugiak, cruising to the first region championship in their program's history.

"The boys are really fired up because of their opportunity to be the first boys swim team to win the state title here at Eagle River High School," head coach Patrick Garrity said.

The 2026 ASAA state swimming championships start Friday and conclude Saturday at Bartlett High School.

"They're very fired up to go out and get the first (state title) for the school and for each other," Garrity said.

Despite hailing from one of the smaller schools in terms of student population, this year's boys team has impressive depth.

"We have a really complete team, and we managed to fill every spot on our team except for two, so we're completely filled up, and a lot of us made state," senior Nate Shockley said. "More than I expected, to be honest."

He is the reigning state champion in the 200 individual medley. But this weekend, he is going for gold in the 500 freestyle and 200 freestyle and is on multiple relay race teams.

"I'm super excited," Shockley said. "I've had a really good four years here. I think finishing it now is going to be a lot of fun, but still a little nerve-wracking."

While state brings a new set of opponents and conditions, he believes that they should be able to achieve their goal.

"I think our boys are doing amazing right now," Shockley said. "We are going in hoping for another victory, but we need to be on our A game going into it."

He opted not to try to defend his state title in the 200 IM because he has the overall team's best interest in mind.

"It's more beneficial for the team, I believe, for me to do the 200 (freestyle) instead of 200 IM," Shockley said. "I think overall, by me doing the 200 (freestyle), we're going to score a little more."

In addition to being deep, this year's boys team is well balanced with a healthy mixture of experience and promising young swimmers who Garrity says are "pushing the pace."

One of the Wolves' up-and-comers got the better of Shockley at regions in the finals of the 500 freestyle. Sophomore Colin Russian came from behind to pull off the upset and finish first by five-hundredths of a second.

"He came out of nowhere," Shockley said. "He came into the season with like, a 4:55 in the 500 (freestyle), and he's just dropped five or seven seconds. He's done great, and then we have Zach (Jacobs), he's just dropped a ton of time on the 100 (butterfly), which is great. ... Overall, the boys team and the girls team, both teams alike, have just improved so much throughout the season."

Eagle River girls set sights on establishing dynasty

Winning back-to-back team championships is no small task and pulling off two successful title defenses can be even more daunting, but the Lady Wolves are battle-tested and determined to three-peat at state.

"They've had to overcome some adversity this year," Garrity said. "They lost a few members and a couple of illnesses that sidelined a couple people. But they are in good spirits, and they got a fighting chance to get that third title."

Senior Lelaina Trembath is the latest torch-bearer for a culture that has been building up over the past decade and was passed on to her from her older sister Sophia.

Sophia graduated last spring and will be tuning in to the finals virtually from the University of Oregon, where she is enrolled as a student.

"They grew into that culture, and it proved to have a similar spirit, and now they're teaching the younger swimmers how to work hard and how to succeed and how to pass it along to the next group," Garrity said.

Lelaina Trembath is the reigning state champion in the girls' 200 freestyle and will also be competing in the 100 breaststroke and two relay races.

"I'm really looking forward to it, especially for the 200, because I've done this event all four years," she said. "It's really special to me, and it's really fun. For the 100 breast, I'm also really excited because it's my first year competing this one at state, and it should be a really tough competition. But that makes it more fun."

Extending Eagle River's recent legacy of excellence on the girls' side means a lot to her.

"It's been really special," Trembath said. "Our team is always like a family, so I'm really happy to be a part of it."

The Eagle River girls won seven of 12 events at regions, including all three relay races, which has their confidence soaring heading into state.

"We're pretty pumped and confident," Trembath said. "We're definitely reaching for first, and we're working as hard as we can to get there."

The Wolves got a small glimpse of some non-CIC competition at the Bartlett/East Invitational, but one team that wasn't there that they're expecting to make waves at state as their top competition is Juneau-Douglas.

"Juneau and Service are probably our top competitors," Trembath said. "(A three-peat) would be a dream, and that's what we're reaching for."

Seeing his swimmers develop as athletes and as people is "probably the most edifying" aspect of being a coach, in Garrity's eyes, and the role he's played in their lives hasn't gone unnoticed or underappreciated.

"He's always asking us what he can do better, and always helping us with what we need specifically, with breaking up sets based on what events we do," Trembath said. "He's really been such a helpful person in my swimming career. So it's been really nice having him."

#2872. Ravens officially place Lamar Jackson, Tyler Huntley on IR with thumb, turf toe injuries

The Baltimore Ravens will officially be without their top two quarterbacks for the near future: The team announced Thursday that it has placed both Lamar Jackson and Tyler Huntley on injured reserve.

The Ravens also activated UFL quarterback Austin Reed to the 53-man roster after signing him earlier this week. Reed will be the backup for undrafted rookie Joe Fagnano, who is set to make his first career start on Thursday against the Jacksonville Jaguars.

Jackson injured his thumb during Sunday's 36-10 loss to the Buffalo Bills on Baltimore's first offensive play. Jackson was making his return from a three-week absence with a high-ankle sprain, and he got injured on his first throw since Week 4 against the Tennessee Titans.

Bills linebacker Greg Rousseau made contact with Jackson's hand while attempting to defend the pass, and Jackson immediately walked off the field and into the Baltimore locker room.

The Ravens quickly ruled him out of the rest of the game with the thumb injury. Head coach Jesse Minter told reporters postgame that Jackson would require surgery on his injured thumb and a stint on injured reserve.

The Ravens did not reveal Sunday the precise nature of Jackson's injury, only that he had injured the thumb on his throwing hand. Asked at the time if the injury was season-ending, Minter said, "We'll see."

Huntley, meanwhile, was diagnosed with a turf toe injury that is reportedly season-ending. Huntley, a career backup, started three games this season while Jackson was out with the ankle injury.

The injury is a critical blow for the Ravens and Jackson, whose recent seasons have been marred by injury. Jackson's a two-time MVP and one of the game's best players when healthy. When Jackson's available, the Ravens are regularly considered Super Bowl contenders.

Still, help is reportedly on the way, with Baltimore reportedly acquiring Jacoby Brissett in a trade with the Arizona Cardinals. The trade will reportedly not be completed until this weekend, meaning that Brissett cannot start for the Ravens on Thursday; he is reportedly expected to be the starter in Week 10.

Both leg and back injuries sidelined Jackson for four games in 2025 and limited his play in others. The result for the Ravens was a disappointing 8-9 campaign that fell short of the playoffs.

Jackson missed five regular games in each of the 2021 and 2022 seasons. Including Sunday's game in which he played just one snap, Jackson has missed a total of 21 games since his rookie campaign in 2018. The 4-4 Ravens now face another season of fighting for a playoff spot with their MVP quarterback on the sideline.

The injury arrives at a critical juncture in Jackson's career as he is on the fourth year of a five-year, $260 million contract. Team owner Steve Bisciotti has expressed his desire to make Jackson a Raven for life, but the two sides reportedly haven't engaged in substantial contract negotiations.

Now those contract talks and decisions will take place in the context of another of Jackson's seasons significantly limited by injury.

#2871. Victor Wembanyama may have scored the most fantasy basketball points we'll see all season Wednesday against Mavericks

Sometimes you look at a Victor Wembanyama stat line and you have to do a double take. After doing just that while observing Wednesday's astonishing 52-point, 24-rebound performance in a win over the Dallas Mavericks, we had to rub our eyes and make sure it was all real.

The San Antonio Spurs' All-Star not only dropped 52 points and 24 rebounds, but also added two assists, two steals and EIGHT blocks for good measure, scoring 110 points in Yahoo High Score fantasy basketball, while putting up even more in points leagues (112.8).

The 52 points vaulted the big man's season average up to 29 points per game, which now ranks fourth in the NBA entering play Thursday.

San Antonio has now won six games in a row after dropping the first two contests of the season. Wemby will look to continue his massive fantasy week with matchups against the Kings and Trail Blazers. For High Score formats, it'll be tough (actually nearly impossible) for Wemby to top that score for managers this week, but those in points and 9-category leagues will welcome the chance at additional production.

#2869. IRS unveils higher capital gains tax brackets for 2027

The IRS has unveiled higher capital gains tax brackets for 2027.

In its announcement Thursday, the agency boosted the taxable income limits for the long-term capital gains brackets, which apply to assets owned for more than one year.

It also increased figures for dozens of other provisions, including federal income tax brackets, the estate and gift tax exemption, and eligibility for the earned income tax credit, among others.

Despite post-election stock market volatility, many long-term investors hold profitable assets after years of market growth.

The new capital gains brackets could impact investors' 2027 plans for sales or rebalancing, which can trigger taxes for brokerage account investments.

Here are the long-term capital gains tax brackets for 2027:

The latest round of IRS inflation adjustments for 2027 come as many Americans wrestle with affordability amid the rising cost of housing, groceries, gasoline and other living expenses.

The capital gains rate you pay — 0%, 15% or 20% — is based on which bracket you fall into based on taxable income.

You calculate taxable income by subtracting the greater of the standard or itemized deductions from your adjusted gross income.

For 2027, the standard deduction will rise to $16,600 for single filers, up from $16,100, and $33,200 for married couples filing jointly, up from $32,200.

Starting in 2027, single filers with taxable income of $51,050 or less and married couples filing jointly with $102,100 or less, will qualify for the 0% long-term capital gains rate.
