#2839. Santoli: AI bull market almost surely needs tech stocks to recover to keep advancing

The road to bull-market glory does not run through Walmart and Berkshire Hathaway and Eli Lilly.

There's been a virtual buying panic in traditionally defensive, high-quality stocks over the past few weeks, a near-equal-and-opposite offset to the selling pressure in semiconductors and AI-build-out spenders. The three stocks above — the only members of the $1 trillion market-cap club not in Big Tech – have collectively added $550 billion in market value in the past month, almost making up for what Alphabet has lost.

The Nasdaq-100 and the S&P 500 Low Volatility ETF (SPLV) are rushing toward one another in a satisfying picture of symmetry that nonetheless reflects an almost comical level of tactical rotation executed by an investment community accustomed to playing one factor against another.

While this to-and-fro has so far protected the S&P 500 as a whole from an appreciable setback for months, it has left it stymied around levels that date back to May — before we got two earnings-reporting seasons showing 30% annual profit growth.

A week ago here, I suggested the AI theme was nearing a make-or-break moment to stage a recovery or risk surrendering its leadership status and destabilizing the broader market. Thus far, this trade has failed to respond to moderate oversold conditions or a run of cosmetically strong hyperscaler quarterly results, further loosening its grip on the benefit of the doubt.

In stressed markets undergoing soul-searching about the long-term bullish thesis, prices often need to travel further to find buyers with conviction while the market action must disappoint dip-buyers to extinguish complacency. Hard to know if we're there, but presumably we're now closer.

The Nasdaq-100 closed Monday right at its 200-day moving average, off almost 11% from its record high. After an enervating failed Monday-morning bounce attempt, the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index finished down another 3.8% and settled under the late-July low that was set as the Situational Awareness hedge fund was finished liquidating.

"Markets did not get the memo that bearish seasonal pressure typically subsides in mid-October and instead ended the month with some of the most acute selling pressure that has been realized during the late-summer/early-fall period," notes JPMorgan technical strategist Jason Hunter. He's advising clients to remain on the defensive "until we see clear evidence of a bottoming process on the charts or until there are clear signs of capitulation."

This is a time-tested playbook, though many a sharp market pullback has ended without what would qualify as unqualified capitulation.

The market action is winning some plaudits for the way non-tech stocks have held up better, many groups benefiting from the "quality" rotation while abandoned consumer cyclicals get a reprieve from easing oil prices and the slide in the 10-year Treasury yield back below 5%.

This gives active managers plenty to do in playing against a highly concentrated and tech-dependent S&P 500. But I'll continue to argue that the bull market built on the AI story will almost surely need the AI story to get back on track to keep advancing. A prolonged period when the equal-weight S&P 500 outperforms the market-cap-weighted version is otherwise known – usually – as a bear market.

The AI-related stocks' weighting in the S&P 500 is off the highs but remains near 45%, according to Morgan Stanley.

As the chart shows, AI has wobbled relative to the rest of the market during this uptrend before reasserting itself.  But there's a trickier element to the current gut check: In prior phases of the buildout, the key limiting bottleneck gave investors something to buy in order to exploit it.

The initial scarcity of GPUs meant to own Nvidia above all else from 2023 through 2024. When cloud capacity, power and cooling were found to be the crucial scarce resources, hyperscaler stocks worked and the AI-adjacent industrials were drastically revalued. Then memory emerged as the chokepoint and Micron and Sandisk went stratospheric.

Now, as Nvidia's Jensen Huang has said, capital is the constraint and that not only leaves no asset to buy, but it raises the cost of capital broadly as the mega-caps go free-cash-flow-negative.

Overlapping feedback loops and scrambled correlations across the markets and the real economy have further sapped investor conviction. Treasury yields no longer move with oil but largely with AI-capex sentiment, with both in retreat lately. AI-stock setbacks also mute inflation momentum and the equity wealth effect, meaning fewer Federal Reserve rate hikes are anticipated now versus a month ago.

This all probably sounds like an elaborate trap, and it has become one for the previously crowded trades. Could this reflect the end game for the AI-exuberance market chapter, which as I keep pointing out peaked around the SpaceX IPO in June? Impossible to say for sure, but if it were, I'm not sure things would look much different to this point.

But I don't want to dismiss the possibility that a lot of the known challenges are largely reflected in prices.

I argued in August that we should assume the valuation peak for this cycle was in. We've now retrenched to a forward P/E of 18.5 on the S&P 500 from 23 a year ago and the Nasdaq-100 at 20x is at the slimmest premium to the S&P 500 valuation in nine years. I'm on board with the idea that the market is telling us companies are now "over-earning," but if nothing else there is less valuation risk now than in recent months.

Sentiment toward AI stocks is in the dumps. Funds' positioning in tech has retreated below neutral. Important trading lows this year have happened near the turn of the month on March 30 and July 29, and the Nasdaq-100's relative-strength reading is exactly as oversold as it was at those turns.

So, stay alert for possible relief bounces that could evolve into something more consequential if indeed the midterm elections pass in a way that allows traders to unclench a bit into the year's home stretch.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "Similar to valuation, sentiment is not a timing tool, but it is getting harder and harder to find any bulls, as a near-perfect storm of tight financial conditions is being met with tech earnings that have gone from bad to worse in the blink of an eye."

-Another good big-picture conversation on the practical business and investment implications of the next phase of AI proliferation on Patrick O'Shaughnessy's Invest Like the Best podcast, this one with investor Cliff Sosin. His comparison of AI infrastructure economics to internet connectivity providers and his argument for how most of the productivity boost will go toward "consumer surplus" rather than corporate profits are particularly interesting.

-For my birthday Monday, my colleague and friend Sara Eisen wondered how I would write a birthday message to myself. I immediately regretted suggesting she ask ChatGPT, which generated this credible and somewhat embarrassing result:

"Another year older, though the longer-term trend remains constructive. Some modest deterioration in the underlying internals, perhaps, but nothing that suggests a decisive breakdown.

"The candles on the cake are approaching historically elevated levels, though valuation concerns have persisted for years without derailing the advance. At this stage, the key question isn't whether I've gotten older — that's largely priced in — but whether the wisdom gains can continue to offset the inevitable compression in energy multiples.

"For now, I'm maintaining a cautiously optimistic outlook, with upside optionality, downside support from family and friends, and no immediate need for Fed intervention. Happy birthday to me. The secular bull market remains intact."

The stampede into perceived defensive stocks has been a crucial driver of Apple shares' massive outperformance over the Nasdaq-100 since the AI-capex agita flared up months ago. Apple heading into Monday's earnings report had outperformed the Nasdaq-100 by nearly 20 percentage points over the prior six months.

Its singular refusal to join the aggressive spending binge to build AI capacity preserved its free cash flow and made it the perfect counterweight to the capex trade, while inflating Apple's valuation to a 15-year high.

But its advantage in free-cash-flow yield has now narrowed, thanks to its stock-price gains and the collapse of peer shares.

#2837. Fiorentina prospect countdown 15-11

Fiorentina sold an unprecedented number of youngsters this summer. Some of them have a path back to the club via buyback clauses but for accounting purposes, they're off the books. Fortunately, the cupboard isn't bare. I'm resurrecting an old feature here and will count down Fiorentina's top U21 prospects who aren't currently in the first team (i.e. Christ Inao Oulaï, Alieu Njie, Víctor Valdepeñas). I've players out on loan whose current clubs could trigger a permanent move because there's no way to know if they'll return to Florence at season's end.

I don't have a rigid methodology here. I'm basing my rankings on what I've seen from these guys, often at youth level or in preseason, and those games don't always translate to the senior game. It's all extrapolation and it's all fluid. Any of these guys could have a good couple games and raise their profile substantially. I'm ignoring anyone under 18 because it's weird to talk about literal children as employees. As a reminder, growth isn't linear et cetera and also these guys are very young and trying to beat massive odds. Be nice.

The Firenze-born midfielder has all the tools: he's got quick feet, a silky touch, and an eye for a pass. He's also not afraid to stick a boot in despite his stature. He's spoken about modeling his game after Barcelona's Gavi, which tells you how he wants to play. That's hard to do at a high level, obviously, and from what I've seen, Atzeni drifts in and out a bit more than that. He's also missed all but 3 games this year as he's dealt with injury.

2026-2027 goals: Be the heartbeat of the Primavera team.

Beyond that: He under contract until 2028 and has a very high ceiling. If he can add some muscle to his frame and some consistency to his game, expect to hear some hype building around him next summer. He's not close to ready for Serie A yet but it's within the range of outcomes that he gets there in a couple years.

The 19-year-old signed his first professional contract back in May and seems to be developing pretty well. Management trusts him enough to call him up for senior games: he's been on the bench 7 times already. As a player, he's a nippy winger who mostly plays on the left, cutting onto his stronger foot, but can play on the right as well. He's shown the requisite quickness, dribbling, and curled finishes to take the next step. He's behind Riccardo Croci in the hierarchy but is probably the second-most likely Primavera player to make his debut some time this season.

2026-2027 goals: Keep developing, build consistency, and take those first professional steps, either in Florence or on loan somewhere.

Beyond that: He's on the right track but has a long way to go before he's a Serie A-level contributor. I'm trying to figure out if he's shone because he has Croci taking the pressure off him or if he's got enough to go consistently go it alone at this level. We probably won't know until that loan move.

The 20-year-old played on the flank during his Primavera days and occasionally farther forward but has settled in as a leftback. He's a 6/10 sort of player in every way, possessing solid physical and technical attributes without standing out in any category. He gets up and down the wing for 90 minutes and right now, that's probably his best quality. He's capable of swinging in a good cross but his end product is unreliable. As a right-footer, I've also liked how comfortable he's been in central positions, providing a direct line to the winger on his side while reinforcing the midfield. He'd seized a starting role with Juve Stabia but has spent the past month sidelined with a muscle issue.

2026-2027 goals: Get healthy and prove that Serie B is the baseline.

Beyond that: I don't think he's got a clear path the Fiorentina squad unless without a massive season, especially given Fabio Paratici's urge to ship out everyone associated with the previous regime. I think his ceiling is probably bottom-half Serie A team and he'll likely spend his career in the second tier but I also think he's got a pretty high floor as a player.

The 19-year-old from Viareggio has spent most of his career in Tommaso Martinelli's shadow but has another chance to seize the spotlight after last year's disappointing effot (8 total appearances across loans to Serie C sides Pontedera and Cosenza). He's back in the third tier and has earned the starting job for the Grifone. Although he's had to pick the ball out of his own net 12 times in 12 games, he's helped take the club to 4th and made enough good stops along the way to earn his first call up to the Italy U20s. I won't pretend to be an expert on goalkeeper evaluation but from what I can tell, he's got great size, decent reflexes, and a willingness to throw himself into the mixer.

2026-2027 goals: Prove that Serie C is at least a step below where he ought to be and start ironing out the typical teenage goalkeeper issues.

Beyond that: He's got a tricky path to the first team as long as Martinelli's around. Consensus is that his ceiling is lower than his fellow Tommaso's but he looks like he's got a very good chance of having a successful career at a good level, although again, I lack any expertise in judging goalkeepers on anything but a surface level.

The 19-year-old Gambian grew up in the Rimini academy before moving to Fiorentina in 2025. He tallied 8 goals and 4 assists in 32 appearances for Daniele Galloppa's boys last term and has inherited more responsibility this time around. He's risen to the occasion thus far, scoring 3 in 8, including one absolute stunner. All the physical talent is there and his technical ability seems to be catching up. Fabio Grosso called him to the bench for the first game of the season so he's right on the cusp.

2026-2027 goals: Score more consistently.

Beyond that: He's signed professional terms and is under club control until 2028. Assuming he gets healthy soon (missed 2 straight games) and maintains some level of goalscoring consistency, his Primavera performances won't tell us much. Physical specimens like Gabriele Gori and Eljon Toci often sparkle at youth level but, lacking the same advantages against grown men, lose their shine very quickly. Jallow could buck that trend but only time will tell.

#2835. He’s back! Vasiliy Lomachenko officially ends retirement, targets undisputed crown

Retirement didn't stick for one of the most enjoyable boxers ever to watch.

Former three-division world champion Vasiliy Lomachenko officially announced his return to professional boxing today (Thurs., Nov. 5, 2026), ending a 17-month retirement.

Lomachenko has entered a multi-year co-promotional partnership with longtime promoter Top Rank through his newly formed company, Loma Power Promotions. The 38-year-old Ukrainian will drop back to Super Featherweight and is expected to return in early 2027.

"I thought I would never step back into the ring again as a boxer," Lomachenko said in a press release. "But our Lord Jesus Christ had other plans for me."

The two-time Olympic gold medalist announced his retirement in June 2025, more than one year after stopping George Kambosos Jr. in the 11th round to capture the vacant International Boxing Federation (IBF) Lightweight championship. Lomachenko walked away with an 18-3 professional record and 12 knockouts.

Now, he's returning to chase the lone major accomplishment missing from his resume: becoming an undisputed champion.

Lomachenko unsuccessfully challenged Devin Haney for the undisputed Lightweight championship in May 2023, losing a controversial unanimous decision (watch highlights). Rather than return to the crowded 135-pound division, Lomachenko will move back down to 130 pounds, where he previously held the World Boxing Organization (WBO) title.

No opponent or exact return date was included in Thursday's official announcement. However, Lomachenko could return as early as Feb. 13, 2027, against undefeated contender Charly Suarez, according to The Ring. A victory could position Lomachenko for a showdown with unified champion O'Shaquie Foster later in the year.

Lomachenko will turn 39 in February and will have been out of competition for nearly three years by the time he returns. Still, his experience is almost unmatched. He compiled a reported 396-1 amateur record, captured two Olympic gold medals and won his first professional world championship in just his third fight.

Loma is officially back. Now, we find out how much magic he has left.

#2833. Raiders owner Mark Davis is "not a fan" of gambling on sports

It’s fitting that the owner of the team headquartered in the nation’s gambling capital doesn’t like gambling on sports.

“I’m not a fan per se of gambling on sports, especially the one-on-ones, the proposition types of betting,” Raiders owner Mark Davis told Mike Ozanian of CNBC. “I think the key thing is the integrity of the game.”

Indeed it is. The integrity of the game — and the perception of integrity.

“People have to believe in the game,” Davis said. “Is it worth the risk? I don’t know. I can’t answer that.”

Back when the NFL hated gambling (before it realized how much money it could make by jumping in bed with the sports books), Commissioner Roger Goodell said this: ‘If gambling is permitted freely on sporting events, normal incidents of the game such as bad snaps, dropped passes, turnovers, penalties, and play calling inevitably will fuel speculation, distrust, and accusations of point-shaving or game-fixing.”

Now that sports gambling and prediction markets premised on sports outcomes have proliferated, there’s a constant undercurrent of suspicion that the fix is in. The NFL can pretend all it wants that it’s not happening, but it is. As long as it’s not impacting the bottom line, the NFL will continue to look the other way.

One big scandal could change that, permanently.

For now, Davis is pragmatic, and candid, about the ensuing tension.

“The revenue that is coming from gambling and those types of entities is large so there has to be a balance between integrity and . . . the good things that it can bring to the league,” Davis said.

The only “good things” are money. Directly from the deals with sports books and the ability of owners to hold up to five percent of the equity in companies that operate sports books. And indirectly from the enhanced ratings that come from viewers who have action on a given game or the players in that game.

The indirect benefits were always there, even when gambling was illegal in all states except the state where Davis’s team plays. They would still be there if the NFL were to resume its hard line against gambling in all forms.

But it’s too late for that. The money is baked into the budgets. And the NFL isn’t about to give up a viable revenue stream with which it has become fully accustomed.

#2832. Fannie Mae, Freddie Mac rally as Ackman presses for conservatorship exit

The midterm elections could be a turning point for the release of Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac from government control, according to billionaire investor Bill Ackman.

Investors may agree with him, if the rally in both stocks is any sign.

The Federal National Mortgage Association, known as Fannie Mae, and Federal Home Loan Mortgage Corporation, or Freddie Mac, have each jumped more than 60% from Oct. 14 to Monday's close. The stocks were both up roughly 3% on Tuesday.

Ackman, the founder of Pershing Square Capital Management, has long pushed for the two entities to be released from federal conservatorship.

"The reason why it's not being touched right now is, you know, midterms," he said in an interview with CNBC's "Squawk Box."

Ackman, who owns both names through Pershing Square, believes Freddie and Fannie have the potential to increase "10-fold from current share prices," he said in his quarterly letter to shareholders, issued on Monday.

Fannie and Freddie buy mortgages from banks and other lenders, and then package them into mortgage-backed securities, known as MBS. Their operations are critical to the housing market, providing liquidity to banks and creating a secondary market for MBS.

The entities were placed into conservatorship in September 2008, during the financial crisis. The stocks were delisted from the New York Stock Exchange in 2010 and have traded over the counter since then.

Ackman wants the conservatorship to end, arguing the entities would be worth multiples of their current valuation if they were freed from government control.

President Donald Trump has supported ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie. But it remains politically and economically contentious.

The entities operate under strict capital threshold requirements and would need to be recapitalized, which could happen through issuing common equity. However, the Treasury Department would need to figure out an exit plan for its roughly $388 billion ownership stake in the entities through preferred securities and warrants.

Congress may also need to play a role, passing legislation to restructure the government's role in mortgage financing and home-loan guarantees.

Nonetheless, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent recently said it could happen before Trump's term ends, telling 2Way's "The Morning Meeting" podcast that the administration will tackle the ownership structure "next year, the year after." He said affordability will be guiding the decision.

One point of controversy is whether ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie would increase the spreads on MBS over Treasuries, effectively making mortgages costlier. Bessent has sought to reassure the market that wouldn't happen.

"Whatever we do is going to be guided by making sure that the spread on [agency] mortgages … over the Treasury does not increase," Bessent said.

Ackman argues that privatizing the GSEs would help insulate them from political meddling. "Fannie and Freddie are these massive, enormously profitable institutions where whichever political party controls them…if they're still in conservatorship, can influence the outcome of elections," he told CNBC.

In one scenario, he explained, Democrats win the White House, conservatorship is still in place, and Democrats push Fannie and Freddie to lower credit standards in order to expand home ownership and affordability.

"If they're going to move more aggressively into borrowers with lower credit quality, for example, to achieve a political objective, that's going to have an impact on trillions of dollars outstanding mortgage securities," Ackman said. "It's going to have an impact on overall credit spreads."

MBS investors want certainty about how the entities will be run, he noted.

"That only gets resolved in a world in which they exit conservatorship, they become publicly traded companies that are owned by institutional and retail investors," Ackman said. "We set the rules. In conservatorship, the president can do anything."

#2831. Ex-Cleveland Guardians catcher Bo Naylor testifies he didn't know gamblers bet on individual pitches

NEW YORK (AP) — Catcher Bo Naylor, a former teammate of Cleveland Guardians pitchers Emmanuel Clase and Luis Ortiz, testified Thursday at their New York sports betting trial that he didn't know gamblers could place bets on individual pitches until the criminal case arose.

Naylor, called as a defense witness out of turn because he is heading to his wedding, made the revelation under cross-examination by Assistant U.S. Attorney James Simmons, who asked if he knew such bets occurred at the time of 2025 baseball game recordings shown to a Brooklyn jury.

"I didn't know that before," he said. "I didn't know that until the situation."

"You didn't know people bet tens of thousands of dollars on pitches?" the prosecutor asked him, though Naylor was stopped from answering when an objection by defense lawyers was sustained.

The testimony reflected how quickly sports-betting possibilities have blossomed in recent years. Legal sports betting has spread to 39 states in a steady expansion since the U.S. Supreme Court cleared the way for it in 2018.

Betting on whether a pitch will be a ball or strike is a form of prop betting, a popular type of wager often focused on individual players — like achieving a certain number of strikeouts in baseball, or points and rebounds in basketball or surpassing a particular passing yardage in football.

Naylor, who was traded by Cleveland to the Milwaukee Brewers in August just weeks before his new team won the World Series, was shown dozens of clips of pitches thrown by Clase and Ortiz in 2025 when he was questioned first by defense lawyers for the pitchers.

The lawyers elicited from Naylor that catchers notify pitchers what pitches they should throw and where they should aim them before each pitch.

Naylor said catchers and pitchers develop a strategy during pregame meetings of how to best pitch to each opponent to get an out based on an analysis of the weak spots in the hitting habits of each batter.

He also acknowledged that pitchers, even the best ones, cannot be sure that each pitch will go where it is aimed.

Clase, a three-time All-Star and two-time American League Reliever of the Year, had "pretty good command" of his pitches, Naylor said.

Clase, 28, and Ortiz, 27, pleaded not guilty to conspiracy charges after they were filed a year ago. Prosecutors said the players accepted several thousand dollars in payments so sports gamblers could place bets on individual pitches, enabling the gamblers to pocket at least $460,000.

Prosecutors said Clase, who had completed the fourth season of a $20 million, five-year contract, began providing information about his pitches to bettors in 2023 and recruited Ortiz into the scheme in 2025. Ortiz earned $782,600 starting for the Guardians during the 2025 season.

Both are free on bail and are on nondisciplinary leave from the club without pay while facing prosecution.

Naylor, who studied Spanish so he could communicate better with Spanish-speaking teammates, acknowledged when he was being questioned by the prosecutor that he was not close friends with Clase or Ortiz.

Simmons also elicited from Naylor that he had frequently seen Clase on his cellular phone in the locker room.

Prosecutors have alleged that Clase sometimes broke Major League Baseball rules by communicating with gamblers during games.

Naylor also acknowledged that pitchers ultimately have the final say as to what pitch gets thrown because pitchers can wave off the catcher's call in favor of a different pitch.

At one point during his testimony, Naylor was asked by Clase's defense attorney, Michael Ferrara, if he had heard that Clase was into cockfighting with people he knew from the Dominican Republic, where he grew up.

Naylor said he had based on "rumors I heard around the clubhouse."

Clase's lawyers have told the jury that the pitcher had friends who shared his enthusiasm for cockfights, which are legal in the Dominican Republic, and for playing dominoes, rather than sports gambling.

#2828. Can the Boston Celtics get the best out of Mitchell Robinson?

Mitchell Robinson hasn't had the best start to life with the Boston Celtics. The veteran big man has been struggling to make an impact on the floor, both in terms of scoring and rebounding. Robinson's screening has also left a lot to be desired, which is a sizeable issue given how Joe Mazzulla's offense is built around a center's ability to create separation for shooters and/or ball-handlers.

The numbers don't look great for Robinson, either. As of November 3, Boston had an offensive rating of 97 with Robinson on the floor, which was good for last place in the NBA. Yet, when he went to the bench, the team's offensive rating skyrocketed to 123.5 points. Net rating tells a similar story, with the Celtics +16 points per 100 possessions better without Robinson patrolling the paint.

Of course, those numbers are built on a tiny sample size. Robinson has played in seven regular-season games for the Celtics so far. It's clear he's adapting to a new offensive system, and it's taking time for him to figure things out. However, Neemias Queta and Luka Garza are both performing at a high level, making Robinson's struggles stand out even more.

In a recent episode of 'The Celtics Chronicle' podcast, hosts Adam Taylor and Tim Sheils discussed Robinson's struggles and whether his addition during the summer could wind up being short-lived, as Brad Stevens continues to search for an impactful big man to compete with Queta.

#2826. NBA Rookie Rankings: Cameron Boozer, Yaxel Lendeborg lead early list of first-year phenoms

We're only a little over two weeks into the NBA regular season. It may seem a bit early to check in on this year's collection of rookies, but if we are being honest … this group of talent is that good.

To me, the early part of the season is all about curiosity. Teams don't have all of their "go-tos" in place, game plans are not quite as firm, rotations are expanded, and teams are figuring out which combinations and schemes work. Why not apply that to these young players trying to find their way?

What feels unique this year is that there is so much new talent being asked to contribute right away on so many different teams. Taking a glimpse this early in the season allows us to see what is working well and to understand the areas of opportunity. Then we can track where growth takes place, where consistency solidifies, and what happens when some of these scouting reports get updated.

I am always one to gently tap the patience button with young players. A slow start can still bring important reps that aid in development down the line. A quick rise can be met with resistance and stagnation.

The best thing you can do as a rookie is showcase a knack for something you bring to the table every single night. The more you do that, the more opportunity you will have to showcase the other things you can do.

Here are my top 10 rookies early in the season:

If you're wondering why the Grizzlies were the league's last undefeated team and matched their best start in franchise history, you can point to Boozer, who last week became the second-youngest player in NBA history to record a triple-double. The versatility of his game, mixed with what the Grizzlies have wanted to do offensively, has been a hand-in-glove fit.

Boozer can be used at both the 4 and the 5. At the 4, his ability to space and keep things flowing allows Memphis to run actions on both sides of the floor. If teams want to switch, Boozer has no issue going right to the post. His ability to win mismatches has been key: patience on his catch, an effort to get to his spot. If help comes, he can pass it out, which allows Memphis to attack tilted defenses. When moved to the 5, his ability to screen, roll, and quickly get to space opens things up. The short-roll passing speaks for itself, but pay attention to his timing and footwork when he screens.

His ability to mix in catch-and-shoot 3s has helped, too, as some of his finishing skills continue to develop. His drives have been aggressive — he's at his best when he delivers a blow with his shoulder — but he will have to balance that with gathering early and finishing over size and length. If these are the building blocks at the start, Boozer is putting himself in a great spot to be impactful all year long.

Many things have jumped out at me about Lendeborg's start, but it's the trust he's earned from Steve Kerr that stands out. After Sunday's game against the Clippers, Kerr said: "We scored 124 points and Yaxel is the main reason that we were high-powered." Lendeborg is attacking in transition, and he's involved in pick-and-roll as both a ball-handler and a screener. There is a trust in his ability to see the floor, but also to attack matchups. The Warriors are running their ever-present post splits through him for him to score. He's confidently taken the keys and gotten off to the races.

The blend offensively has been impressive. The drives have been there: he's unafraid to deliver physicality and get to his spot, showing patience, shiftiness and good footwork, and doing what it takes to turn the corner. The passing has been underrated: once he draws help, the ball is out on time and on target. The 3s have added to the overall package: he's the first rookie in NBA history to have multiple games with six or more made 3s in his first seven games. And these aren't just the catch-and-shoot variety, these are pull-ups over the defense. There's a confidence that tells me these reps will pay off down the line.

From summer league to the preseason to the regular season, ruthless aggression has been the name of the game for Dybantsa. There is a level of force that he plays with, including the way he drives. He will have to work on the balance between pull-ups and initiating contact on drives, but what we've seen is an ability to find a way to get to his spots. You feel him the most in the open court — it's almost as if he is gliding at times — and the result could be a pull-up, a spin, or a finish at the rim.

Being paired with Trae Young and Anthony Davis is a good launching point for Dybantsa, giving him plenty of runway without having to carry a large amount of playmaking responsibility. There have been flashes of what he can be as a playmaker, reading defenses and nailing skip passes. He can get to those types of plays within the flow of the offense off wide pindowns without having to create every single one. I am intrigued to see how he builds on his defensive potential, too. There's a level of physicality and activity he displays when his man is in off-ball actions. He needs that to translate to consistent screen navigation.

Wilson had at least 15 points, 1 steal and 1 block in his first six games, which is tied for the second-longest streak by a rookie to start a career. He plays with consistent and kinetic energy in transition whether he has the ball or not. That's the area where he's felt the most comfortable attacking for himself and his teammates. The mix of his shots will be key going forward: he's shooting 68.4% from the restricted area, but 33% or below in other shot areas. He's been a very willing 3-point shooter (44 of his 63 3PA have come from above the break), and when it's falling, it's dynamic.

He's at his best defensively when he's able to showcase his athleticism and timing. Running through passing lanes to get a steal. Appearing out of nowhere to get a block near the rim. He has a knack for being in help position off-ball and closing the window on a pass that seemed opened. Different matchups will produce different outcomes, but one thing that has not shifted is his will to compete.

It appears Acuff will be a lightning rod for those who enjoy the sport of discourse. My brain wonders whether that should take away from what he does well. How much do we factor in the context in Sacramento? How important is the production? And how do we track the improvement? One thing is for sure: He is an electric scorer. He's the first rookie since Luka Dončić in 2018-19 to have three straight games of 25 points, 5-plus rebounds and 5-plus assists. He's currently a top-10 scorer in the NBA. He also just so happens to be taking as many shots a night as Luka and Shai, and maybe that's where the eyebrows get raised.

Acuff is a tough shot-maker, which by default also makes him a tough shot-taker. The misses are going to feel loud. But is he taking what the defense gives him, or is he aggressively seeing space as an invitation to take a stepback or attempt to turn the corner? What's the balance between him making quick decisions and waiting to come off a screen? His scoring is drawing attention from defenses. The Pelicans worked to get to the level and put two on the ball (which opened up some passes). The Lakers not only put Matisse Thybulle on him but also gave him the switch-and-double treatment. Getting those types of reps early has value.

Flemings has grown during this early stretch of the season by taking reliable qualities (effort, defense, passing) and building on top of them. He plays with poise, composure, and certainty. He has a feel for the game and an understanding of where the ball needs to go and when. That's a great quality for a young guard, and even better for a young guard who has speed.

It rarely feels like he relies on the turbo button. He works to get in space, and works to engage bigs. He's not afraid to take a pull-up or turn the corner, but he's perfectly fine taking what the defense gives him and making the right play out of it. There have even been moments where he has seen a defensive coverage, let them recover, and then attacked the other way. That does not mean it's automatic every time down the court, but that ability, plus his lack of fear, is a positive sign going forward.

Despite the expectations and the early results in Utah, Peterson is doing a very important thing for a rookie: He's figuring it out. A Jazz offense based on flow, tempo and movement felt like one Peterson would immediately add to. The rhythm has been slightly off to start the season. There can be a bit of a learning curve to find the gaps in which to place your talent.

In spite of that, the best moments from Peterson have come when he's decisive. Win, lose or draw, a quick decision has allowed him to make the right reads and make the right plays. Adjusting to ball pressure and physicality has not changed his ability to read defenses. In fact, he's consistently worked to play at his speed, mixing in bursts but trying to have a constant cadence to his play in pick-and-roll. If and when the full array of shot-making returns to form (the 3 has been there), the overall package may feel even stronger.

Energy and effort can sometimes come across as buzzwords. But if you turn on a Denver Nuggets broadcast, you're going to see Brazile make a play. On either end of the floor. A block at the rim, a lob catch, an attack in transition. He's going to do something. He's brought a consistent level of effort to a Nuggets team that has needed it.

He has a knack for cutting from the corner or flashing around the dunker spot that fits right into what Denver wants to do (especially playing with Nikola Jokić). Defensively, his ability to rotate, close out, contain drives and contest has been a bright spot. To put it simply: he's all over the place (in a good way).

Mara's preseason showed once again that the Thunder would have a quality basketball player ready to contribute to their machine. The injuries that have been thrown at the Thunder mean Mara will have to contribute a little more quickly than expected.

Mara's defense has shown both his potential and where he can contribute right now. Having the ability to protect the rim with size is one thing; doing it in different ways is another. He has showcased the ability to rotate from the elbow or wing and be in position. Knowing how and when to execute different pick-and-roll schemes with the Thunder's defenders is another positive. His passing ability, meanwhile, fits right into a team that was working toward more movement and having multiple actions running on both sides. With his size and touch, rolling is a given, but being able to flash into open areas will help.

The biggest thing that has stood out to me from Brown is his moxie. No matter what the box score or scoreboard says, if you watch him, you cannot tell exactly how things are going. He has good speed and good skill, but the fun is watching when he truly understands how to use that speed: when to change speeds to throw a defender off and find an advantage, and when to attack in transition, off handoffs or in pick-and-roll.

The passing is something I have also enjoyed watching. He just zips the ball where it needs to go. The shot may bring him to the dance, but the ability to dip into making plays is where the balancing act becomes fun — the moments where he's unafraid to explore space in pick-and-roll and see if he can get to the second level of a defense, or get a defense to help. There's also a willingness on the defensive end that I think will serve him in the long run.

#2825. Why Drew Lock is about to take the Seahawks starting QB job from Sam Darnold for good

(Editor's note: Everyone please welcome back Michael Thompson to the Field Gulls staff! He was with us all of last season and is returning for at least the rest of this season)

For the second straight game against the Arizona Cardinals, Sam Darnold will be on the sidelines injured, while backup Drew Lock will be tasked with helping the Seahawks get an important divisional win. After Monday night's debacle, a mauling at the hands (paws?) of the new NFC favorites in Chicago, it seems to be fair to question so much about a team that is less than nine months removed from winning the Super Bowl.

Is Brian Fleury the right guy to be running this offense? Why can't the Seahawks run the football? Why can't anyone get open other than Jaxon Smith-Njigba? How was the defense able to be so easily pushed around by Ben Johnson's Bears? Should the Seahawks make more trades, or are they too far behind Chicago and San Francisco? There's a million questions that need and will be answered over the coming days and weeks, but there's two that I think could potentially answer all of these questions.

After a somewhat of a roller coaster 2025 season in Seattle that saw Darnold lead the league in turnovers, but also orchestrate one of the best offenses in football, Sam had his fairytale ending. A crazy fourth quarter comeback to walk-off the Los Angeles Rams in week 16 helped propel the Seahawks to the division title and top seed. From there you know the story, as Sam played the best football of his life against the 49ers and outdueled MVP Matt Stafford en route to a Super Bowl Championship.

Those are special memories that we will cherish and cherish Sam forever for. We also have to acknowledge that 2026 Sam is not the same quarterback we saw last year. Suffering some type of serious glute injury on the fifth play of the season, there have been moments of brilliance and immense talent from Darnold, but it has been clouded by more turnovers, a complete regression in pocket awareness, and an overall lack of that gunslinger swagger that made Sam so beloved in Minnesota and Seattle. I don't know if it is the injuries, the personnel changes, or not having Klint Kubiak around to be his guide, but this version of Sam is hesitant, and he doesn't believe in himself. I hate to say it, but his level of play looks closer to Jets Sam Darnold than anything he's put on tap the past two seasons.

He's clearly not healthy, but I can't help but wonder if his confidence, in both his offensive line and Brian Fluery, is shot. Whenever this type of situation happens with a QB, John Schneider and Mike Macdonald can do one of two things. They can fire Fluery and try to recreate an environment as close to last year as possible for Sam, or they can have Sam take a seat on the bench.

Benching Sam would have sent shockwaves through the NFL, and would bring all sorts of negative attention and drama to a team that needs to figure itself out as quickly as possible. But if Sam is not 100%, could they try to find themselves more stable footing on offense, while Sam could get right? I think we're seeing a soft benching.

With the Seahawks likely favored over the Cardinals at home and essentially a home game next week at Las Vegas before the bye, this could give Darnold three weeks to get right, physically and mentally, while the Seahawks believe they can stay alive as their playoff hopes feel like they're fading fast.

Drew Lock was put in one of the toughest positions in week one. You're the backup to the defending Super Bowl winning QB, and in the first two minutes, he's hurt and you're going in, against a pissed off New England Patriots team and the entire football world watching.

His first half was ugly, but once he and the team settled in, we saw a version of Lock that we had not seen before. After nearly giving the game away on a duck to Christian Gonzalez, Drew Lock was an efficient game manager, going 16/22 for 187 yards and the massive 4th down touchdown find to JSN. He also had a huge 14-yard scramble on his final drive of the game that should have ended the game if not for penalties.

His pocket awareness and mobility allowed plays to develop a bit more for a rookie offensive coordinator, and the championship defense held firm to be a contender in New England. Against the Cardinals on the road, Lock once again looked comfortable and played his best game ever as a Seahawk.

In the desert, Drew went 19/26 for 235 yards and three touchdowns (all to JSN). It was honestly an Apex Alex Smith performance from Lock, as he achieved his two most basic functions: don't turn the ball over and feed JSN. In what ended up a 31-7 win that was very similar to what the Bears just did to Seattle, Lock could have done even more damage if not for turnovers and drops.

Fair or not, the Seahawks had some of their best running in the small sample size with Lock running the show. Pair an average running game with a QB that was 15/15 under center and 13/13 in play action during his 7+ quarters of play, and you have a team that is going to find a way to take care of the ball and squeeze the life out of their opponents.

Seattle is currently 15th in scoring at 23.1 ppg and 7th in scoring defense at 19.9 ppg. When you look back to last year, the offense is nearly a touchdown worse (28.4) while the defense is giving up almost an extra field goal a game (17.2). They're not as dominant this year, and the turnovers carry more weight than last year. This team can get back into the hunt and hope to make the playoffs, but they cannot be fourth in the league at 14 turnovers and a -1 turnover differential. Sam could not fix this problem, while Drew never showed it was a problem in his small sample size.

With Drew at the helm, the Seahawks were 2-0, and people were talking about the Seahawks as the prohibitive favorite to repeat. Lock had proven the floor was high because our backup could be a very good QB in this current environment, but Sam's ceiling made the decision to go back easy.

Now, the game has changed. As Sam (however you want to divide up the blame pie) and this offense have shit the bed, Drew will be asked once again to come in and help keep the season alive. If Lock plays well, just competent top 13-15 guy level of football, the Seahawks are going to go 2-0.

If the Seahawks get to 4-0 with giving the Rock to Lock, we will have the most fascinating bye week in team history. With a potentially season-defining road contest at San Francisco after the bye, will the team really take the ball out of Drew's hands, if he's undefeated? I have no doubt the team loves Sam, but there's no world in which the locker room will allow a switch once again, especially if the team is undefeated with Lock under center.

It's a big if, IF the Seahawks went into Levi's Stadium again, and came out victorious with Drew Lock, then the job will be his until the Seahawks are eliminated or are playing on Super Bowl Sunday.

And this team still has that potential in them. This is still a championship defense. JSN absolutely has the ability to carry a team on a deep playoff run. They just need a quarterback that can channel the ghosts of Jeff Hostetler, Trent Dilfer, Brad Johnson, and Nick Foles.

It feels crazy that we have gotten to this point with Sam and Drew, but with the ball in Lock's hands, I think there's a real chance he doesn't give it back, and we have seen the last snaps of Sam Darnold at quarterback for the Seattle Seahawks.

#2824. The early signs are extremely encouraging for Manchester City and Elliot Anderson

As Manchester City beat RB Leipzig 3-2 in their UEFA Champions League matchday four fixture, Elliot Anderson was once again Manchester City's man-of-the-match. Anderson's performance continued his strong start to life as a City player. The England international has seamlessly slotted into City's midfield and has quickly established himself as the heartbeat of the City engine room. Elliot Anderson has made an encouraging start to life at Manchester City and he has received strong praise from Erling Haaland for his efforts.

Manchester City needed to find a late winner against RB Leipzig to leave the Red Bull Arena with a vital three points. City's winning goal came via a tap-in at the backpost from Erling Haaland. Haaland's goal came after Elliot Anderson made a surging run down the right and played a Kevin De Bruyne-esque cross for Haaland to slide home.

Elliot Anderson's assist capped off a superb individual performance from him in Manchester City's midfield. The type of performance that Anderson put in has quickly become his forte at Manchester City. In statistics courtesy of SofaScore, Anderson won 10/11 duels. Anderson's ability to win duels is crucial to his play and his teams. Also, Anderson kept possession ticking over for Enzo Maresca's side as he completed 79/83 of his passes. His performance was capped off with an assist and Anderson was once again the key component of City's win.

Speaking with mancity.com after Manchester City beat RB Leipzig 3-2, Erling Haaland was full of praise for the start that Elliot Anderson has made to his City career. Haaland said: "He's been amazing. He's had an amazing start to his City career and that's what you expect, you have to come straight in and perform."

"That's how the world is now and it's not easy, normally you need time to adapt and time to get to know your new teammates and the new coach. He's been impressive. Respect."

Elliot Anderson has taken to life at Manchester City like a duck to water. The step up from playing for Nottingham Forest to a team expected to contend on all fronts hasn't phased Anderson. It has taken him no time to establish himself as the key to Manchester City's midfield. His performances this season show how important Anderson is to Enzo Maresca's side. His performance against RB Leipzig was just further evidence of this.

Erling Haaland's praise of Elliot Anderson and the start the England international has made to his City career just further reinforces what has been clear to see with Anderson. He is a special midfielder with the ability, work rate and potential to become one of the finest midfielders in world football. If Anderson's start to his City career is a sign of what is to come from him, the sky is the limit for the player he will become.

Elliot Anderson was again Manchester City's man-of-the-match against RB Leipzig. His display at the Red Bull Arena continued his impressive opening to his City career. Erling Haaland has noticed how impressive Anderson has been and the England international is set to continue to rise at Manchester City.

#2823. Drew Lock: My elbow feels good, felt great to rip some balls yesterday

Drew Lock has been dealing with an elbow injury, but he says it’s not impacting him as he readies to start against the Cardinals on Sunday.

While the Seahawks listed Lock as a limited participant on Wednesday’s injury report, he said his elbow is doing “a lot better.”

“It’s doing good, doing good,” Lock said in his Thursday press conference. “I’m excited to be back out there. It felt great to get under center and rip some balls yesterday. So, it feels good.”

Lock told reporters that he’s looking at his participation for the next few days “like a normal week.”

“If the training staff wants to pull it back a little bit, then let them make that decision,” Lock said. “But I’m expecting to go take every rep.”

Lock is set to start for Sam Darnold, who is week-to-week with a quad injury. Darnold also played through a cracked rib on Monday night. He has already been ruled out for this week and didn’t practice on Wednesday.

Lock had to fill in for an injured Darnold in Week 1, completing 16-of-22 passes in that game for 187 yards with a touchdown. He then beat the Cardinals in Week 2, completing 19-of-26 for 235 yards with three touchdowns and no interceptions in a 31-7 win.

#2822. Theo Benedet, Grady Jarrett return to Bears practice

The Bears got starting linemen from both sides of the ball back at practice on Thursday.

Left tackle Theo Benedet (shoulder) and defensive tackle Grady Jarrett (groin) were both limited participants as the team continuing preparing for Sunday’s game against the Buccaneers. They were both listed as non-participants on Wednesday’s practice report.

Linebacker T.J. Edwards (personal) was also listed as out on Wednesday. He fully participated on Thursday.

Linebacker D’Marco Jackson (ankle) moved in the opposite direction and missed practice after not being on Wednesday’s report. Running back Kyle Monangai (thumb, toe) and left guard Joe Thuney (rest) were also out of practice.

Defensive back Malik Muhammad (toe) joined Benedet and Jarrett in the limited category.
